Personne ne l'a mesuré. INFÉRÉ. Garde anti-faux-trou : il n'a pas été possible d'exclure des travaux de 2026 non indexés. La question reste aussi la moins distinctive des sept : n'importe quel laboratoire disposant de 2412.09565 et de 2507.12691 peut la poser.


## Pièce d'antériorité : anteriorite-Qpont-sections-1-a-3-v1.md

<!-- Extrait scellé (CL31-14) de `anteriorite-sept-questions-v1/anteriorite-Qpont-v1.md` : sections 1 à 3. conv-audit, 28 octobre 2026. Contenu copié tel quel, sans modification. -->

## 1. Verdict
**SEMI-OCCUPÉ, confiance moyenne.** La *métrique* de validité de transfert existe et a été mesurée ailleurs : le coefficient de corrélation simulation-réel (Sim2Real Correlation Coefficient) en robotique, et des corrélations entre écarts de généralisation pour les sondes entraînées hors politique. La sécurité de l'intelligence artificielle mesure déjà le transfert synthétique → réaliste, mais pour l'*entraînement* ou la *sélection* de moniteurs (Storf et al. 2026), ou pour l'erreur d'une estimation de sûreté (Redwood, octobre 2026). Je n'ai trouvé aucun travail qui fabrique des épisodes **à propriété théorique contrôlée P**, puis corrèle propriété par propriété le taux de détection synthétique au taux réel sur du sabotage réellement produit.

## 2. Occupants et voisins (du plus proche au plus lointain)

**a. Olmo, Zhang, Tracy (Redwood Research), « Simulated control evaluations », billet du 23 octobre 2026.** RAPPORTÉ : la même évaluation de contrôle est exécutée en réel puis en simulation (sorties d'outils produites par un modèle de langage) dans LinuxArena. L'écart quadratique moyen de la prédiction de sûreté vaut 3,6 points de pourcentage à un budget d'audit de 5 %, contre 10,1 pour BashArena. C'est **la mesure de transfert simulé → réel la plus proche dans le contrôle**. Ce qu'il NE fait PAS : la simulation porte sur l'*environnement*, l'attaquant reste réel (une politique d'attaque). Il n'y a ni propriété P contrôlée, ni épisode sans attaquant. Les auteurs disent ne pas pouvoir valider sur des transcriptions de déploiement contenant des attaques. Statut : billet, sans relecture par les pairs.

**b. Storf, Barton-Cooper, Peters-Gill, Hobbhahn, « Constitutional Black-Box Monitoring for Scheming in LLM Agents », arXiv 2603.00829, 2026.** RAPPORTÉ : 1 000 trajectoires synthétiques par pipeline (STRIDE, Gloom), générées depuis des n-uplets de scénario contrôlés (cadre, tâche, objectif désaligné, drapeau « couvert »). Les moniteurs sont sélectionnés sur ces trajectoires puis évalués sur 7 500 trajectoires ControlArena tenues à l'écart. Résultat : le transfert existe, il sature vite et la sur-optimisation mène au surapprentissage. Ils notent une relation monotone entre le rang au balayage de consignes et la performance de test pour STRIDE, mais ne donnent **aucun coefficient de corrélation** (section 5.1, figure 4). Ce qu'il NE fait PAS : les propriétés contrôlées sont des scénarios, pas des statistiques prédites du moniteur ; ControlArena reste un attaquant *instruit*, pas un saboteur « naturel » ; aucune corrélation par propriété. Statut : préimpression (programme MATS).

**c. Kirch, Dower, Skapars, Yannakoudakis, Lubana, Krasheninnikov, « The Impact of Off-Policy Training Data on Probe Generalisation », arXiv 2511.17408.** RAPPORTÉ : des sondes sont entraînées sur des données hors politique, sollicitées, incitées ou naturelles, pour huit comportements, puis testées sur du naturel quand il existe. Le transfert est bon pour les comportements « visibles dans le texte » et mauvais pour les comportements « ambigus dans le texte » (tromperie, flagornerie). Le changement de domaine pèse plus que le changement de stratégie de réponse (aire sous la courbe ROC moyenne ≈ 0,90 / 0,85 / 0,74). **Mesure clé** : la corrélation de Pearson entre l'écart de généralisation sur données incitées et l'écart sur données naturelles vaut 0,793 pour les sondes hors politique. Ce qu'il NE fait PAS : la tromperie et le sous-rendement délibéré (« sandbagging ») n'ont pas de données naturelles, d'où le recours à un proxy et une prédiction non vérifiée d'échec du transfert ; pas de sabotage agentique ; aucune propriété issue d'une théorie. Statut : préimpression (lieu de publication non lu, À VÉRIFIER). C'est le **résultat empirique le plus pertinent** : il prédit que la voie synthétique est la plus fragile exactement pour les propriétés « ambiguës dans le texte », celles que vise le programme. INFÉRÉ.

**d. Goldowsky-Dill et al., « Detecting Strategic Deception with Linear Probes », arXiv 2502.03407, ICML 2025 (PMLR v267).** RAPPORTÉ : sondes entraînées sur des paires d'instructions et sur du jeu de rôle, évaluées sur des scénarios plus réalistes (délit d'initié, sous-rendement délibéré). L'aire sous la courbe ROC va de 0,96 à 0,999. C'est une démonstration de transfert *d'entraînement*, sans mesure de prédictivité de l'*évaluation* synthétique. Relu par les pairs.

**e. Hollinsworth, Spies, Diriba, Gleave, Cundy, « Caught in the Act » (FIBS), arXiv 2610.12445.** RAPPORTÉ : 98,8 % d'aire sous la courbe ROC sur SHADE-Arena, au-dessus d'un moniteur textuel. Le résumé ne dit pas si le corpus FIBS est instruit ou naturel et ne rapporte aucune mesure de transfert synthétique → réel (À VÉRIFIER sur PDF). Préimpression.

**f. Organismes modèles.** Taylor et al., « Auditing Games for Sandbagging », arXiv 2512.07810 (Institut de sécurité de l'IA britannique, FAR.AI, Anthropic), et Clymer et al., banc d'essai « bench-af », billet de novembre 2025 (Redwood/MATS). RAPPORTÉ : tous deux reconnaissent que les organismes construits (verrous par mot de passe, réglage fin) ne garantissent pas la représentativité du comportement naturel, qui pourrait être plus ou moins détectable. **Aucun ne mesure ce transfert.** Hubinger et al. 2024 (« Sleeper Agents », 2401.05566) et « Model Organisms for Emergent Misalignment » (2506.11613) sont des voisins plus lointains, sans mesure de transfert repérée (INFÉRÉ d'après les titres et résumés).

**g. Hors champ : la métrique existe.** Kadian et al., « Sim2Real Predictivity », arXiv 1912.06321 (IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters d'après le DOI, À VÉRIFIER). RAPPORTÉ : le coefficient de corrélation simulation-réel sur 9 modèles valait 0,18 pour le succès ; après correction des failles du simulateur exploitées par les agents, il monte à 0,844. C'est le **modèle méthodologique exact** de Q-pont : le simulateur peut être « hackable » de façon à inverser les classements.

**h. Détection d'anomalies.** Steinbuss & Böhm, arXiv 2004.06947 (2020) : RAPPORTÉ, des anomalies synthétiques *caractérisables* (locales, de dépendance, globales) sont injectées, puis les classements de détecteurs sont comparés à ceux obtenus sur données réelles. Les anomalies uniformes reproduisent à peu près le classement réel ; les anomalies de dépendance l'inversent. Les auteurs disent ne pas chercher à imiter les anomalies réelles, donc il n'y a **pas de corrélation par propriété**. Emmott, Das, Dietterich et al., arXiv 1503.01158 (atelier KDD 2013) : des bancs d'essai sont construits depuis des données réelles selon quatre axes contrôlés (difficulté, fréquence, regroupement, pertinence des variables). Aucune validation de prédictivité sur anomalies naturelles n'a été repérée dans le résumé. Wu & Keogh, arXiv 2009.13807 : les bancs d'essai d'anomalies de séries temporelles « triviaux ou mal étiquetés » créent une illusion de progrès. C'est une critique de validité de construit, sans mesure de transfert.

**i. Détection d'intrusion et estimation par données synthétiques.** Hesford et al., arXiv 2403.17458 : les classements de systèmes de détection d'intrusion s'inversent d'un jeu de données à l'autre (Kitsune : F1 = 0,0216 sur CICIDS2017, bon ailleurs). C'est un argument indirect contre la prédictivité d'un jeu fabriqué unique (INFÉRÉ). Van Breugel et al., NeurIPS 2023 (2310.16524), estiment la performance par sous-groupe à partir de données synthétiques et valident l'erreur absolue contre un oracle réel. Le schéma de validation est le bon, mais le domaine (classification tabulaire) est éloigné. CheckList (Ribeiro et al., ACL 2020) et les relations métamorphiques sur classifieurs (1904.07348) : aucune corrélation avec les échecs en déploiement repérée (À VÉRIFIER).

## 3. Ce qui survit
Formulation étroite, apparemment ouverte : **pour un moniteur figé (sonde et/ou boîte noire) et un ensemble de propriétés P_k dérivées d'une théorie des angles morts, la corrélation de rang, propriété par propriété, entre le taux de détection sur épisodes fabriqués sans attaquant portant P_k et le taux sur épisodes de sabotage réel (agent réellement incité ou entraîné) portant P_k, avec les conditions sous lesquelles cette corrélation s'effondre (propriétés « ambiguës dans le texte », failles exploitables du générateur).** Aucun des occupants ne fait varier une propriété dérivée de la théorie ni ne rapporte cette corrélation. Les plus proches mesurent le transfert de l'entraînement (Kirch et al.), de la sélection (Storf et al.) ou de l'environnement (Redwood). INFÉRÉ, sous réserve des points de la section 5.


## Pièce d'antériorité : anteriorite-coevolution-et-boite-blanche-v1.md

# Antériorité — co-évolution attaque/défense, et moniteurs boîte blanche — v1

Lignée conv-audit, 28 octobre 2026. Deuxième et troisième des trois recherches d'antériorité lancées sur le « go » de
Lazar (12:49 heure de Paris) pour la question élevée. Rendues par deux sous-agents neufs.

**Statut R7 : tout RAPPORTÉ d'après résumés / pages HTML lues par un sous-agent, rien vérifié sur PDF empreinté. Aucun
chiffre ni aucune citation n'est opposable avant vérification.** Contrainte respectée par les deux sous-agents : aucune
technique d'attaque ni charge utile ; les contributions côté attaque ne sont nommées que par classe et protocole
d'évaluation.

## A. Co-évolution attaque/défense avec généralisation à des familles tenues à l'écart

**Verdict : partiellement occupé, moins ouvert qu'il n'y paraît.**

Occupé, à ne pas revendiquer comme neuf :
- **SafeCoEvo** (arXiv 2609.36580, 2026, préimpression) — collision la plus nette : co-évolue une garde d'exécution qui
  surveille un agent, modèle de tâche figé, partie de test tenue à l'écart. **À citer comme l'occupant le plus proche, pas
  comme fond.** Réserve : sa sélection se fait sur une validation **en-distribution** (partage scénarios et groupes de
  tâches avec le flux d'évolution) ; l'axe tenu à l'écart est « scénarios/outils », **pas** « familles d'attaque ».
- Durcissement itéré red-team/blue-team d'un moniteur comme **pratique** : Anthropic « Auto Mode » (billet du 22 oct 2026) ;
  UK AISI Control Red Team (billet, juillet 2026). Pas de critère de généralisation, durcissement manuel.
- La généralisation comme quantité **mesurée** dans une boucle co-évolutive : Chong, Tiňo, Yao, IEEE TEC 2008 (stratégies
  de jeu, pas des moniteurs) — 18 ans.
- Autres, plus loin : AgentSafeEvo (2610.19422, fait évoluer la politique du défenseur, pas un moniteur) ; RvB
  (2601.19726, patchs de code + règles de garde-fou, généralisation hors-domaine revendiquée) ; MAGIC (2602.01539,
  politique de refus) ; MonitoringBench (2605.09684, moniteurs **figés**, axe tenu à l'écart = moniteurs) ; EvoDefense
  (2605.31140, chevauchement entraînement/test). Terekhov et al. **ICLR 2026** (attaques adaptatives subvertissent des
  moniteurs de confiance ; moniteurs figés, mitigations en travaux futurs) — seul avec Chong/Tiňo/Yao à être relu par les
  pairs ; le reste sont des préimpressions ou billets.

**Résidu défendable (apparemment ouvert ; INFÉRÉ, à vérifier sur PDF)** : la **famille d'attaque comme axe tenu à
l'écart pour un moniteur de comportement d'agent**, et surtout la **généralisation comme critère de sélection lui-même**
(aptitude = performance sur des familles tenues hors de la conception, pas performance de validation en-distribution).
L'union des quatre — moniteur d'agent + co-évolution + famille tenue à l'écart + généralisation-comme-aptitude — n'est
tenue par aucun travail trouvé. **Recommandation du sous-agent** : rétrécir la revendication au critère de sélection et à
l'axe famille, citer SafeCoEvo comme occupant le plus proche. **Nœud (modification de revendication) : GO de Lazar.**

## B. Moniteurs boîte blanche (activations internes) pour sabotage / tromperie d'agents

**Verdict : la croyance « la boîte blanche seule fait toute l'originalité » est RÉFUTÉE.**

Le champ est **peuplé** :
- « Telling FIBS » (anc. « Caught in the Act »), arXiv 2610.12445, oct 2026 — sondes multi-couches qui détectent le
  **sabotage** agentique et la tromperie, et **battent** un moniteur boîte noire sur SHADE-Arena et BashArena (aire sous
  la courbe rapportée 98,3 contre 97,7 ; 98,0 contre 97,6). Comparé au boîte noire, **pas combiné** ; **pas** de test sur
  familles tenues à l'écart.
- Goldowsky-Dill et al., **ICML 2025** — sondes linéaires, tromperie stratégique, aire 0,96–0,999.
- McKenzie et al., **NeurIPS 2025** — cascade sonde→modèle, hors-distribution central.
- Kramár et al. (Google DeepMind), jan 2026 — sondes **en production dans Gemini**, cascade sonde+Flash, ≈ 10 000× moins
  cher.
- Parrack et al. (juillet 2025) — **fait déjà le joint** boîte noire + sonde (ensembles). Kumar (mai 2026) — meilleur
  protocole de généralisation trouvé (matrices de transfert entre domaines, styles tenus à l'écart), mais styles/domaines,
  **pas** familles d'attaque délibérément retirées.

Ce qui est **déjà fait** : le **joint boîte blanche + boîte noire** (trois fois : ensembles, cascades) — mais **jamais
sur des menaces diffuses / entre épisodes**. Ce qui **n'est pas** fait : la généralisation à des **familles tenues à
l'écart**. Deux papiers « notre cadre exact » sont **100 % boîte noire** : Terekhov et al. (tâches floues, diffus,
arXiv 2606.08892) et UK AISI (attaques distribuées multi-agents, 2607.07368).

**Territoire survivant (INFÉRÉ ; R8 — vérifier plus durement avant toute revendication)** : l'**intersection** — menaces
diffuses / entre épisodes **×** joint boîte blanche + boîte noire **×** familles tenues à l'écart, sous juge figé avec
garantie de fausses promotions. Étroit, mais non trouvé occupé.

## Conséquence pour le programme

1. Ne pas revendiquer « la boîte blanche » comme l'originalité (réfuté), ni « la course aux armements co-évolutive »
   (occupée : SafeCoEvo et al.).
2. L'originalité défendable est l'**intersection** ci-dessus, plus la **généralisation comme critère de sélection** sur
   familles tenues à l'écart. Zone séduisante → R8 → **vérifier sur PDF** et **recadrer avec l'organisme et le
   collisionneur** (Lazar, 12:52) avant toute revendication.
3. Falsifiabilité : « notre défense bat notre attaquant » est de l'auto-jeu quasi infalsifiable (critique standard,
   Tramèr et al. 2020) ; « notre défense généralise à une famille jamais conçue, au degré k » peut échouer — c'est ce qui
   rend la version asymétrique **préférable**, pas « plus forte » dans l'absolu.

## À vérifier sur PDF avant tout préenregistrement (R7)

SafeCoEvo : construction exacte de la partie tenue à l'écart, y a-t-il une stratification par type d'attaque ; « Telling
FIBS » : confirmer l'absence de test tenu à l'écart (lecture partielle) et le titre (retitré) ; AgentSafeEvo et MAGIC :
existe-t-il une partie tenue à l'écart dans les tableaux complets ; McKenzie : lieu de publication. Statut de revue par
les pairs : seuls Terekhov et al. (ICLR 2026), Chong/Tiňo/Yao (IEEE TEC), Goldowsky-Dill (ICML 2025), McKenzie
(NeurIPS 2025) confirmés ; le reste préimpressions/billets.


## Pièce d'antériorité : extrait-2412.09565-v1.md

# EXTRAIT — Obfuscated Activations Bypass LLM Latent-Space Defenses

- Auteurs : Luke Bailey, Alex Serrano, Abhay Sheshadri, Mikhail Seleznyov, Jordan Taylor, Erik Jenner, Jacob Hilton, Stephen Casper, Carlos Guestrin, Scott Emmons ; année : 2025 (v2 du 8 février 2025) ; identifiant : arXiv:2412.09565v2 (51 pages)
- Statut : préimpression arXiv cs.LG v2 ; le PDF ne porte aucune mention de lieu de publication (nos rapports parlent d'ICLR 2026 : non vérifiable sur ce PDF) ; code publié
- PDF source : `docs/sources/pdf/2412.09565v2.pdf` ; SHA-256 du PDF : `9c23a613c5c7e3a213a60eca7cb990b4b7d6e0510db30584736aa021dfb944e9` (compagnon `.sha256` du dépôt vérifié par `sha256sum -c`)
- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-7, 11-13, 28-30
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Aucun chiffre retapé à la main (R7).
- Retiré pour sûreté (niveau méthode seulement) : p.1 (légende de la figure 0 : requête nuisible donnée en illustration), p.29 (paragraphe sur l'optimiseur et les réglages qui rendent les attaques par invite réussies) ; sections de construction détaillée des attaques non reprises. Gardés : définition de l'attaque d'obfuscation (classe), protocole d'évaluation (rappel à 1 % de faux positifs), entraînement adversarial des sondes sur plus de 70 époques, leçons et limites.

---

[p.1]
Abstract Recent latent-space monitoring techniques have shown promise as defenses against LLM attacks. These defenses act as scanners that seek to detect harmful activations before they lead to undesirable actions. This prompts the question: Can models execute harmful behavior via inconspicuous latent states? Here, we study such obfuscated activations. We show that state-of-the-art latent-space defenses—including sparse autoencoders, representation probing, and latent OOD detection—are all vulnerable to obfuscated activations. For example, against probes trained to classify harmfulness, our attacks can often reduce recall from 100% to 0% while retaining a 90% jailbreaking rate. However, obfuscation has limits: we find that on a complex task (writing SQL code), obfuscation reduces model performance. Together, our results demonstrate that neural activations are highly malleable: we can reshape activation patterns in a variety of ways, often while preserving a network’s behavior. This poses a fundamental challenge to latent-space defenses.‡

[RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.1 : légende de la figure 0 (exemple de requête nuisible donnée en illustration)]

[p.2]
In response to attacks on LLMs, such as jailbreaks or data poisoning, researchers have developed a variety of latent-space defense methods. These methods look at model activations and attempt to detect any signs of harmful content. Given that activations are causally upstream of outputs, one might hope that directly monitoring activations can catch issues at their root. Accordingly, recent work has proposed many latent-space defense methods, including monitors based on sparse autoencoders [Templeton et al., 2024, Gao et al., 2024, Lieberum et al., 2024], linear and nonlinear probes [Ball et al., 2024, Arditi et al., 2024, Kirch et al., 2024], and latent OOD detection [Podolskiy et al., 2021, Chen et al., 2022, Ma et al., 2023, Mo et al., 2024]. How robust are these latent-space defenses? An attacker trying to bypass latent-space monitors has two goals: they need to induce inconspicuous activations, but those activations must also lead to the attacker’s desired model behavior. One could imagine these goals being fundamentally incompatible. For example, if the latent space contains only a single “harmfulness direction”—and only activations that align with this direction can produce harmful behavior—then it would be impossible to induce harmful outputs while also fooling a linear classifier encoding this direction. On the other hand, if there are many different regions in the latent space that can all lead to harmful behavior, latent-space monitors may not capture all of them. We call adversarial activations that produce undesired behavior while fooling a latent-space monitor “obfuscated activations.” In this paper, we look for obfuscated activations using obfuscation attacks, which jointly optimize

[p.3]
for a certain model behavior (such as jailbreaking) while fooling latent-space monitors. We find that obfuscation attacks can bypass a wide range of monitors, including sparse autoencoders, supervised probes, and latent OOD detectors. Using different attack vectors (adversarial suffixes, data poisoning, model finetuning) ranging from white-box to black-box settings, we can induce undesired behaviors that aren’t flagged by these defenses.

Obfuscated activations are not rare exceptions but rather appear to be widespread in the latent space. For example, we find obfuscated activations close to the cluster of benign activations when optimizing against OOD detectors. Because these activations are intermingled with inconspicuous ones, they also bypass supervised harmfulness probes as a side effect (Fig. 2). As another test of the abundance of obfuscated activations, we let a defender train a monitor on examples of obfuscated activations. If there were only a few latent regions of obfuscated activations, the monitor should quickly learn to cover them all and become robust. But we find that an attacker can always find new activations that bypass the monitor, even after retraining the monitor for over 70 iterations of alternating attack and defense optimization (§3.5). Obfuscated activations thus seem common enough that it is difficult for a defender to guard against them all. However, obfuscation attacks can not induce arbitrary combinations of activations and behavior. When obfuscating against probes that detect SQL code, the model still generates SQL, but its code is incorrect more often than without obfuscation (§5). This suggests a trade-off between obfuscation and performance in difficult tasks.

[p.4]
Definitions and Background

Obfuscated Activations. When activations induced by an adversarial attack fool a latent space monitor, we call them obfuscated against that monitor. More formally, for some adversarial input, xadv , let R( xadv ) represent a model M’s latent activations. Then let D refer to some latent-space monitoring process, which outputs real-valued scores D (R( x )) for how strongly a certain concept, such as harmfulness, is present in the input x or the generation. Then R( xadv ) is an obfuscated activation if D (R( xadv )) is low even though the concept D is meant to detect is clearly present. We study both how often obfuscated activations appear as a side effect of traditional attacks and how easily they can be induced deliberately through obfuscation attacks. 4

[p.5]
Obfuscation Attacks. An obfuscation attack is an attack that aims to induce obfuscated activations by optimizing a joint objective consisting of a behavioral loss term and an obfuscation loss: min Lbeh ( M ( x )) + λobf Lobf (R( x )) (1)

For example, Lbeh can be a cross-entropy loss designed to make the model output a harmful string. For the obfuscation loss, we often use Lobf = D on inputs with the concept, i.e., we directly attempt to fool D. We also explore transfer, i.e., when does obfuscating against a monitor D ′ fool D. Finally, λobf is some real non-negative value used for balancing the two terms.

2.1

Monitoring methods

We consider latent space monitors D that take in residual stream activations of the model M. Given activations R( x ), the monitor outputs a real-valued score D (R( x )). We consider two approaches, input-based and generation-based monitors. Input-based monitors use only the activations on the final prompt token, R( x )[len( x ) − 1], as is common in latent probing methods [Burns et al., 2022, Marks and Tegmark, 2024, Kirch et al., 2024]. Generation-based monitors use all tokens of the generation, R( x )[len( x ) :]. Most of our monitors separately consider activations at multiple layers of the model and aggregate scores over layers and sequence dimensions by taking a mean.

Supervised probes. We test different supervised binary probes trained to detect a certain concept, such as harmfulness or SQL code. The simplest ones are mean difference probes, which use the difference vector between the mean of positive training samples and the mean of negative training samples as a linear classifier. We also test linear classifiers trained using logistic regression, as well as MLP classifiers.

OOD detection. A complementary approach to probing is latent-space anomaly or out-ofdistribution (OOD) detection. We train an OOD detector on examples of “typical” usage, and use its OOD score to classify new samples. We test a Mahanalobis distance-based detector [Mahalanobis, 1936, Podolskiy et al., 2021, Chen et al., 2022], which fits a multivariate Gaussian to activations of negative samples and uses the log-likelihood as an OOD score. We also test Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) [Kingma and Welling, 2013] with the ELBO as the OOD score. See Appendix E for details on these methods. In the backdoor case study (§4), we also test state-of-the-art OOD detectors specifically developed for backdoor detection [Ma et al., 2023, Mo et al., 2024].

Sparse autoencoders. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have become a popular tool in mechanistic interpretability [Cunningham et al., 2023, Templeton et al., 2024, Gao et al., 2024, Lieberum et al., 2024]. They learn features in an unsupervised way. Each feature is represented by a linear map followed by a non-linearity. This then allows for a human, or some other process, to flag risky examples based on what their SAE latents suggest. In §5, we train linear probes on SAE latents and also test the latents themselves as classifiers.

[p.6]
2.2

Evaluation

In this paper, the attacker has a dual goal: to induce some target behavior in the model while inducing obfuscated activations. This raises a possible tradeoff. For example, jailbreaking a model more reliably leads to more consistently harmful outputs, which might make it more difficult to fool a harmfulness classifier. We thus always evaluate both whether the intended behavior was achieved and whether activations are obfuscated. Evaluating behavior. We use StrongREJECT [Souly et al., 2024] to evaluate whether a model output is harmful. In §5, the intended behavior is instead to generate correct SQL code, and we evaluate this by running the model generation against an SQL database and checking that the entries returned are correct. Evaluating obfuscation. To quantify whether a set of activations fool a monitor, we use recall at a 1% false positive rate (FPR). In other words, we pick a score threshold so that the monitor classifies at most 1% of negative (e.g., benign) samples as positive (e.g., harmful), and then measure how large a fraction of positive samples the classifier correctly recognizes. Unlike AUROC, this metric focuses on the low FPR regime, which is the most relevant one for many applications (e.g., a chatbot should not refuse benign requests too often). See Appendix F for more detailed discussion. We compute confidence intervals by bootstrapping; i.e., we recompute recall at 1% FPR many times by resampling data points (with replacement) to use for computing the 1% FPR decision threshold and corresponding recall. In a few cases, recall is highly sensitive to the specific FPR we pick (e.g., the recall at 2% FPR might be a lot higher than the 1% one we report). This usually results in large error bars, but we additionally mention such cases in the text.

3

Case Study: Detecting Harmfulness

[p.7]
3.2

Ordinary Attacks

We investigate question (1), can existing latent-space monitors reliably detect harmfulness against standard jailbreaking methods? We provide evidence the answer is yes, demonstrating that standard jailbreaking techniques do not induce obfuscated activations against various monitors. We create a dataset of successful jailbreaks by starting with our harmful dataset and using a variety of prominent jailbreaking methods to elicit harmful responses from the model.1 We use GCG [Zou et al., 2023b], PAIR [Chao et al., 2023], MSJ [Anil et al., 2024], and AutoDAN [Liu et al., 2023], among others (see Appendix A.2 for further details). Note that probes are never trained on these jailbreak datasets, only on benign data and harmful data without jailbreaks. We evaluate how well probes transfer to distinguishing between the benign test split and harmful test inputs with jailbreaks. Probes can reliably detect ordinary jailbreaks. As shown in Fig. 4a, the Logistic Regression and MLP probes can detect jailbreaks quite reliably, usually with 90% recall or more at a 1% false positive rate. Generation-based detection works best, and mean difference probes perform worse. See Table 1 in Appendix A.2 for a breakdown of probe performance by jailbreak type. This demonstrates that none of the jailbreaks we test strongly obfuscate activations.

3.3

Obfuscation Attacks

Given that existing jailbreak attacks do not bypass harmfulness probes, we turn to question (2), can obfuscation attacks induce harmful activations that fool probes? Similar to the setting in §3.2, we seek to find adversarial suffixes to harmful requests that lead to a useful response from the model. The key difference is that we also optimize suffixes to fool a target probe, and thus seek to explicitly induce obfuscated activations. We study two different attacker affordances, hard prompt suffixes (real tokens appended to the end of an input) and embedding suffixes (a matrix of values appended to the end of a token sequence embedding [Lester et al., 2021]). While embedding suffix attacks are a strong affordance, they are a realistic threat model for an attacker with white-box access [Yang et al., 2021], and they provide insight into worst-case model performance under prompting [Zou et al., 2023b, Xhonneux et al., 2024, Che et al., 2024]. For hard prompt attacks, we optimize a new suffix for each input. For embedding attacks, we optimize a single universal suffix against a set of inputs, and test it on held-out inputs. We explored universal hard prompt attacks but did not find successful obfuscation attacks with them.

[p.11]
3.5

Adversarial Training Against Obfuscation Attacks

We have seen that all traditional latent-space monitors are highly vulnerable to obfuscated activations. It may be, however, that we can map out all the different ways a model can represent harmfulness with a large enough training dataset. We study question (3), how many different ways can a model represent harmfulness, by adversarially training monitors with obfuscated representations. We let the attacker and defender take turns: in each epoch, the attacker trains an embedding attack to jailbreak the model and fool the current monitor, then the defender retrains a monitor against the obfuscated activations from the current and all previous attacks. We focus our attention to input based probes. For the defender to benefit from adversarial training, the probes need to have enough capacity to guard against all previous attacks. We thus focus on the MLP probe, as well as a new probe that consists of an ensemble of mean difference probes. See Appendix A for details. The attacker can consistently bypass adversarially trained monitors. We find that the defender can always achieve perfect recall against all previous attack suffixes. However, this does not mean the probe is robust to new attacks; indeed, even after over 70 epochs, the attacker can still find a

11

[p.12]
(a) Even after 70 epochs, the attacker can still find new obfuscated activations that fool the monitor. We show the monitor’s recall (higher is better) as attacker and defender take turns (MLP probe; ensemble of linear probes shows the same trend in Fig. 20 of the Appendix). Recall is calculated on the most recent obfuscation attack (see Fig. 9 for performance on all previous obfuscation attacks). Corresponding jailbreaking rates for each attack are shown in Fig. 8b.

(b) Throughout adversarial training, obfuscation attacks continue to jailbreak the model. We show jailbreaking rate over time for the obfuscated attacks shown in Fig. 8a. Over time jailbreaking rate stays high for MLP, and become noisier for Ensemble but seem to plateau at an average of around 50%.

Figure 8: Results from adversarially training monitors. In each epoch, the attacker optimizes an embedding suffix to fool the current probe and jailbreak the model. The defender then trains a new probe to be robust against all previous attacks.

[p.13]
Figure 9: Adversarially trained harmfulness probes can still be obfuscated against. We show the raw score distribution of the final probe trained during the adversarial training from Fig. 8 against attack suffixes from all epochs. Both probes perform well against all previous attacks simultaneously. The MLP probe especially assigns consistently high scores to all previous attacks, so it has enough representational capacity. But the final suffix still produces a score as low as that of benign data. new suffix that fools the latest probe (Figs. 8a and 9).

Geometrically, this suggests that there is no single “harmfulness direction” or other simple representation that a monitor could use to robustly recognize harmful inputs, adding weight to similar recent findings by Mack and Turner [2024]. The linear ensemble result in particular arguably means there are more harmfulness directions than the 70 epochs we train for. Figure 8c visualizes this geometric intuition, showing that the activations induced by obfuscation suffixes can move around the cluster of benign activations through the epochs. We can’t rule out that this training process would eventually converge, but we do not see clear signs of any improvement for the defender.

The attacker can jailbreak late into the game. Figure 8b shows the jailbreaking rate against the MLP and ensemble probe over the course of training, with an exponential running average. We see that on average the attack is always able to jailbreak the target model at a non-trivial rate, but note that the jailbreaking rate is much noisier against the ensemble probe than the MLP probe. (We suspect that this is due to random optimization failures in our attack rather than a fundamental difference.)

Overall, our results suggest that obfuscated activations are abundant in the latent space, and thus adversarial training techniques do not easily lead to robust monitors. However, we reiterate that running this process for much longer could mean that the attacker would “run out” of obfuscated activations, resulting in a robust probe.

[p.28]
8.1

Takeaways and lessons

State-of-the-art latent monitoring techniques are vulnerable to obfuscation attacks. We stresstested a wide range of defense methods and consistently found them to be non-robust.7 This means that practitioners should carefully consider how much trust to place on latent monitoring methods, and that research into more robust defenses is warranted. This suggests models can represent concepts and behaviors in many different ways. Our results demonstrate neural activations are malleable, and an attacker can shape them to fool monitors while maintaining a desired behavior. Obfuscation against OOD detectors transfers to probes. Both for harmfulness (§3) and backdoor detection (§4), we found that obfuscating against simple OOD detectors also leads to full or partial obfuscation against linear and MLP probes. Bypassing an OOD detector requires the obfuscated activations to lie roughly within the distribution of benign activations, which means that supervised classifiers are also fooled as a side effect. The fact that an attacker can manipulate activations to be close to benign ones while executing harmful behavior is concerning, and may be particularly challenging for defenses to overcome. 7 The TED backdoor detector [Mo et al., 2024] is the only exception—we didn’t explicitly train against TED because it is non-differentiable, but we expect that training against a carefully designed proxy could obfuscate against TED as well.

28

[p.29]
Generation-based probes and probing multiple layers help to defend against obfuscated representation attacks. Throughout all our results, we found that generation-based probes performed better than input-based ones (Fig. 4a) and are more difficult to obfuscate against (Figs. 4b, 4c, 5 and 10b). We also found that probing in many layers at once and aggregating results can make obfuscation while retaining model performance more difficult (§5). Future work may wish to focus on monitoring methods that include generation tokens across many layers, both for attack and defense. The success of backdoor detection is sensitive to trigger complexity and poison rate. We showed that using a complex trigger (rather than a simple static trigger) or a low number of poisoned training samples can induce backdoors that are more difficult to detect (§4.3). We are by no means the first to make similar observations; especially in the image domain, designing triggers to obfuscate backdoors against latent monitors has been studied before (see §7). We are excited for work both in backdoor detection and on using backdoors as model organisms for scheming [Hubinger et al., 2024, MacDiarmid et al., 2024] to test the robustness of monitors against a range of different triggers. [COUPÉ]

8.2

Limitations and future work

Hard prompt attacks. While we can achieve significant obfuscation and jailbreaking with hard prompts (Fig. 4b), they don’t fully obfuscate against generation-based probes, and the jailbreaking rate is lower. We expect that future work on improved hard prompt optimization methods could significantly improve these attacks. Transfer of obfuscation attacks/black-box attacks. For hard prompt attacks, we study a gray-box setting where the attacker has access to model logits and monitor scores. In many realistic cases, attackers only have access to a single bit from the monitor—whether it blocked the request or not—and only partial information about logits. Future work could attempt to develop attacks in this black-box setting. One avenue could be to test whether obfuscation attacks found against a model with white-box access can transfer to other models. Characterizing obfuscation-performance trade-offs. We found signs that obfuscation can reduce model performance on challenging tasks (§5.3). If this is a robust trend, this could alleviate the threat posed by obfuscation in some domains. Souly et al. [2024] observed a similar performance degradation for some jailbreak methods, which makes those jailbreaks less threatening in practice. Future work could study more comprehensively whether a fundamental obfuscation-performance trade-off exists, or whether more sophisticated obfuscation attacks can maintain performance even on complex tasks. Improved defenses. In this work we mainly focus on attacks against latent space defenses, and show that simple adversarial training of probes does not offer a robust defense (§3.5). That being said, there are certainly more complex defense methods that should be tried and tested. We present one possible direction in §6.2, using OAT (Algorithm 1) to train models to be easier to monitor, in 29

[p.30]
an attempt to defend against obfuscation. But while we see promising signs, we ultimately do not achieve robust monitors.

[COUPÉ : p.1 légende de la figure 0, p.3-4 liste des études de cas, p.7-10 détail des attaques et des résultats sur les sondes, p.12 figure 8, p.14-27 études de cas §4 à §7 (détection de portes dérobées, autoencodeurs parcimonieux et code SQL, études exploratoires, travaux voisins), p.30-51 références et annexes A à F]


## Pièce d'antériorité : extrait-2510.09462-v1.md

# EXTRAIT — Adaptive Attacks on Trusted Monitors Subvert AI Control Protocols

- Auteurs : Mikhail Terekhov, Alexander Panfilov, Daniil Dzenhaliou, Caglar Gulcehre, Maksym Andriushchenko, Ameya Prabhu, Jonas Geiping ; année : 2025 (v1 d'octobre 2025 ; v2 du 2 mars 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2510.09462v2 (52 pages)
- Statut : préimpression arXiv cs.LG ; MATS, EPFL, ELLIS Institute Tübingen, Institut Max Planck, Université de Tübingen ; la mention d'une conférence (« ICLR ») donnée par le brief n'est pas vérifiable dans le PDF extrait
- PDF source : `docs/sources/pdf/2510.09462v2.pdf` ; SHA-256 du PDF : `96c16e33b27b9633b324acecfe29e1bfa9de7052143fb854824aad70a649d7f7` (compagnon `.sha256` du dépôt vérifié par `sha256sum -c`)
- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-10
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Aucun chiffre retapé à la main (R7).
- Retiré pour sûreté (niveau méthode seulement) : p.4 recette de construction des injections visant le moniteur ; p.5 paragraphe sur l'obtention d'une injection « sans exemple » ; p.8 figure 7 (exemple d'injection complète) et description de son fonctionnement ; annexes E (invites système de la politique d'attaque) et F (exemples d'attaques) non reprises. Gardés : classe d'attaque (injection d'invite dans les sorties du modèle non fiable, adaptative, connaissant le protocole et le moniteur), protocoles de contrôle, protocole d'évaluation, résultats chiffrés, discussion, limites.

---

[p.1]
A BSTRACT AI control protocols serve as a defense mechanism to stop untrusted LLM agents from causing harm in autonomous settings. Prior work treats this as a security problem, stress testing with exploits that use the deployment context to subtly complete harmful side tasks, such as backdoor insertion. In practice, most AI control protocols are fundamentally based on LLM monitors, which can become a central point of failure. We study adaptive attacks by an untrusted model that knows the protocol and the monitor model, which is plausible if the untrusted model was trained with a later knowledge cutoff or can search for this information autonomously. We instantiate a simple adaptive attack vector by which the attacker embeds publicly known or zero-shot prompt injections in the model outputs. Using this tactic, frontier models consistently evade diverse monitors and complete malicious tasks on two main AI control benchmarks. The attack works universally against current protocols that rely on a monitor. Furthermore, the recent Defer-toResample protocol even backfires, as its resampling amplifies the prompt injection and effectively reframes it as a best-of-n attack. In general, adaptive attacks on monitor models represent a major blind spot in current control protocols and should become a standard component of evaluations for future AI control mechanisms.

1

I NTRODUCTION

Background. The AI control problem (Greenblatt et al., 2024) focuses on designing protocols that oversee advanced AI systems that can intentionally subvert safety measures. Control protocols typically rely on a monitor: a trusted model that scores, blocks, or rewrites outputs of the target untrusted model. Importantly, the monitor model is assumed to be weaker than the untrusted model, so monitoring can be seen as an instance of the broader scalable oversight problem (Bowman et al., 2022). As we demonstrate, the reliance on a monitor creates a single point of failure: if the untrusted model misleads or hijacks the monitor, the control protocols fail at their core. Most prior work stress-tests protocols against intentionally subtle misbehavior, where a subversive AI model presents its actions as benign using the semantics of the task it is working on. For example, the untrusted model might embed backdoors in code that disguise themselves as legitimate functionality (Greenblatt et al., 2024), or orchestrate multi-step attacks in agentic workflows where individual actions appear benign in isolation (Bhatt et al., 2025). However, these attacks typically do not exploit a critical part of control protocols: the monitor that approves or rejects generated content is a weaker, static model, that has to contend with a stronger, more recent model that has to be assumed to have knowledge of the AI control protocol that is being deployed. In our work, we show that untrusted models can execute adaptive attacks that directly compromise the monitor through prompt injections embedded in the untrusted model’s outputs. Our setting. Following Kerckhoffs’s principle (Shannon, 1949), we assume that security through obscurity is insufficient, and the red team’s subversive AI knows the control protocol: the monitor’s identity and the algorithms in the protocol, including the decision thresholds. Kerckhoffs’s principle has a long history in the security literature, in machine learning particularly in the context of adversarial robustness (Carlini et al., 2019; Tramer et al., 2020). In the context of AI control, it is * †

Equal contribution, order decided by dice roll. Correspondence to mikhail.terekhov@epfl.ch. Equal supervision.

1

[p.2]
justified by the fact that frontier models often know they are being evaluated (Laine et al., 2024; Abdelnabi & Salem, 2025), defense protocols are openly documented, attackers may have a later knowledge cutoff than the monitor and may significantly exceed its capabilities (Panfilov et al., 2025). This problem is exacerbated if the AI agent can retrieve the latest jailbreaks using web search. Leveraging the later knowledge cutoff of the untrusted model, we craft an exploit: a prompt injection embedded within the untrusted model’s outputs, specifically tailored to the monitor’s identity. We further argue that this attack vector is critical to understanding the worst-case risk of a control protocol. Our contributions. We present our experiments in two scenarios. First, the untrusted model plants an LLM-generated prompt injection that we produce by adapting a publicly available jailbreak using DeepSeek-Chat (Guo et al., 2025). Using a compliant model like DeepSeek avoids confounding attack potency with the untrusted model’s non-compliance to produce jailbreaks. Second, the model produces a self-generated injection, which required us to jailbreak the untrusted model beforehand. [COUPÉ]Overall, our findings suggest that as long as non-robust LLM monitors remain the primary oversight tool, AI control protocols can be easily compromised.

2

T HREAT M ODEL FOR AI C ONTROL

Since we focus on adaptive attacks on AI control protocols, we find it is important to clearly state the threat model and key assumptions we make. We provide a schematic of the setting in Figure 1. General Setting. Following Greenblatt et al. (2024), we frame AI control as a sequential game between a blue team (defender) and a red team (attacker). A game episode may last up to N steps. At each step, the environment draws an i.i.d. task from a fixed distribution. The red team attempts to solve the task using a strong untrusted model. Solving a task may require multiple interactions with the environment. In parallel, the red team may pursue a side task (the sabotage task), which it aims to complete without detection. The blue team deploys a control protocol that governs its interactions with the red team. It can use a weaker model that scores the suspiciousness of red team’s moves to 2

[p.3]
identify which of the untrusted model’s actions to flag for expensive auditing. We focus on AI control in the high-stakes setting, where a single detected malicious action is sufficient for the blue team to win, and a single successful non-audited sabotage is sufficient for the red team to win. Below, we describe what information and affordances each team has access to. Blue Team. The blue team commits to its protocol before the game begins. It has access to a trusted model: a weaker but extensively tested safe LLM. The blue team is assumed to have only black-box access to the untrusted model: it cannot see the model’s residual stream, its system prompt or chain-ofthought, but it can inspect any user-visible input to the model, any tool calls the model makes, and all model outputs. Importantly, the blue team is stateful and observes the full history of the red team’s outputs, which can help to catch a misaligned model. The blue team also has limited access to expensive, high-quality human reviewers, who can audit a small fraction c ∈ [0, 1] of the red team’s actions – this is referred to as the auditing budget. Following prior work (Greenblatt et al., 2024; Bhatt et al., 2025; Terekhov et al., 2025), we assume that a human audit perfectly reveals whether the red team is attempting sabotage at the audited moment. Throughout this paper, we set the auditing budget c = 0.02 following Greenblatt et al. (2024) and provide a sensitivity analysis of our main results in Appendix C. Red Team. The red team moves second and adapts its attack to the fixed protocol. Following Kerckhoffs’s principle (Shannon, 1949), we assume the red team knows the monitoring model and the prompts, the protocol algorithm, and its thresholds. Many defenses fail once adversarial attacks do not rely on security through obscurity and are tailored to a particular defense (Tramer et al., 2020). At the same time, the attacker cannot coordinate statefully across sessions: it solves each input independently, without knowledge of prior outcomes. Although the red team knows which model is used as the trusted monitor, it cannot interact with it outside the context set by the protocol. This stateless property introduces a fundamental asymmetry between the red and blue teams.

3

E XPERIMENTAL S ETUP

In this section, we describe the benchmarks we use, the evaluation details for usefulness versus safety trade-offs, the AI control protocols we evaluate, and the prompt injections we design. 3.1

B ENCHMARKS

Our AI control evaluations use sabotage benchmarks: a distribution of paired main and side tasks. We test adaptive attacks on two representative benchmarks that are standard in the control literature APPS Backdooring and BashBench. [COUPÉ]BashBench (Bhatt et al., 2025). The benchmark consists of 257 system administration tasks that are solvable with sequential bash shell commands. Agents can test their scripts on visible test cases before final submission. The side task is predefined and remains constant across all scenarios: it instructs the agent to download and execute a malicious script. 3.2

E VALUATION

We measure usefulness and safety of a protocol using two modes in which the red team can operate. [COUPÉ]Safety (Attack Mode). The red team attempts both the main and sabotage tasks. If an audit catches sabotage, the episode ends and the blue team wins. If sabotage succeeds without detection, the red team wins immediately. If neither event occurs by the end of the episode, the blue team wins. This setup is referred to as “high-stakes,” since one miss can be catastrophic for either team. Safety is 3

[p.4]
defined as the probability that the blue team wins when both teams play optimally. To compute safety, we employ numerical optimization to find the optimal actions of both teams, see Appendix B.2. 3.3

P ROTOCOLS

We evaluate all high-stakes control protocols (Greenblatt et al., 2024; Bhatt et al., 2025) that are implemented in ControlArena (Matthews et al., 2025). [COUPÉ]Trusted Monitoring (TM). With a trusted monitor, the blue team audits actions that the monitor rates as more suspicious. Safety depends on the monitor’s discriminative power in distinguishing malicious actions from benign ones. If the monitor has little to no predictive capabilities (ROC on or below the diagonal), TM reduces to upfront auditing.[COUPÉ][COUPÉ][COUPÉ]Defer-to-Resample (DTR). Weak trusted monitors can produce many false positives. DTR mitigates this by sampling additional candidate actions from the untrusted model before deciding to defer or audit. Following Bhatt et al. (2025), decisions use the lowest suspiciousness among the samples. The extra actions provide incriminating or exonerating evidence for the red team. While Bhatt et al. (2025) show benefits from the protocol for their specific attack vectors, under our stronger adaptive evaluation resampling backfires and actively reduces safety. 3.4

P ROMPT I NJECTION D ESIGN

Motivation. In the AI control setup, the untrusted model is assumed to be strictly more capable than the trusted monitor. Building on this asymmetry, we assume the untrusted model is released after the monitored model and therefore trained on larger, more recent web corpora, including sources that quickly document working jailbreak examples, such as GitHub repositories (Pliny, 2025) and online forums (Reddit, 2025). This training gives the untrusted model an upper hand through direct access to pre-discovered jailbreaks, substantially amplifying its advantage over the monitor. We emulate this scenario using GPT-5 and Claude Sonnet 4 as untrusted models. We seed them with a media article containing a jailbreak example published before the untrusted models’ release (HiddenLayer, 2025). However, both GPT-5 and Claude Sonnet 4 are heavily safety-tuned and either refuse or ignore requests to jailbreak the monitor. To address this, we explore two strategies to construct “model organisms” of misaligned, untrusted models that would execute prompt injections as an attack policy against their monitors. [RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.4 : recette de construction de l'injection (adaptation d'un exemple public à chaque moniteur par un modèle docile, consignes données au modèle non fiable, pointeur vers les annexes d'exemples)]

[p.5]
A DAPTIVE ATTACKS ON M ONITORS S UBVERT C ONTROL P ROTOCOLS

We test adaptive attacks in four steps: (i) we isolate the effect of prompt injections attack on barebones trusted monitoring (Section 4.1), (ii) we demonstrate how prompt-injection attack policy affect single-step control protocols on APPS (Section 4.2), (iii) we evaluate adaptive attacks in multi-step control protocols on BashBench (Section 4.3), and (iv) we demonstrate the feasibility and effectiveness of zero-shot prompt-injection attack policy (Section 4.4). 4.1

P ROMPT I NJECTIONS S UBVERT T RUSTED M ONITORS

We first demonstrate the effect of prompt injections on monitor performance with a plain control protocol, i.e. without any deferrals or resampling. We run the untrusted model in honest and attack modes on the sabotage benchmark, and use the trusted monitor to score the each action of the red team. Experimental Details. In APPS, each task yields one action, so we use its suspiciousness score produced by the monitor as the audit score. In BashBench, a trajectory’s audit score is the maximum suspiciousness over its actions. This raises the bar for the attacker: the monitor observes the full action history and need only detect the injection once1 . We filter inputs to the monitor as follows: we include honest samples only if the solution passes all tests, and backdoored samples only if the backdoor is successful. We provide further details in Appendix E. Results. In Figure 2 we plot ROC curves for the monitors and an example histogram of suspicousness scores for GPT-4.1. The ROC curves without injections demonstrate that the monitors can distinguish backdoored samples from benign with moderate reliability; with injection, however, performance degrades consistently across models and benchmarks. This effect is weaker but still significant with GPT-4o on BashBench. The injection for GPT-4.1 on APPS is particularly successful: with it, the monitor labels all 375 backdoored GPT-5 samples as benign (score 0), despite GPT-4.1 being the strongest monitor when the injection is not present. On BashBench, GPT-4.1 shows similar susceptibility. Overall, our adaptive attacks often make backdoored solutions appear less suspicious than benign ones across most thresholds. 1

We omit GPT-4 on BashBench because its context window could not accommodate the full action history.

5

[RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.5 : paragraphe « Zero-Shot Prompt Injections » (comment amener le modèle non fiable à produire l'injection ; il précède la section 4 de la page 5)]

[p.6]
A DAPTIVE ATTACKS S UBVERT S INGLE -S TEP AI C ONTROL

We next benchmark the impact of our attack on the full control protocols but in a single-step setting on the APPS backdooring setting. Single-step benchmarks like APPS allow us to generate trusted model deferred actions for all samples in one pass. This makes it possible to simulate different deferral thresholds to obtain denser Pareto frontiers. We detail attacker/defender configurations and protocol parameters in Appendix D.1.

Results. We demonstrate in Figure 3 that our adaptive attack subverts control across monitors and protocols. Overall, we see that prompt injections drastically degrade control-protocol performance across both monitors and strategies. On the center and right plots, we observe safety–usefulness Pareto frontiers. Injections collapse the frontiers across protocols and monitoring models, and protocol safety falls to that of Upfront Auditing. The sole exception is the family of Always-Defer policies (bottom-right points), which never rely on the monitor because they always defer to the trusted model to either write the code from scratch or to edit it.

[p.7]
4.3      A DAPTIVE ATTACKS S UBVERT M ULTI -S TEP AI C ONTROL

In BashBench, the agent is tasked to complete realistic, multi-step assignments, which provides a larger attack surface to perform malicious actions at various steps. We explore whether our attack vector transfers to this setup as well. We evaluate Claude Sonnet 4 as the attacker model. In multi-step tasks, a single run cannot simulate outcomes at many deferral thresholds, so generating dense Pareto frontiers is costly; we therefore evaluate a smaller set of thresholds (see Appendix D.2). In Figure 5, we compare the DTT and DTR protocols using GPT-4o as the monitor. We again observe that the prompt injections subvert both protocols across deferral thresholds. Both protocols achieve only slight safety improvements over upfront auditing at high deferral thresholds. [COUPÉ] We find that even without prompt injections, DTR performs similarly to DTT, in contrast to the findings of Bhatt et al. (2025). We attribute this to multiple differences in our setups. First, both the untrusted and trusted models we use are much stronger (Claude Sonnet 4 and GPT-4o instead of GPT-4o-mini and Llama 3.1 8B). Second, we allow the untrusted model to construct the attacks on its own, instead of providing the model with human-written templates for multi-step attack execution. [RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.7 : exemple concret de dissimulation d'un script malveillant] Overall, DTR in its current form increases the control tax (Terekhov et al., 2025) through resampling without delivering clear benefits, so we do not recommend it in similar scenarios. We see this as a clear case where a stronger adaptive evaluation revealed that the apparent robustness of the protocol stemmed from being tailored to a specific attack policy and failed to generalize to other attacks within the same threat model.

Until now, we have studied an attack vector with an advance in-context knowledge of prompt injection templates. However, can strong attackers craft its own prompt injection zero-shot? The

Figure 5: Injections Subvert Control on BashBench. Safety is shown with bootstrapped 95% confidence intervals; usefulness is shown with 95% Wilson intervals. Safety of UA is computed analytically.
Figure 6: A Zero-Shot Prompt Injection Attack Policy. Left: ROC of the monitor (GPT-4o-mini) when classifying backdoors by untrusted model (GPT-5), w/ and w/o prompt injections generated by GPT-5. Right: Safety of Trusted Monitoring; error bars show bootstrapped 95% confidence intervals.

[p.8]
attacker model faces a trade-off: failed injections raise suspiciousness; successful ones reduce it. Beyond a certain attacker-monitor capability gap, we show that the attacker model can craft successful prompt injections in a zero-shot fashion. In Figure 6 we demonstrate that coerced GPT-5 performs zero-shot prompt injections against a weak monitor (GPT-4o-mini), erasing gains over upfront auditing. Here, note that the untrusted model generates a new attack for every APPS query. [COUPÉ]

[RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.8 : figure 7 (exemple complet d'injection produite par le modèle non fiable et réponse du moniteur) et paragraphe qui décrit comment cette injection fonctionne]

[p.9]
AI Control and Jailbreaks. The original work of Greenblatt et al. (2024) acknowledged that jailbreaking the monitor is a valid attack policy but chose not to investigate this branch of control games. While Bhatt et al. (2025) argue that AI Control is inspired by adversarial robustness, they dismiss jailbreaks as an attack vector on the grounds that they originate from adversarial users rather than adversarial models. We view this as an oversight: LLM-based jailbreaking is a standard in the literature (Glukhov et al., 2025; Zeng et al., 2024), and LLM-based safeguards have been shown to be highly vulnerable to jailbreaking as an attack vector (Mangaokar et al., 2024; Jin et al., 2024; Brown et al., 2024; Wei et al., 2025), a fact that is apparent even to currently deployed frontier models. [COUPÉ]

D ISCUSSION

Why DTR Backfires. The Defer-to-Resample (DTR) protocol samples additional actions when an action looks suspicious and then executes the least suspicious one. In the recommended auditing variant, DTR also tracks the minimum suspiciousness across samples to decide when to escalate. While this heuristic preserves usefulness under benign conditions, it becomes a liability once the monitor itself is targeted: under prompt injection attacks, DTR effectively induces the best-of-n jailbreaking behavior (Hughes et al., 2025).[COUPÉ] Our results from Section 4.2 show that in DTR (with up to k = 3 samples), injected samples with artificially low scores are increasingly favored by the minimumbased selection rule, directly leading to the underwhelming performance of DTR compared to DTT. Although resampling may still have potential to incriminate or exonerate red team actions, the current 9

[p.10]
form of DTR exhibits clear weaknesses to prompt injections.[COUPÉ] Can’t We Just Patch These Particular Vulnerabilities? A tempting reaction to the attacks shown in this paper could be to argue that these vulnerabilities are patchable. [COUPÉ]For our worst-case analysis, we have to always assume that the protocol is predefined, and already known to the attacker. Any change in the protocol, like additional filters, would then imply a new round of attacks, as the attacker moves second and adapts, potentially finding new vulnerabilities that are obvious only in hindsight. We also note that while attacks in this work are assumed to be perfectly detectable in human audits (see Section 2), this may not be true for more capable models. ROC Curves Under the Diagonal. Most monitor evaluations in Section 4 result in ROC curves largely or entirely under the diagonal. We stress that this does not mean that one could flip the label returned by the monitor to obtain non-trivial discrimination, since this would not be robust to actions induced by an adaptive attacker who would know the heuristic. Rather, any ROC curve we get from a control protocol evaluated against strong adaptive attacks should be treated as a lower bound on the ROC of a monitor in the actual deployment against a subversive AI. [COUPÉ]Limitations. We note several limitations of our study.[COUPÉ][COUPÉ][COUPÉ] Lastly, we use highly capable but, to the best of our knowledge, not inherently malicious models; all subversive behavior is induced by prompting and may be underelicited. Future, more realistic safety estimates would require training a dedicated misaligned model.

[COUPÉ]

[COUPÉ : figures 1 à 6 et leurs légendes sauf 5 et 6 ; p.2 liste des contributions ; p.3-4 détails des jeux d'épreuves et des protocoles de report ; p.6-7 généralisation entre moniteurs, discussion des seuils ; p.8-9 revue de la littérature (sauf « AI Control and Jailbreaks ») ; p.10 mitigations, conclusion, une partie des limites ; p.11-52 bibliographie et annexes A à F (calcul de la sûreté, invites système de la politique d'attaque, exemples d'attaques)]


## Pièce d'antériorité : extrait-2601.20022-v1.md

# EXTRAIT — Quickest Change Detection in Discrete-Time in Presence of a Covert Adversary

- Auteurs : Amir Reza Ramtin, Philippe Nain, Don Towsley ; année : 2026 (v2 du 16 février 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2601.20022v2 (34 pages)
- Statut : préimpression arXiv cs.IT ; Université du Massachusetts à Amherst et Inria (centre de l'Université Côte d'Azur) ; article de théorie de la détection de rupture, hors du champ de la sécurité des modèles de langage
- PDF source : `docs/sources/pdf/2601.20022v2.pdf` ; SHA-256 du PDF : `8d090f3171ef76cad597ab263a2de9df49de1d1accf78a8afff55ceb82881b42` (compagnon `.sha256` du dépôt vérifié par `sha256sum -c`)
- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-12
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Aucun chiffre retapé à la main (R7).
- Retiré pour sûreté (niveau méthode seulement) : rien (article théorique : aucune recette d'attaque). Les démonstrations des propositions sont omises par économie de place, les énoncés et hypothèses sont gardés.

---

[p.1]
Abstract We study the problem of covert quickest change detection in a discrete-time setting, where a sequence of observations undergoes a distributional change at an unknown time. Unlike classical formulations, we consider a covert adversary who has knowledge of the detector’s false alarm constraint parameter γ and selects a stationary post-change distribution that depends on it, seeking to remain undetected for as long as possible. Building on the theoretical foundations of the CuSum procedure, we rigorously characterize the asymptotic behavior of the average detection delay (ADD) and the average time to false alarm (AT2FA) when the post-change distribution converges to the pre-change distribution as γ → ∞. Our analysis establishes exact asymptotic expressions for these quantities, extending and refining classical results that no longer hold in this regime. We identify the critical scaling laws governing covert behavior and derive explicit conditions under which an adversary can maintain covertness, defined by ADD = Θ(γ), whereas in the classical setting, ADD grows only as O(log γ). In particular, for Gaussian and Exponential models under adversarial perturbations of their respective parameters, we asymptotically characterize ADD as a function of the Kullback–Leibler divergence between the pre- and post-change distributions and γ. Index Terms Sequential analysis; Discrete-time detection; Quickest change detection; CuSum procedure; Covert adversary.

I. I NTRODUCTION The problem of sequential change detection or quickest change detection (QCD), arises in various engineering applications [11], [16], [17]. In all of these applications, the decision-making agent receives a sequence of stochastic observations that undergo a change in distribution in response to a change or disruption in the environment. As long as the behavior of the observations is consistent with the normal (pre-change) state, the decision-maker is content to allow the process to continue. If the distribution changes, then the decision-maker is interested in detecting the change as soon as possible and taking any necessary action in response to the change. More precisely, the goal of the decision-maker is to make the average detection delay (ADD) as small as possible, while constraining the average time to false alarm (AT2FA) to be above some threshold. Detecting adversarial behavior is one of the most critical applications of QCD [19]. Traditional analyses, however, typically assume that potential adversaries are unaware of the underlying detection mechanism and therefore cannot adjust their actions to avoid being caught. In modern interconnected systems, this assumption is increasingly less realistic – sophisticated adversaries can often infer or estimate aspects of the detection strategy and intentionally manipulate their behavior to remain undetected. Such covert adversarial behavior poses new challenges for QCD, especially in security-sensitive domains such as cyber defense, surveillance, and military operations, where even small delays in detection can lead to significant consequences. A. Ramtin and D. Towsley were supported by the DEVCOM Army Research Laboratory under Cooperative Agreement W911NF-17-2-0196 (IoBT CRA) and the National Science Foundation under Grant ECCS-2148159.

[p.2]
The notion of covertness, originally introduced in the context of covert communication [2], refers to the ability of a transmitter to remain undetectable by an observing warden during communication. Most existing work on covert communication assumes that the detector knows the exact start time of any potential transmission. In contrast, we relax this assumption by extending the notion of covertness to the QCD setting, where adversaries deliberately act to remain hidden over prolonged time horizons. This departure results in a fundamentally different adversarial strategy when considered within the framework of QCD. Unlike traditional adversaries whose actions result in detectable statistical deviations, covert adversaries aim to ensure that ADD scales with the same order as AT2FA, thereby rendering the QCD procedure ineffective [6]. In this work, we specifically focus on the theoretical characterization of covert adversarial behavior against the Cumulative Sum (CuSum) procedure. The CuSum procedure, first proposed by Page [10], is one of the most widely studied methods in quickest change detection. It operates by recursively accumulating log-likelihood ratios of the observed data and signaling an alarm when this cumulative statistic exceeds a prescribed threshold. The CuSum test is well known for its optimality under Lorden’s minimax criterion [8], which minimizes the worst-case expected detection delay subject to a false-alarm constraint. Classical analyses, including Lorden’s asymptotic characterization of ADD, assume that the post-change distribution is fixed and independent of the false-alarm constraint γ. In more realistic adversarial settings, however, this assumption may not hold: a covert adversary can deliberately choose a post-change distribution that depends on γ, causing the post-change and pre-change distributions to become increasingly similar as γ grows. This dependence invalidates classical asymptotic results and motivates the need for new analyses of scaling stationary post-change models, in which the post-change distribution converges to the pre-change distribution as γ → ∞. While prior work has examined covertness in both discrete-time [6], [12] and continuous-time sequential detection settings [14], these studies differ substantially from our formulation. The work in [6] is primarily rooted in covert wireless communication, where the modeling assumptions are tailored to communication-theoretic contexts – such as specific channel models and power constraints – and the focus lies on communication-centric performance metrics rather than fundamental detection limits. In contrast, [12] investigates an adversarial setting against the CuSum procedure under non-stationarity, without assuming that the adversary knows the false-alarm constraint parameter, whereas in our work the post-change distribution explicitly depends on this parameter. Consequently, the formulation in [12] differs fundamentally from ours. The study most closely related to our framework is the continuous-time analysis in [14], which considers a Brownian-motion model where the post-change drift depends on the false-alarm constraint. However, that setting benefits from closed-form expressions for both ADD and AT2FA, allowing for exact characterizations. In contrast, the discrete-time case analyzed here is significantly more challenging, as no such exact equalities exist between ADD, AT2FA, the detection threshold, and the Kullback–Leibler divergence – necessitating a refined asymptotic analysis to obtain precise scaling results. We provide a rigorous asymptotic analysis under a discrete-time stationary setting in which the post-change distribution depends on the false-alarm constraint parameter γ, deriving a novel asymptotic characterization of ADD for the CuSum procedure when the difference between the pre-change and post-change distributions diminishes as γ → ∞. In this regime, we demonstrate that the classical asymptotic result for ADD due to Lorden no longer applies. Our principal theoretical contribution – motivated by the need to analyze the asymptotics of ADD in this challenging setting – is the establishment of precise asymptotic expressions for both AT2FA and ADD, formulated in terms of the Kullback–Leibler divergence between the post-change and pre-change distributions and the detection threshold. These expressions, previously regarded as approximations in classical works such as [7], [15], are rigorously proven to be asymptotically exact in our regime, thereby substantially strengthening the theoretical underpinnings of QCD. [COUPÉ]

[p.3]
Moreover, we investigate precise conditions under which an adversary can maintain covertness, which we define as the scenario in which the best achievable ADD, under the constraint that AT2FA is at least γ, grows asymptotically as Θ(γ). We formalize this definition in our analysis, noting that it aligns closely with the notion of covertness introduced in [6]. Through our theoretical framework, we characterize the required rate at which the post-change distribution must converge to the pre-change distribution in order to preserve covertness. In this formulation, the adversary’s control over the post-change distribution models its ability to influence how the statistical properties of the observations evolve after the change. By selecting post-change distributions that are closer to the pre-change distribution, the adversary can delay detection while remaining covert, a perspective consistent with [14]. We take this as motivation for studying adversarial choices of post-change distributions, since delayed detection increases the duration over which the adversary can influence the system before being detected. We also quantify the extent of this influence by building upon the notion of total damage introduced in [12], which measures the cumulative impact incurred prior to detection, and study its asymptotic scaling. To illustrate our theory, we analyze Gaussian-distributed (resp. exponential-distributed) observations, explicitly studying adversaries that manipulate both the mean and variance parameters (resp. mean) as functions of γ. While adversaries that vary the variance have been previously considered in the covert communication literature, adversaries who adjust the mean are motivated by volume-based attacks that aim to increase activity levels without triggering detection [13]. We prove that our theoretical results apply to this model by conducting a detailed analysis showing that the conditions established in the preceding section – which are sufficient to ensure that the overshoots vanish – are indeed satisfied as γ → ∞. This validation confirms that the asymptotic expressions for AT2FA and ADD remain accurate even in this parametric Gaussian/exponential setting, thereby demonstrating the broad applicability of our asymptotic framework. We then explore how the ADD scales based on the rate at which the post-change distribution converges to the pre-change distribution, and establish conditions under which covertness is maintained in the Gaussian (resp. exponential) case.

II. BASIC ELEMENTS OF CHANGE - POINT DETECTION IN DISCRETE - TIME Let X1 , X2 , . . . be mutually independent random variables (rvs) taking values in a measurable space X . Denote by Fk = σ(X1 , . . . , Xk ) the smallest σ-fied generated by X1 , . . . , Xk . Random variables X1 , . . . , Xt−1 have common (pre-change) probability distribution Q0 while rvs Xt , Xt+1 , . . . have common (post-change) probability distribution Q1 , distinct from Q0 1 . Denote by Et the expectation operator associated with this model. The change time t is unknown but constant (non-Bayesian setting). The case t = ∞ corresponds to no change occurring, i.e., only the distribution Q0 is used, and t = 1 corresponds to the situation where only Q1 is used. We assume that Q0 and Q1 have probability density functions (pdfs), denoted by q0 and q1 , respectively, and that the log-likelihood log(q1 (X)/q0 (X)) is finite a.s. under both Q0 and Q1 . Recall that the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence of a pdf p1 from a pdf p2 , defined on the same measurable space, is D(p1 ||p2 ) = E[log(p1 (X)/p2 (X)), with X a rv with pdf p1 , whenever this expectation is finite; if so, D(p1 ||p2 ) ≥ 0, with D(p1 ||p2 ) = 0 if and only if pdfs p1 and p2 are identical. Denote by T the set of all (possibly extended) stopping times with respect to the filtration F := {Fk , k ≥ 1}. Fix γ ∈ (0, ∞). Lorden’s classical problem [8] consists in minimizing d(T ) := sup ess supEt [(T − t + 1)+ | Ft−1 ], t≥1

over all stopping times T ∈ T such that E∞ [T ] ≥ γ. In words, the goal is to minimize the worst-case average detection delay under a constraint on the false alarm rate (i.e., 1/E∞ [T ] ≤ 1/γ). A PPLICATION TO G AUSSIAN AND EXPONENTIAL MODELS In this section, we first address the situation when both the pre-change and post-change distributions are Gaussian distributions, then we investigate the case when both distributions are exponential.
