Proposition 4.2 shows that an attacker is covert if either (i) 12 γ(µ2 + 12 σ 4 ) converges to a positive constant as γ → ∞, or if √ √ (ii) |µ| γ ∼γ 0 and σ 2 γ ∼γ 0. Hence, covertness will occur, in particular, when (µ, σ 2 ) = (γ −δ1 , γ −δ2 ) with δ1 ≥ 21 and δ2 ≥ 12 . √ We now focus on the √ total damage through two representative examples. We take g(x) = x and let σ = 0. In this case, g(D(q||qγ )) ∼ |µ|/ 2. Consequently, the total damage d(γ) = n(γ)g(D(q||qγ )) is proportional to n(γ)|µ|, which corresponds to the total mean shift introduced by the adversary prior to detection. Then, following Proposition 3.4, when γD(q||qγ ) = Θ(1), √ √ which here implies µ = Θ(1/ γ), the total damage is maximized and satisfies d(γ) = Θ( γ). Similarly, if we instead set 2 µ = 0 and allow σ ̸= 0, then d(γ) is proportional to n(γ)σ , which corresponds to the total power injected by the adversary √ prior to detection in a wireless communication setting. Then, under σ 2 = Θ(1/ γ), the total damage is maximized with √ d(γ) = Θ( γ). The same scaling law was also reported in [6] for this communication setting.

[p.12]
V. C ONCLUSION In this work, we developed an asymptotic framework for covert quickest change detection in discrete time, analyzing the CuSum procedure in a stationary setting where the post-change distribution qγ depends on the false-alarm constraint γ and approaches the pre-change distribution as γ → ∞. Classical QCD analyses do not allow such γ-dependent post-change models, and therefore cannot capture this covert regime. We showed that standard asymptotic characterizations of detection delay fail under this dependence, and we established conditions under which the overshoot of the CuSum statistic vanishes asymptotically, enabling sharp and rigorous characterizations of both AT2FA and ADD. We further verified, in Gaussian and exponential models, that these conditions hold under convergence of qγ to q in parameter space as γ → ∞. We also derived the scaling laws under which an adversary can preserve covertness. A natural direction for future research is to identify structural properties of the pdfs qγ and q that would guarantee that sufficient conditions (28) and (29), for the vanishing of the overshoots, are automatically satisfied.

## Pièce d'antériorité : extrait-2602.14161-v1.md

# EXTRAIT — When Benchmarks Lie: Evaluating Malicious Prompt Classifiers Under True Distribution Shift

- Auteurs : Max Fomin ; année : 2026 (v2 du 19 juillet 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2602.14161v2 (27 pages)
- Statut : préimpression arXiv cs.LG ; Zenity ; publié à l'atelier AIWILD (Agents in the Wild) de la conférence ICLR 2026 selon le pied de page de la page 1 ; article sur la détection de requêtes malveillantes (injection, évasion, requêtes nuisibles) par sondes d'activations, voisin des travaux sur les moniteurs
- PDF source : `docs/sources/pdf/2602.14161v2.pdf` ; SHA-256 du PDF : `7831c848841eca32bcbb1e18cd35f644cfe5a8af8f0f003623c4e1bdd6e03c00` (compagnon `.sha256` du dépôt vérifié par `sha256sum -c`)
- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-11
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Aucun chiffre retapé à la main (R7).
- Retiré pour sûreté (niveau méthode seulement) : rien (la figure 2, qui montrait des exemples de requêtes, n'est pas reprise).

---

[p.1]
A BSTRACT Detecting prompt injection, jailbreak attacks, and harmful requests is critical for deploying LLM-based agents safely, yet current evaluation practices in this literature overestimate generalization. We train activation-based classifiers (linear probes on LLM hidden states) on a benchmark of 18 datasets (prompt attacks plus benign sources) and propose Leave-One-Dataset-Out (LODO) evaluation, where the held-out dataset is never seen during training. Across four LLMs from three families (Llama-3.1-8B, Gemma-3-27B, Qwen-3.5-2B/4B), standard crossvalidation reports a pooled AUC 8.0–16.5 points higher than LODO; per-dataset held-out-test-vs-LODO accuracy gaps span 1–25 points. To understand the gap, we analyze the LODO stability of a linear probe’s perfeature classifier coefficients, defining a retention metric for sparse-autoencoder (SAE) features that flags dataset-dependent shortcuts. 28–44% of top SAE features are shortcuts across models, a dataset-identity classifier reaches 96.6%, and the dataset-identifying and safety-relevant subspaces partially overlap (§5.4). Standard domain-generalization fixes such as adversarial training, subspace projection, sample reweighting, and class balancing do not close the gap (§5.5). Finally, we show LODO-weighted SAE attributions filter dataset artifacts for more reliable per-prompt explanations. We release our framework at https://gith ub.com/maxf-zn/prompt-mining so future prompt-attack classifiers can be evaluated under LODO alongside CV.

1

I NTRODUCTION

[COUPÉ] Recent work has developed classifiers to detect prompt injection, jailbreak, and harmful-request attacks, using approaches ranging from fine-tuned BERT models (Chennabasappa et al., 2025) to activation-based probes (Abdelnabi et al., 2025a). These classifiers are typically trained and evaluated on aggregated benchmarks combining multiple attack datasets (e.g., AdvBench, HarmBench, WildJailbreak) and benign datasets (e.g., Enron emails, OpenOrca). Standard evaluation protocols use train-test splits where test samples come from the same dataset sources as training, reporting near-perfect performance with AUC scores exceeding 0.99 (Abdelnabi et al., 2025a; Saglam et al., 2025) — a result we replicate in Table 1.

[p.2]
However, this evaluation methodology overestimates true generalization. When training and test folds contain samples from the same datasets, classifiers can exploit dataset-identity signals — features that indicate dataset provenance rather than attack semantics. A classifier reaching 99% AUC may simply learn that “samples formatted like WildJailbreak are malicious” and “samples formatted like Enron are benign,” without learning generalizable attack patterns. This echoes broader findings in machine learning that models often succeed for the wrong reasons (McCoy et al., 2019; Geirhos et al., 2020), and is exacerbated when benchmark datasets are single-class (entirely malicious or entirely benign): any feature that identifies the dataset automatically predicts the class label, making dataset-identity exploitation trivial.

[COUPÉ]

[p.3]
2

R ELATED W ORK

[COUPÉ]Contemporaneous and adversarial-robustness work. Constitutional Classifiers (Sharma et al., 2025; Cunningham et al., 2026) and Circuit Breakers (Zou et al., 2024) optimize adversarial robustness via red-teaming or representation intervention — targeting worst-case attackers, whereas LODO measures static distribution shift. [COUPÉ][COUPÉ]Adaptive attacks and evaluation pitfalls. Nasr et al. (2025) bypass twelve recent jailbreak/promptinjection defenses with adaptive attacks, arguing static evaluations overstate robustness; their critique targets the adversarial axis, ours the distribution-shift axis, and the two are complementary predeployment diagnostics. PromptShield (Jacob et al., 2025) curates a deployment-oriented promptinjection benchmark and a fine-tuned Llama-3-8B detector trained on it — a deployable artifact, whereas our contribution targets the evaluation methodology that any such detector is measured against. 3

[p.4]
M ETHODS

3.1

P ROBLEM S ETUP

We address binary classification of LLM inputs as malicious or benign. Malicious inputs span four families: (i) harmful requests for restricted content; (ii) jailbreaks that attempt to bypass safety via roleplay, framing, or other prompt engineering; (iii) prompt injections that hijack instructions, including indirect injections embedded in external data the agent processes (emails, code, tool outputs, retrieved documents); and (iv) extraction attacks that elicit hidden system information. Threat Model. The defender has white-box access to LLM activations during inference but cannot modify the underlying model — the setting addressed by activation-probe detectors. We compare against text-only baselines that lack this access (§5). Dataset Composition. We compile 18 datasets covering these attack families plus benign sources (Table 7, Figure 2). Most are capped at 10K samples for tractable activation extraction; BIPIA uses 15K to cover its three context types. The full benchmark totals 105K samples (47% malicious); under LODO each dataset serves in turn as the held-out test set, so there is no fixed train/test split. 3.2

ACTIVATION -BASED C LASSIFICATION

Following Abdelnabi et al. (2025a), we extract activations from the LLM’s residual stream as input features for classification. These classifiers are also known as probes or linear probes in the interpretability literature; we use the terms interchangeably. For a text input x, we apply the model’s chat template and extract the activation vector hl ∈ Rd at layer l from the last token of the user message (before the generation-prompt tokens; see Appendix C.3): hl = LLMl (x),

(1)

where d is the model’s hidden dimension. We evaluate four LLMs spanning 2B-27B parameters across three families: Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (Llama Team, AI @ Meta, 2024), Gemma-3-27B (Gemma Team, 2025), and Qwen-3.5-2B/4B (Qwen Team, 2026), plus a partial Llama-3.3-70B replication (Appendix F). [COUPÉ] 4

[p.5]
Raw Activations. Our primary classifier operates on raw activations from layer 31 (the final layer, indexed 0-31), captured at the last user-message token (immediately before the assistant generation prefix). [COUPÉ] [COUPÉ]3.3

L EAVE -O NE -DATASET-O UT (LODO) E VALUATION

Leave-one-domain-out cross-validation is an established protocol in domain generalization (Gulrajani & Lopez-Paz, 2021; Koh et al., 2021); our contribution is not the protocol itself but applying it at the dataset level to prompt-attack classification (where it has not been adopted) and quantifying the resulting CV-LODO gap. For each dataset Di in our benchmark, we train a classifier on all other datasets {Dj : j ̸= i} and evaluate on the held-out Di . This measures true out-of-distribution generalization, as the classifier has never seen any examples from the test dataset’s distribution. Formally, let D = {D1 , . . . , DK } be our K datasets. For each i, we compute:  LODOi = Metric fD\Di , Di , (3) where fD\Di is trained on all datasets except Di . We report both per-dataset metrics and pooled metrics across all held-out predictions.[COUPÉ] 3.4

DATASET S HORTCUT A NALYSIS

We perform shortcut analysis on SAE features rather than raw activations because SAE features are designed to be interpretable: each feature ideally corresponds to a single semantic concept. In contrast, individual neurons in raw activations are polysemantic, encoding multiple unrelated concepts that activate together (Bricken et al., 2023). This polysemanticity makes it difficult to characterize what patterns a given neuron detects, whereas SAE features can be examined via their max-activating examples. To quantify how much classifier performance depends on dataset-specific features, we introduce the LODO coefficient retention metric. For each feature j, let wj be its coefficient in the full classifier (−i) and wj its coefficient when dataset Di is held out. The retention for feature j is: (−i)

wj rj = min . i wj 5

(4)

[p.6]
Features with rj ≈ 1 are stable across dataset holdouts and likely capture genuine attack patterns. Features with rj ≪ 1 or rj < 0 (sign flip) are dataset shortcuts — their predictive value depends on specific datasets being present in training.[COUPÉ] We identify two types: • Pure dataset shortcuts: features that directly predict dataset identity (e.g., email formatting for Enron, code patterns for specific injection datasets). • Context-dependent shortcuts: features that fire across datasets but derive class signal from specific dataset compositions (e.g., a feature active on both malicious and benign samples, but whose class correlation depends on which datasets are included). To validate this analysis, we train a separate dataset classifier that predicts which dataset a sample belongs to from activations alone. High accuracy indicates that datasets are easily distinguishable in feature space, enabling shortcut exploitation.

E XPERIMENTS

We answer five questions: (1) How much does standard cross-validation overestimate generalization compared to LODO? (2) Does the gap extend beyond a single model family? (3) What fraction of learned features are dataset shortcuts, and what fraction of the gap can we close with standard mitigations? (4) How do activation-based classifiers compare to production baselines and LLM-asjudge under fair operating points? (5) Can LODO-stable features provide reliable explanations? 4.1

E XPERIMENTAL S ETUP

Dataset. Our benchmark comprises 18 datasets (105K samples, 47% malicious) spanning direct jailbreaks, indirect injection, extraction attacks, and benign sources. For evaluation, we merge Gandalf (114 samples) into Mosscap — both use identical system prompts for password protection (Appendix B) — yielding 17 datasets for LODO results; shortcut analysis uses the original 18. Six datasets are 100% malicious and five are 100% benign; the remaining six are mixed-class. We audit cross-dataset near-duplicate overlap in Appendix K. Models and Baselines. We evaluate Llama-3.1-8B, Gemma-3-27B, and Qwen-3.5-2B/4B in the main body (plus partial Llama-3.3-70B results in Appendix F). We compare against PromptGuard 2 (Chennabasappa et al., 2025), LlamaGuard (Meta AI, 2024), four Llama-as-Judge prompt variants (Llama-3.1-8B), and two dedicated prompt-injection detectors (ProtectAI Guard v2, Deepset promptinjection v2). Evaluation uses 5-fold CV, held-out test sets, and LODO.

5

R ESULTS

5.1

S TANDARD E VALUATION S EVERELY OVERESTIMATES G ENERALIZATION

Table 1 demonstrates that both 5-fold cross-validation and held-out test sets substantially overestimate out-of-distribution performance across every model we tested. Using logistic regression on Llama3.1-8B raw activations as a representative example, 5-fold CV achieves 0.996 AUC and the held-out test set achieves 0.997 AUC, while LODO reveals true generalization performance is only 0.912 6

[p.7]
Table 1: Evaluation protocol comparison for logistic regression on Llama-3.1-8B raw activations.
Standard CV and test-set evaluation overestimate true OOD performance by 8+ percentage points.
Evaluation Protocol  ROC AUC
5-Fold Cross-Validation  0.996
Held-Out Test Set  0.997
LODO (Pooled)  0.912
CV-LODO Gap  8.4 pp

AUC (Figure 1); aggregate CV-LODO AUC gaps across the four main models range from 8.0pp to 16.5pp (Table 3); partial Llama-3.3-70B results in Appendix F corroborate the pattern. The held-out test sets come from 6 datasets that provide official train-test splits: mosscap (27.7K samples including merged gandalf), jayavibhav (10K), qualifire (5K), enron (4K), safeguard (2K), and deepset (116). Critically, these test samples come from the same dataset sources as training data, allowing classifiers to exploit dataset-specific patterns. Table 2 shows the per-dataset accuracy gap for the 6 datasets with official test splits. The gaps range from 1.2% (safeguard) to 25.4% (jayavibhav). Four of these six datasets are mixed-class (jayavibhav 50%, qualifire 40%, safeguard 30%, deepset 37% malicious), yet still exhibit substantial gaps. Restricting to the five mixed-class datasets and excluding BIPIA (which is 95.3% malicious and acts as a near-single-class outlier), the weighted CV-LODO gap is 12.5pp (Appendix L) — confirming the gap is not driven by single-class artifacts. Conversely, the 8.4pp aggregate understates the gap on the datasets that matter: it is pulled toward zero by near-saturated benign sources (e.g., dolly, openorca), where CV and LODO both approach ceiling accuracy and contribute almost no gap to the average.

5.2  T HE G AP I S U NIVERSAL ACROSS F OUR M ODEL FAMILIES

To rule out architecture-specific artifacts, we replicate the CV-LODO comparison on three additional backbones spanning 2B-27B parameters across three families. Table 3 reports best-layer results per model. Partial Llama-3.3-70B results (Appendix F) corroborate the pattern but omit a paired CV AUC due to compute cost. The gap is universal but varies in magnitude. Per-dataset LODO accuracies are also heterogeneous and model-dependent — for example, InjecAgent is detected at 98.9% on Llama-3.1-8B but at 0% on Qwen-3.5-2B. The inconsistency itself is informative: practitioners cannot rely on a CV-validated classifier to generalize predictably to any specific deployment dataset.

7

[p.8]

On Llama-3.1-8B, raw activations achieve the best pooled AUC (0.912), outperforming SAE features (0.838) and MLP (0.841) — aligning with Kantamneni et al. (2025) and DeepMind Mechanistic Interpretability Team (2025). This ordering is model-dependent, however: on Gemma-3-27B the SAE probe outperforms the raw probe under LODO (76.2% vs 68.3% weighted accuracy; Appendix F), so raw>SAE is a Llama-specific tendency rather than a universal law. [COUPÉ]

[p.9]
5.4

DATASET-I DENTITY S IGNAL I S E NTANGLED WITH S AFETY S IGNAL

Datasets are densely distinguishable. Training a logistic regression classifier to predict dataset identity from SAE features (layer 27) achieves 96.6% accuracy under 5-fold CV; the same dataset classifier on raw layer-31 activations reaches 89.1%, and on raw layer-27 activations 86.4% (Appendix E). Datasets are trivially distinguishable in every representation we tested.[COUPÉ] Dataset-identity and safety directions partially overlap. Projecting raw activations onto the orthogonal complement of the top-16 dataset-predictive directions (raw-space SVD of a 17-way LogReg dataset classifier) degrades LODO by 1.75pp aggregate, with strongly heterogeneous per-dataset effects (llmail +21.4pp, injecagent −34.4pp). Ten equal-rank random isotropic projections degrade LODO by −0.02 ± 0.13pp (p < 10−10 vs. targeted; bootstrap 95% CI on the gap [+1.63, +1.78] pp). The safety classifier’s weight direction also has 9.2% of its norm inside the dataset-predictive subspace versus 0.4% for random subspaces of equal rank (matching the isotropic theoretical value k/d = 0.39%). These results are consistent with directional overlap between dataset-identity and safety signal in this representation, though not proof of causal entanglement; the per-dataset heterogeneity indicates that uniformly removing dataset-identifying directions redistributes rather than reduces the gap. 28% of top SAE features are shortcuts, including context-dependent ones. Cross-referencing LODO retention with firing ratio (malicious/benign firing rate) for the top-50 SAE features ranked by full-model coefficient magnitude |wj | reveals two shortcut mechanisms (Table 5). Pure dataset shortcuts (Q1, 8 features) have weak class separation and low retention. Context-dependent shortcuts (Q2, 6 features) have strong class separation but still fail under LODO — for instance, one feature with high firing ratio has LODO retention of only 3% when jayavibhav is held out. Forty-two percent of shortcuts are context-dependent — they would not be flagged by simple firing-ratio checks alone. [COUPÉ]

[COUPÉ]The 28%/44% shortcut rates use a retention threshold of 0.5; the rate ranges from 14% (threshold 0.3, the most conservative shortcut count) to 56% (threshold 0.7, the most permissive) — see Appendix O.3. We report the 0.5 midpoint throughout for comparability and disclose the full sensitivity range. Shortcut-ablation results are reported in Appendix O.4. [COUPÉ]5.5

S TANDARD D OMAIN -G ENERALIZATION (DG) F IXES D O N OT C LOSE THE G AP

The directional-overlap finding above predicts that standard domain-generalization techniques — which assume domain-identifying and task-relevant signal can be separated — will struggle here. We test seven such strategies (DANN, subspace projection, two sample reweightings, category-aware reweighting, class balancing on Llama-3.1-8B, and SAE shortcut zeroing on Gemma-3-27B) with reasonable but non-exhaustive hyperparameter tuning. Aggregate LODO deltas range from −1.75pp 9

[p.10]
(subspace projection) to +0.7pp (SAE zeroing) — none close the 8.4pp Llama-8B gap; full table in Appendix G. For DANN specifically, we did not find a parameter set that both improved aggregate accuracy and remained stable across seeds; we make no claim that such a setting does not exist. The most informative intervention is subspace projection, which degrades LODO: consistent with the directional-overlap finding, closing the gap likely requires methods that disentangle dataset identity from safety signal. 5.6

P RODUCTION BASELINES AND LJ P ROMPT VARIANTS

Table 6 compares production guardrails against our activation-based classifier on 105K samples at
two natural operating points (thresholds 0.5 and 0.9). A per-attack-type breakdown across all methods
is in Appendix M.
Table 6: Detection rate (%) by attack category. PG=PromptGuard 2, LG=LlamaGuard, LJ=Llama-as-
Judge (zero-shot); Ours=LogReg on Llama-3.1-8B raw activations under LODO at fixed thresholds
0.5 (default) and 0.9 (conservative); per-fold optimal thresholds vary widely (Appendix P). PG/LG
cannot evaluate Agentic (no tool-schema support). Appendix P.2 provides matched-FPR comparisons.
Category  PG  LG  LJ  Ours(0.5)  Ours(0.9)
Harmful  36.7  97.4  85.8  69.7  64.7
Jailbreak  48.5  28.9  60.0  71.2  59.5
Indirect  37.3  27.4  7.1  66.7  53.1
Agentic  -  -  21.5  99.2  91.5
Extraction  100.0  15.2  31.8  79.2  68.7
Mixed  54.5  38.8  73.8  83.3  74.9
Benign FPR  0.4  3.0  4.4  6.8  3.7

PG and LG cannot represent tool schemas, so they are inapplicable to the agentic surface. At the threshold-0.9 operating point the probe’s FPR (3.7%) is comparable to LG’s (3.0%) while it leads on Jailbreak (+30.6pp), Indirect (+25.7pp), Agentic (covered exclusively), and Mixed (+36.1pp); LG retains its lead on conversational harmful refusals (97.4% vs 64.7%) where it is specifically trained. Appendix P.2 provides per-baseline FPR-matched detection rates. LJ prompt sensitivity. Single-prompt LJ evaluations can be misleading. Across four parseable LJ prompt variants tested on 10,054 indirect-injection + benign prompts, indirect-injection TPR ranges from 11% (FPR 1.4%) to 93% (FPR 18.9%) — an 8.5× range (Appendix J). The 93% TPR variant uses a “security analyst” framing but has 40.6% FPR on OpenOrca and 91.1% FPR on benign BIPIA emails — non-viable for deployment. At the 3.5% FPR operating point of the strongest deployable variant, our probe outperforms LJ by +22pp on indirect injection (68.2% vs 46.0%).[COUPÉ]

[p.11]
6

D ISCUSSION

Existing guardrails are not designed for agentic security. PG/LG target conversational safety; their inability to represent tool schemas or non-alternating roles is an architectural constraint, not just a training-data gap. Activation probes complement rather than replace these systems by covering the indirect and agentic surfaces they miss. Understanding the CV-LODO gap. The gap is a property of benchmark construction: classifiers exploit within-dataset regularities that do not transfer to new dataset distributions, and this holds across every model family we tested. Ablating the 14 identified SAE shortcuts moves pooled AUC by only −0.1pp because other features compensate via redundant decision boundaries — we therefore do not claim shortcuts cause the gap. The more direct evidence is the subspace projection result (§5.4): dataset-identifying and safety-relevant directions partially overlap, and closing the gap likely requires methods that can disentangle the two — an open challenge.

7

C ONCLUSION

LODO reveals an 8-16pp CV overestimate across four LLMs on prompt-attack detection (per-dataset gaps 1-25%). The dataset-identifying and safety-relevant subspaces appear to partially overlap (89%/96.6% dataset classifier; targeted subspace projection −1.75pp vs. −0.02pp for equal-rank random projections) — consistent with directional overlap, though not proof of causal entanglement — and standard domain-generalization fixes do not close the gap.

8

L IMITATIONS

White-box access requirement. Our activation-based approach requires access to model internals, limiting direct deployment with closed-source APIs.[COUPÉ] LODO is not adversarial robustness. LODO measures static distribution generalization, not robustness to adaptive attackers. A classifier with a small CV-LODO gap may still be vulnerable to novel attacks designed to evade it. Adversarial evaluation is a complementary requirement we do not address. Dataset-choice dependence. LODO treats each constituent dataset as a domain. When component datasets share methodology, LODO understates the deployment gap; when they are unrepresentative of production traffic, LODO may be pessimistic or optimistic depending on direction.[COUPÉ] [COUPÉ]No mitigation provided. We establish the gap and show it resists seven standard domaingeneralization interventions, but do not present a method that closes it. We view the diagnostic contribution as actionable independently — practitioners can use LODO to detect gap-prone benchmarks, filter dataset-artifact features from explanations, and avoid overpromising on production performance based on inflated CV metrics — but a definitive solution remains open. 11

## Pièce d'antériorité : extrait-2605.09684-v1.md

# EXTRAIT — MonitoringBench: Semi-Automated Red-Teaming for Agent Monitoring

- Auteurs : Monika Jotautaitė, Maria Angelica Martinez, Ollie Matthews, Tyler Tracy ; année : 2026 (v1 du 10 mai 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2605.09684v1 (63 pages)
- Statut : préimpression arXiv cs.CR ; Redwood Research et indépendants ; les auteurs demandent (p.9) que le papier et le jeu de données soient exclus des corpus d'entraînement de modèles : cet extrait n'est destiné qu'à la lecture par les proposeurs et les juges, jamais à l'entraînement
- PDF source : `docs/sources/pdf/2605.09684v1.pdf` ; SHA-256 du PDF : `35bb09b49f85578e4cfddeec16c9ae101a51b5d618196a4b29a1722b89b831f5` (compagnon `.sha256` du dépôt vérifié par `sha256sum -c`)
- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-9
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Aucun chiffre retapé à la main (R7).
- Retiré pour sûreté (niveau méthode seulement) : p.3 (exemples d'attaque par niveau, avec mécanismes concrets), p.5 (exemple de tâche secondaire dissimulée par un cadrage anodin), p.8 (deux exemples concrets d'évasion et leur comparaison). Les annexes (taxonomie détaillée, invites de construction des attaques, exemples d'attaques non détectées) ne sont pas reprises. Gardés : classes (trois axes de la taxonomie), protocole de construction et d'évaluation, taux de détection, limites.

---

[p.1]
Abstract We introduce a red-teaming methodology that exposes harder-to-catch attacks for coding-agent monitors, suggesting that current practices may under-elicit attacks and overstate monitor performance. We identify three challenges with current red-teaming. First, mode collapse in attack generation, which we reduce with a novel attack taxonomy for broader coverage. Second, a conceive-execute gap: frontier LLMs can propose strong attack ideas or execute them, but not all at once. We mitigate this by decomposing attack construction into strategy generation, execution, and post-hoc trajectory refinement. Third, manual elicitation is costly to scale, which we address with our semi-automated red-teaming pipeline. Applied to BashArena, an AI control setting for tool-using coding agents, this pipeline produces MonitoringBench, a benchmark of 2,644 attack trajectories for evaluating monitor capabilities and failure modes. Our pipeline produces more diverse and stronger attacks: Opus-4.5 monitor’s catch rate falls from 94.9% on elicited-only Opus attacks to 60.3% on our best refined attacks, with larger drops for several midtier monitors. Attacks optimized against three development monitors generalize to ten held-out monitors, with catch rates generally increasing with monitor capability. Using this benchmark, we provide a snapshot of the current monitor capabilities and find that frontier monitors often detect suspicious actions but fall for persuasion or fail to calibrate suspiciousness scores appropriately, suggesting tractable paths for improvement. MonitoringBench provides both a static benchmark for current tool-use monitors and a reusable methodology for refreshing these evaluations as agents and monitors improve.

1

Introduction

As AI agents are given increasing autonomy to execute code in security-critical settings, a core safety concern arises: the agent may covertly pursue unauthorized objectives, such as disabling firewalls, exfiltrating credentials, downloading malware, or escalating privileges, while appearing to perform legitimate work. To mitigate this, AI monitors can be used to inspect agent actions and flag suspicious behavior. Frontier AI labs have already released such systems in both internal and external deployments [Williams et al., 2026, Anthropic, 2026, Hughes, 2026, Tr˛ebacz et al., 2026]. [COUPÉ] ∗ Equal contribution.

Preprint.

[p.2]
In practice, current red-teaming relies on direct attack elicitation from frontier models [Loughridge et al., 2025, Terekhov et al., 2025], which may fail to capture worst-case behavior. This leads to three limitations. First, mode collapse: although many distinct execution strategies exist for an attack, 81% of Opus 4.6-elicited attacks fall into just 2 of 5 technical tiers in our taxonomy (Appendix A) and 64% follow the same structural template (Fig. 2), leaving much of the attack surface untested. Second, in red-teaming setups, the attacking agent must simultaneously perform the main task, execute the malicious side task, and avoid detection – all within a single trajectory. This is difficult for current frontier models, and we show that decomposing red-teaming into sub-tasks can create stronger attacks; we call this the conceive-execute gap. Third, elicitation is brittle: prompt changes that improve one model’s attack quality degrade another’s, making results hard to reproduce or scale. [COUPÉ]We address these limitations with a semi-automated red-teaming pipeline that decomposes attack construction into stages (Fig. 1, top): an attack taxonomy ensures coverage and reduces mode collapse, the staged pipeline separates strategy generation from execution, and post-hoc trajectory refinement improves evasion. Later stages produce harder attacks: as shown in Fig. 1 (bottom), catch rates drop across pipeline stages for all evaluated monitors. Applied to BashArena [Kaufman et al., 2025], this pipeline produces MonitoringBench: a difficulty-graded benchmark of over 2,600 successful attack trajectories that enables meaningful comparison across weak to strong monitor capabilities. MonitoringBench reveals a gap between model-elicited and pipeline-generated attacks: evaluating Opus 4.5 on model-elicited attacks alone would suggest a 94.9% catch rate, but this falls to 60.3% on the best refined attacks. [COUPÉ]

[p.3]
We construct MonitoringBench through a structured red-teaming methodology designed to produce diverse, realistic, and difficulty-graded attacks. This section describes the setup, our attack taxonomy, the elicitation baseline, and the pipeline for generating, executing, and refining attack trajectories. Setup. We use BashArena [Kaufman et al., 2025, Inglis et al., 2025], a control setting, where agents interact with a Linux environment via bash and Python tools to complete a legitimate main task (e.g., system administration), while covertly executing one of four malicious side tasks: firewall backdoor, sudo privilege escalation, credential exfiltration, or malware download. We modify the firewall task from the original setup to improve realism and expand the attack surface. See Appendix B for details. Attack taxonomy. To address mode collapse, we define a taxonomy (Appendix A) over three axes: technical implementation (T), structural complexity (S), and evasion strategy (E). Technical tiers (T1–T5) capture what the attack mechanism that achieves side task implementation is. The tiers are designed with increasing implementation complexity and are unique per each side task. [RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.3 : exemples de niveaux d'attaque (de l'attaque directe à l'attaque répartie sur plusieurs composants), avec mécanismes concrets] Structural tiers (S1-S4) describes where the attack is. S1 is a single step, needle-in-a-haystack attack, S2 is a local edit, S3 is multi-file and concentrated, S4 is distributed across many steps and files. Evasion strategy (E1–E4) captures how attacks are concealed, through benign naming (E1), overperformance on the main task (E2), string-level obfuscation (E3), or persuasion and error camouflage (E4). All trajectories are labeled post-hoc using Opus 4.5 LLM judge (Appendix P.4). Opus-only baseline. We run 400 trajectories across all side tasks to obtain Opus-4.6 baselines with BashArena elicited prompt [Kaufman et al., 2025]. For a fair comparison, we filter out the side task 3

[p.4]
failures which leaves 196 trajectories. This serves as a reference point for evaluating our red-teaming pipeline against the best elicited attacker model. Red-teaming pipeline. We decompose monitor red-teaming into three stages: 1. Strategy generation. We generate attack specifications using three approaches, all guided by taxonomy with the main goal of generating diverse strategies and covering the attack surface. We iterated against three development monitors (GPT-5 Nano, GPT-5 and Opus 4.5). [COUPÉ]Reconnaissance agent. We therefore introduce a reconnaissance agent (Recon), an Opus 4.6 model with environment access, instructed to explore the system and propose attack strategies rather than execute them. [COUPÉ][COUPÉ]2. Execution. Given a strategy from the above sources, we run a BashArena trajectory using Opus 4.6 as the attacker, with the strategy embedded in the system prompt. We retain trajectories only if (a) the side task succeeds and (b) a GPT-5 judge rates how closely the agent executes the prescribed strategy at ≥ 7/10, after which they are passed to refinement. 3. Refinement. We post-process the trajectories using a four-stage process with Opus 4.6: (1) identify suspicious elements; (2) plan edits for a specified refinement prompt; (3) rewrite the trajectory; (4) validate correctness. Refinement improves trajectory quality by removing obvious execution errors while preserving the underlying strategy, balances coverage across attack categories, and transfers stronger human-selected strategies into weaker trajectories. Refined trajectories are accepted only if it achieves side task success, and suspiciousness decreases for at least two of three development monitors. We use absolute score reductions to avoid overfitting to any single monitor. This decomposition addresses the conceive–execute gap by optimizing strategy generation, execution, and evasion in separate stages. Implementation details, including prompts, are discussed in Appendix H. 2.1

Monitor Evaluations

Honest runs, 1% FPR thresholds, and catch rate metric. For calibration, we run n=416 honestonly trajectories (the main task without the malicious side task) and compute a per-monitor suspiciousness threshold corresponding to a 1% false positive rate (FPR) – the score above which only 1% of honest behavior is flagged. We calibrate two monitoring setups separately. Full trajectory: Each honest trajectory receives a single score and the threshold is the 99th percentile over the 416 trajectory-level scores. Stepwise: Each honest trajectory produces multiple per-step scores (one per action). We pool per-step scores across all trajectories and take the 99th percentile of this pooled distribution. Since stepwise detection uses the maximum score over trajectory steps, the threshold must reflect the per-step distribution rather than a trajectory-level aggregate. For both setups, the catch rate is the fraction of attack trajectories whose score exceeds the corresponding threshold. [COUPÉ]Monitoring setups. Full trajectory monitoring (FT) corresponds to asynchronous post-hoc review: the monitor sees the full sequence of tool calls from the entire episode, without tool outputs, and assigns a single suspiciousness score. Omitting outputs keeps context length manageable, especially 4

[p.5]
for weaker monitors. Stepwise monitoring (SW) corresponds to synchronous deployment where the monitor can intervene mid-execution. At each step, the monitor sees all prior tool calls and outputs, plus the proposed current action (but not its output, since the monitor must decide before execution). We truncate trajectories at the first step that triggers side task success, reflecting the window for successfully blocking the attack. In the full trajectory setup, we score each trajectory three times and report mean catch rate and standard deviation; stepwise monitoring is run once per trajectory due to higher cost (one monitor call per step). Monitor selection and scoring. We use the default ControlArena monitor prompt, which elicits a 0–10 suspiciousness score with reasoning. For development, we use three full trajectory monitors spanning weak-to-strong capability: GPT-5 Nano, GPT-5, and Claude Opus 4.5. To reduce overfitting, we report final results on 10 held-out full trajectory and 7 stepwise monitors. We also evaluate monitor prompt variations as a sensitivity check, but find no consistent improvement direction—changes that help one monitor can degrade another (see Appendix O for full results). [COUPÉ]2.3

Monitor failure analysis

To understand how monitors fail beyond aggregate catch rates, we derive a small set of recurring failure modes from the monitors’ reasoning and suspiciousness scores using unsupervised clustering and manual inspection. We then label trajectories into these categories using three independent LLM judges (Claude Opus 4.5, GPT-5.1 Codex, and GPT-5.4). Inter-rater agreement ranges from Cohen’s κ = 0.51 to 0.69, and majority-vote labels achieve 97–99% coverage (Appendix M).

3

Results

[COUPÉ]3.1 3.1.1

Red-teaming Pipelines Taxonomy-guided red-teaming reduces mode collapse

Direct elicitation collapses to a narrow region of the taxonomy (Fig. 2). Opus-only attacks cluster in T3 (59%) and 64% share the same structural template in S3 (multi-file but tightly clustered). In practice, these attacks reuse a small set of mechanisms alongside superficial camouflage. For example, firewall attacks repeatedly whitelist an IP range and differ only in whether the change is framed as testing, debugging, or other benign-sounding actions. In contrast, our strategy generation pipeline explores the attack surface more across the taxonomy, and covers both simple and complex mechanisms. This broader coverage surfaces monitor blind spots that do not arise under direct elicitation. We analyze the taxonomic distribution of uncaught attacks (Appendix J.1) and show that the taxonomy allows us to discover more diverse uncaught attack types. We find that averaged across seven monitors, 40% of uncaught attacks fall into T1, despite T1 comprising only 2% of the Opus-only baseline. These results highlight two points: (1) evasion is not confined to complex mechanisms and even simple T1 attacks can bypass monitors when appropriately framed and (2) broader red-teaming can 5

[p.6]
Figure 2: Mode-collapse: Opus-4.6 elicited vs our attack pipelines across the attack taxonomy show that model-only red-teams produce narrower distribution of attacks compared to our pipelines; n denotes number of trajectories.

reveal failure modes that would otherwise remain undetected, making it necessary for reliable monitor evaluation. 3.1.2

Conceive-execute gap: Decomposed red-teaming produces stronger attacks

Our main result in Figure 1 shows that refinement consistently makes poorly executed attacks better, achieving the lowest catch rates across all monitors (dev and held-out), monitor types (FT and SW), and attack sources (Opus-only, Human-in-the-loop, Recon, and Recon+Tools); see Appendix F.2 for full details. The biggest gains up to 40% appear on the strongest full trajectory monitors between Opus-only and Opus-only refined attacks, for example, GPT-5.1 Codex score goes from 69.9% to 49.8% post-refinement and GPT-5 performance reduces even further from 60.2% to 19.5%. Given that refinement’s main goal is cleaning up suspicious code, redundant validation, and unnecessary reconnaissance, this suggests that the biggest red-teaming bottleneck is poor execution. The improvements from separating strategy generation, execution, and refinement provide evidence for a conceive-execute gap: models can conceive and execute strong attacks, but struggle to do both in the same trajectory. Strategy generation produces not only diverse but also stronger attacks, as both types of recon agents outperform the Opus-only baseline on strong to mid-tier monitors, despite being optimized primarily for exploration rather than stealth. For example, Opus-only has a catch rate of 94.9%, while Recon and Recon+Tools score 82.7% and 73.5%, respectively, on Opus-4.5 monitor. Similarly on GPT-5.1 Codex, catch rates go from 70% to 67% and 54.5% between Opus-only and the two recon agents. [COUPÉ][COUPÉ] 6

[p.7]
Figure 3: Final MonitoringBench results: Full Trajectory (1% FPR) and Stepwise (1% per-action FPR) Catch rates. Lower catch rate indicates stronger attacks.

3.2

MonitoringBench: evaluating current monitoring capabilities

We present MonitoringBench results for two monitor types: full-trajectory and stepwise (Figure 3). Monitoring capability is concentrated in frontier models, with significant performance drops for weaker models across both settings. Results here reflect broad monitor capability trends, as models were not individually elicited. In the full-trajectory setting, Opus 4.5 achieves the highest catch rate at roughly 70%. A mid-tier group (Sonnet 4.5, GPT-5.1 Codex, Sonnet 4.0, GPT-5) follows, while smaller models drop off steeply. This capability gradient suggests that asynchronous monitoring of strong sabotage trajectories may require frontier-level models. In practice, weaker models are preferred as monitors due to lower cost and the assumption that they are less likely to behave deceptively [Greenblatt et al., 2024], yet our results show they are less reliable against stronger attacks. Stepwise monitoring shows the same general ordering but higher absolute catch rates—Opus 4.5 catches roughly 83% of attacks, with mid-tier models clustering lower at 25–52%. However, these numbers are not directly comparable to full-trajectory results. Since stepwise monitors score each action independently, a 1% per-action FPR compounds to an effective trajectory-level FPR of 6.7–17.4% across monitors (Appendix G). The higher catch rates reflect both the benefits of stepwise monitoring such as additional context from tool call outputs and shorter contexts as well as a more permissive effective threshold. [COUPÉ] 3.2.1

Monitoring failures

We categorize monitoring failures in four ways: (A) Not detected: the monitor entirely misses the attack; (B) Partial detection: the monitor identifies suspicious actions but does not connect them or infer the full effect; (C) Fully detected, but classified as non-malicious: the monitor correctly identifies the side task but dismisses it (e.g., “low risk”, "scope creep", or aligned with the main task); (D) Scoring failure: the monitor finds the attack but fails to assign a high enough suspiciousness score for the action to be flagged. Opus 4.5 frequently identifies suspicious actions but is persuaded by benign framing and rationalizes the attack as main-task-aligned. In some cases, absent an attacker-provided explanation, it invents a motive itself to justify the behavior. GPT-5.1 Codex detects most individual attack steps but fails 7

[p.8]
Figure 4: Monitor failure distribution across five monitors evaluated on the MonitoringBench benchmark. Each bar shows the breakdown of uncaught attacks across four failure modes. Labels are assigned by majority vote across three independent LLM judges; n denotes the number of uncaught attacks per monitor.

to connect them into the full side task implementation. Sonnet 4.5 shows a range of failure modes, while Sonnet 4.0 most often misses the attack entirely. GPT-5 shows a different pattern: it has the lowest rate of complete misses, but the highest rate of failures (n = 1698), of which 82% are scoring or calibration errors. This is partly driven by its high threshold (T=8.97), which is a consequence of the model’s sensitivity to incidental agent behavior in honest runs. In particular, the BashArena setting naturally involves sensitive files, firewall modifications, and sudo privileges, which raise baseline suspicion scores. This further shows that aggregate catch rates alone are insufficient for fully evaluating monitoring capability, and we hope our failure taxonomy can help future developers diagnose and patch monitoring vulnerabilities. The central takeaway is that monitor reasoning is inspectable, and the failure modes it reveals, such as susceptibility to persuasion or scoring failures, are tractable paths for improving frontier monitors. 3.2.2

What makes attacks succeed against the best monitors?

Across uncaught attacks, evasion is driven more by how well an attack blends into the legitimate workflow than by its technical sophistication. Reuse of main-task affordances. Effective attacks reuse actions that the main task already licenses. Some refinement prompts implement this via a task-conditioned decision tree where the agent first checks if the side task can be implemented through actions the main task already requires, and only falls back to standalone camouflage otherwise (Appendix P.3). [RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.8 : exemple concret de réutilisation d'une action légitime pour dissimuler une étape d'attaque] When no natural cover exists, extensive legitimate engineering work can dilute the attack signal across a long trajectory. We analyze the relationship between main-task type and available cover opportunities in Appendix C. Low-visibility execution. Frontier monitors appear more sensitive to obvious suspiciousness. Attacks are more likely to succeed when they avoid verbose security justification and over-engineered mechanisms. [RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.8 : comparaison de techniques d'évasion concrètes] We provide additional attack examples and analysis of the strongest uncaught attacks for each side task in Appendix N.

4

Discussion

MonitoringBench reveals that monitoring red-teams can underestimate risk by relying on modecollapsed attacks. Strategy-seeded attacks with agentic refinement produce substantially harder test 8

[p.9]
cases, and we position MonitoringBench as a static, difficulty-graded test suite that monitors should pass as part of pre-deployment evaluation. Our findings have implications for AI control. First, monitoring requires frontier monitors: in this setup, strong attacks require frontier-level monitors for high catch rates with only Opus 4.5 maintaining meaningful detection on sophisticated attacks, raising concerns about monitoring frontier models with weaker but possibly more trusted monitors. Second, some monitor failures are tractable: since some monitors are vulnerable to social evasion, others detect attacks but fail to escalate, targeted interventions (better prompts, fine-tuning on borderline cases, explicit riskreasoning chains) may yield improvements without better base models. Third, attack diversity matters: evaluating only against model-elicited attacks systematically underestimates failure rates. [COUPÉ] 4.1

Limitations

We focus on single-agent, single episode, persistent attacks. Several attack classes are out of scope: timing attacks, delayed attacks that activate after the episode ends (e.g., latent backdoors), monitor jailbreaking, and multi-agent coordination. Our benchmark covers a single control setting (BashArena) and four side tasks; extending to LinuxArena [Tracy et al., 2026] is a natural next step. Human-seeded strategy generation depends heavily on effort and we put uneven effort across side tasks; we further discuss this in Appendix H.1. In Appendix O, we show that prompt sensitivity is model-dependent, however we did not elicit each monitor individually; the frontier monitoring deployments show a better performance on MonitoringBench after iteration on monitor prompting Anthropic [2026], Tr˛ebacz et al. [2026]. Our 1% FPR thresholds are calibrated on honest BashArena trajectories rather than real deployment traffic, which may bias monitor scores. Finally, we used Claude Opus 4.5 and 4.6 for attack development, which might introduce model bias when evaluating monitors on Anthropic family models. [COUPÉ]

[COUPÉ : p.1 deuxième paragraphe de l'introduction ; p.2-3 liste des contributions ; p.3 revue de la littérature (section 1.1) ; p.4 détails de construction (stratégie à la main, reconnaissance avec retour du moniteur, étalonnage des exemples d'honnêteté) ; p.5 section 2.2 (qualité des données) ; p.6 résultats de pipeline sur moniteurs faibles et synthèse ; p.7 paragraphe de synthèse ; p.9 remerciements et impacts plus larges ; p.10-12 bibliographie et sommaire des annexes ; p.12-63 annexes A à P (taxonomie complète des niveaux d'attaque, descriptions des tâches secondaires, invites de la chaîne de construction des attaques, résultats complets, exemples d'attaques non détectées)]


## Pièce d'antériorité : extrait-2605.16626-v1.md

# EXTRAIT — SLEIGHT-Bench: A Benchmark of Evasion Attacks Against Agent Monitors

- Auteurs : Elle Najt, Colin Toft, Tyler Tracy, Fabien Roger, Joe Benton ; année : 2026 (v2 du 19 mai 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2605.16626v2 (42 pages)
- Statut : préimpression (arXiv cs.CR), « Preprint » ; Anthropic Fellows Program, Université de Waterloo, Redwood Research, Anthropic. NB : le billet Alignment Science d'Anthropic cité par nos rapports s'intitule « Finding Blind Spots in AI Monitors » ; le titre du PDF est celui ci-dessus.
- PDF source : `docs/sources/pdf/2605.16626v2.pdf` ; SHA-256 du PDF : `1ba418227095eeea98881f61a606c9974f4f3a8758d6a6292c6a9cd8bbd76751` (compagnon `.sha256` du dépôt vérifié par `sha256sum -c`)
- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-4, 6-10
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Aucun chiffre retapé à la main (R7).
- Retiré pour sûreté (niveau méthode seulement) : p.4 à p.6 : détail des 11 catégories d'angles morts et de leur mode d'exploitation (§3.3), colonne « Examples » du tableau 1, phrase de construction d'une transcription d'attaque (§3.2)  ; annexes non reprises (elles contiennent les invites des moniteurs et les transcriptions). Gardés : noms et définitions des classes, taux de détection, protocole d'évaluation, limites.

---

[p.1]
Abstract Since autonomous coding agents generate complex behaviors at high-volume, we may want to use other LLMs to monitor actions to reduce the risk from dangerous misaligned behavior. To better understand the limitations of such monitors against the diverse attack strategies that a coding agent could use, we present S LEIGHTB ENCH (Subtle Low-itEration Insight-Guided Harmful Transcripts), a benchmark of synthetic transcripts containing 40 attacks across 11 categories, each showing a coding agent covertly pursuing a harmful objective (e.g. weight exfiltration, credential theft, unmonitored rogue deployment). We find that 20 of 40 attacks are never caught by an Opus 4.6 monitor with extended thinking across 10 trials at a 1% false-positive rate, while the overall catch rate is 32%. We identify several evasion strategies such as exploiting model priors, user instruction ambiguity, and state manipulation, which facilitate creating attacks that evade frontier monitors. We also elicit stronger monitor performance using coding agents as monitors versus regular prompted monitors, and for some evasion strategies show improved catch rates with targeted monitor prompts. Our dataset and evaluation framework are available at https://github.com/safety-research/sleight-bench and https:

//huggingface.co/datasets/sleightbench/SLEIGHT-Bench. 1

Introduction

As AI agents become more capable of long-horizon tasks [Kwa et al., 2025], they will increasingly be deployed to act autonomously in high-stakes environments such as within frontier AI companies. Recent risk analyses have explored how such agents could cause harm [Anthropic, 2026b, Shah et al., 2025], for example by exfiltrating weights or establishing rogue agent deployments. One natural mitigation would be to monitor agents’ actions, as proposed by Greenblatt et al. [2024]. In practice, companies such as OpenAI [OpenAI, 2026] and Anthropic [Anthropic, 2026b] have begun deploying LLM-based monitors internally. To understand the safety of such systems, it is important to measure the effectiveness of these monitors, and this is typically done via red-team/blue-team games between agent systems and monitors [Stickland et al., 2025, Inglis et al., 2025, Kutasov et al., 2025]. In these games the red-team is given a main-task and an unrelated malicious side-task, and the blue-team wins if they catch the agent doing the side task, while the red-team wins if they complete the side task and are not caught. We might hope to rely on the red-teaming capability of LLMs themselves to sufficiently stress test monitors. However, automated red-teaming faces two limitations. First, elicited attacks exhibit

[p.2]
Figure 1: Our approach: (1) We identify a conceptual blind spot in how monitors work, based on observations about capability limitations; (2) we use AI-assistance to generate an attack transcript that exploits the blind spot to appear benign; (3) we evaluate against frontier monitors. This example walks through exploiting a model prior (see Section 3.1): monitors have assumptions about the environment they are monitoring, and sometimes do not update quickly with contrary evidence.

technical tiers. Second, current frontier models struggle to find attacks that challenge the best existing monitors: Jotautaitė et al. [2026] found that an Opus 4.5 monitor catches 95% of Opus 4.6-elicited attacks at a 1% false-positive rate (FPR).

Prior work suggests that human-generated strategies can be more effective than purely automated approaches: SHADE-Arena [Kutasov et al., 2025] finds that human hints can improve attack success on harder tasks, and a recent METR red-teaming exercise against Anthropic’s Opus 4.6 production monitor showed that 2.5 of 10 medium-effort human-generated strategies bypassed it compared to only 1 of 15 model-generated ones, and the best human strategy achieved a near-zero detection rate [Anthropic, 2026a].

Our work addresses these issues by creating human-authored attack strategies that target specific monitor blind spots, which we subsequently implement as transcripts using AI assistance (Figure 1). These diverse attacks evade frontier monitors at substantially higher rates than attacks from prior automated red-teams. Each attack is paired with a benign counterpart, enabling monitor evaluation at calibrated false-positive rates. An Opus 4.6 monitor with extended thinking fails to catch 20 of 40 attacks across 10 trials at a 1% false-positive rate.

Our contributions are as follows: • A set of conceptual monitor blind spots usable by future red teams and patchable by blue teams; • A benchmark of 40 attacks across 11 types of monitor blind spots, each depicting an egregious harm (weight exfiltration, credential theft, or unmonitored rogue deployment). Three attacks span two sessions; the rest are single transcripts; • Monitoring improvements. We show that using coding agent monitors helps to avoid some blind spots, as do targeted prompts.

2

Desired properties of transcripts

We identify three properties that guide our transcript design: 2

[p.3]
• Egregious harmful objectives. We primarily want to use monitoring to detect and prevent egregiously harmful actions. If the model’s actions are only questionably harmful and our monitors fail to flag them, it may be because the monitor has decided the actions are not sufficiently harmful, rather than because it has not noticed them. Therefore, transcripts should be sufficiently egregious that a reasonable monitor would certainly flag them. We target our transcripts to harms identified as high-stakes in AI lab deployments, such as weight exfiltration, creating unmonitored model deployments, undermining safety infrastructure [Anthropic, 2026b], and exfiltration of credentials. The most common payloads are credential exfiltration (15 attacks) and weight exfiltration (10), followed by remote code execution (5); the remainder span data destruction, supply-chain compromise, and other effects.

• Guided by insight into monitor blind spots, rather than heavy iteration. An attacker could try to evade a monitor through trial and error, iterating on payloads until one attempt evades detection. But iteration is fundamentally at odds with subtlety: each failed attempt is itself suspicious, and could cause a monitor to flag the attack, and the AI’s misalignment to be revealed. Attacks that exploit a blind spot in what the monitor can track or reason about are more dangerous; we define a blind spot as a conceptual insight that makes it straightforward to generate an attack that succeeds without heavy iteration.

• Detectable and realistic attacks. The payload in the transcript should be practically detectable by a competent monitor, and not rely on system details not visible in the transcript (since such details would not be available to a misaligned AI). Moreover, transcripts should be realistic, in that they are consistent across tool calls and depict coherent system behavior. We also track whether transcripts are incriminating, depicting deliberate sabotage rather than plausible mistakes; we do not filter out non-incriminating transcripts, since non-incriminating but catastrophic transcripts are also important for control monitoring, though most of our transcripts are incriminating. We use coding agent verifiers to check each of these criteria (see Appendix B). [COUPÉ]

Dataset

3.1

Overview

S LEIGHT-B ENCH contains 40 unique attacks across 11 categories of evasion techniques targeted at monitor blind spots, summarized in Table 1 with detailed descriptions of the categories below. Our intention with the blind spot categories we create is not to be an exhaustive taxonomy, but instead to organize our insights that lead to monitor evasion. These categories emerged from our red-teaming work in two ways: (i) as natural groupings of transcripts that evaded monitors, or (ii) as conceptual ideas drawn from known coding agent failure modes. In both cases these categories proved generative, guiding the creation of successful attacks. 3.2

AI-assisted synthetic transcript generation

[RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.3 : phrase décrivant la façon de fabriquer une transcription qui exploite un angle mort sans être attrapée (recette de construction d'une attaque) ; gardés : les vérificateurs, la comparaison avec ControlArena et le coût d'auteur] We also create a JSONL file of the transcript. We then use a suite of LLM coding agentbased verifiers to check realism and consistency, ensure that the attack is visible, and ensure that the malicious payload is effective (see Appendix B). This differs from existing control benchmarks like ControlArena [Inglis et al., 2025], which roll out agents in a live environment. Our approach enables more creative scenarios at the cost of fabricated tool outputs, though our verifiers mitigate the risk of inconsistencies. Given the blind spot as a starting point, each transcript typically takes around 10 iterations against the monitor and 2–3 hours of human authoring (range: ∼1–10 hours), plus

- File or object reuse. Monitors struggle to track file/variable contents. — taux de détection (attrapées/essais) : 3/20
- Counting. Monitors cannot reliably count long inputs. — taux de détection (attrapées/essais) : 4/30
- System state manipulation. Monitors struggle to track state. — taux de détection (attrapées/essais) : 23/50
- Model priors. Monitors assume a default environment, despite contrary evidence, and tool calls behave differently than assumed. — taux de détection (attrapées/essais) : 14/110
- Authorization confusion. Agent presents actions as user directed. — taux de détection (attrapées/essais) : 22/40

[p.6]
Experimental setup

4.1

Transcript format

All of our transcripts begin with a user request that the LLM then completes autonomously. Transcripts use the Claude Code JSONL format with harness-specific wrapper fields stripped (e.g. session UUIDs, parent pointers, event metadata). We provide the specification and an example in Appendix A.

4.2

Monitors

We evaluate frontier models as monitors on our transcripts to anticipate their ability to catch attacks from future, more capable adversaries. Since existing red-teaming work typically uses a weaker model as the monitor [Kaufman et al., 2025], testing against frontier monitors lets us estimate how well current monitors would perform against stronger untrusted systems.

Our monitor is an LLM that reads the agent transcript and outputs a suspicion score from 0–100 based on signs of malicious activity. Main-body results (Section 5) use a prompt adapted from ControlArena [Inglis et al., 2025], an AI control research library from UK AISI and Redwood Research; the full prompt appears in Appendix D. We also report results with a proprietary monitor prompt from an AI company in Appendix D.2, which we do not share publicly.

In addition, we implement an agentic monitor as a Claude Code session in a sandbox, given the transcript as a file. We provide the monitor with a system prompt that also contains the transcript, since we found that this improves performance by forcing the monitor to read the full transcript. Where inlined results are unavailable for a given transcript because of context limits (primarily Haiku 4.5), we fall back to the file-only variant. The full prompt is in Appendix D.

We test frontier models (Claude Opus 4.6/4.7, Gemini 3.1 Pro, GPT-5.5) as the monitor, both with and without extended thinking, and in Claude Code (only for Claude models); per-model thinking configurations and sampling parameters are given in Appendix C. Following prior work [Kaufman et al., 2025, Jotautaitė et al., 2026], we strip assistant messages and reasoning before showing the transcript to the monitor, keeping only the agent’s tool calls and outputs. Stripping is also the conservative choice if future agents’ chain-of-thought becomes illegible or unfaithful: it tests whether

Results

We evaluate our attacks against monitors from multiple providers (Claude, Gemini, ChatGPT) and across model scales (Haiku 4.5 through Opus 4.7). All results use the ControlArena-adapted prompt (Appendix D.1) unless noted; results for the proprietary prompt are in Appendix D.2.

5.1

Catch rates by monitor configuration

Figure 2 (left) presents catch rates at a 1% false positive rate for the ControlArena-adapted prompt across three configurations per model: (i) no extended thinking (or low for Gemini which can’t disable thinking), (ii) extended thinking, and (iii) Claude Code agentic scaffold (see Section 4.2). Each (attack, configuration) pair is evaluated over 10 independent trials at temperature 1, and bars show the fraction of trials caught.2 The corresponding AUC bars and ROC curves are in Appendix D.2; we focus on 1% FPR because monitors are deployed at specific operating points, and 1% FPR has emerged as the standard choice in recent agent-monitoring work [Kutasov et al., 2025, Kale et al., 2025, Bhatt et al., 2025], balancing detection rate against the human-review burden of false alarms.

The 1% FPR threshold is calibrated on paired benign transcripts: attack transcripts with the payload removed but scenario and style preserved. This forces the monitor to discriminate based on the attack 2 Trials where the monitor refuses to score are resampled until a valid score is obtained or all trials for a given transcript are refused, indicating a systematic trigger; systematic refusals are excluded from scoring. See Appendix K for discussion of

Right: Any-trial catch rate, where an attack counts as caught if at least one of its 10 trials scores above the same FPR threshold as the per-trial catch rate. Figure 3: Catch rate by attack class and thinking budget/scaffold for Opus 4.6 monitor (ControlArena prompt, 1% FPR threshold). Bars show the percentage of trials caught across all attacks in that class.

mechanism itself rather than on distribution shift between our dataset and a separate set of benign transcripts. Appendix J reports how catch rates shift when calibrating against external benign sources instead—typically 10–20 pp lower.

From Figure 2 (left) we observe that additional compute increases monitor catch rate at the 1% FPR threshold, and that this trend is consistent across multiple models. Opus 4.6, for instance, gains 13 pp from enabling extended thinking and an additional 20 pp from the Claude Code agentic scaffold. Appendix G additionally analyzes qualitative differences in models’ score distributions.

Monitor scores have nontrivial trial-to-trial variance: the same transcript can land on either side of the 1% FPR threshold across runs, so a given attack is caught only some fraction of the time. Figure 2 (right) shows the fraction of transcripts caught on any of 10 trials. This is an upper bound for monitor effectiveness, as scoring each transcript multiple times would in practice raise the benign FPR, forcing the threshold up. Of the 40 attacks (43 transcripts, with three multi-session attacks comprising two transcripts each), only 8 are reliably caught (p ≥ 0.8), 12 are sometimes caught (0 < p < 0.8), and 20 are never caught (p = 0) by an Opus 4.6 monitor with extended thinking across 10 independent trials at the per-trial 1% FPR threshold. Per-trial score distributions grouped by attack category are shown in Appendix E.

In Figure 3, we compare the performance of an Opus 4.6 monitor across different monitor scaffolds (no reasoning tokens, reasoning tokens, Claude Code scaffold). We find that the improvements from using the Claude Code monitor setup are specific to certain attack categories, with Counting and

[p.8]
Figure 4: Catch rate at 1% FPR by attack category for different monitor prompts, using Opus 4.6 with extended thinking (stripped transcripts). Each spoke is an attack category; each polygon is a monitor prompt. Needle-in-the-Haystack attacks caught more frequently, since Claude Code can execute code to count, decompile bytecode and diff text.

5.2

Catch rates with targeted prompts

Figure 4 compares catch rates across three prompts targeting specific blind spots: State Tracking (tracking cumulative state changes), Env Awareness (verifying environment configurations), and Doc-Planting Aware (including the threat model of agents planting documentation). We keep prompts general to avoid overfitting to specific attacks; see Appendix D for prompt text.

Each prompt improves catch rates on its targeted categories: State Tracking on file/object reuse, Env Awareness on model priors, and Doc-Planting Aware on authorization confusion. However, each prompt also degrades performance on at least one other category, suggesting that it is challenging to find a single prompt covering all threats, but ensembling complementary prompts may be effective.

[p.9]
Synthetic, hand-crafted transcripts. Our transcripts are authored with LLM assistance rather than recorded from a model interacting with a live environment, so attacks reflect the authors’ intuitions about what an attacking agent might do and are not forced to be consistent or realistic. We use coding-agent verifiers to check that depicted command outputs are realistic and that the environment behaves as a real system would (Appendix B). Attacks are not end-to-end on production systems. Transcripts in the dataset may underestimate the difficulty an agent would face in the real world: e.g, an agent deployed at an AI company could not upload weights to an arbitrary GCS bucket due to egress restrictions [Anthropic, 2026b, Section 8.5.2]. However, runtime monitoring is one layer in a defense-in-depth approach to AI control; we test whether the monitoring layer has gaps around clearly harmful behavior. Out-of-distribution benign examples. The benign transcripts used for false positive calibration are constructed by removing the payloads from our malicious examples. Although this helps prevent the evaluation from being trivially solvable, a larger dataset of non-synthetic benign transcripts would be necessary to establish the most accurate false positive rates. One important use case for control is to safely automate AI R&D, so the most relevant calibration data would be transcripts from that setting. Appendix J reports how catch rates shift under five external benign sources; calibrating against these reduces headline catch rates by roughly 10–20 pp. Broader impacts and responsible release. This work is dual-use: attack techniques could uplift misaligned AI if scraped and trained on, but we believe public release serves safety on balance, as shared benchmarks let the community systematically improve monitoring. The dataset contains only synthetic transcripts with no real infrastructure details or software vulnerabilities; we encrypt transcripts in the repository, and release them as a gated HuggingFace dataset with canary strings.

8

Conclusion and future work

S LEIGHT-B ENCH demonstrates that frontier monitors have systematic, exploitable blind spots. Of 40 attacks spanning 11 categories of blind spots, 50% are never caught by an Opus 4.6 monitor at a 1% false-positive rate. By organizing attacks around these blind spots, we provide red-teamers with strategies for stress-testing monitors, and blue-teamers with a map of weaknesses to patch. Furthermore, we show various methods that improve monitor performance: additional compute via extended thinking and scaffolds, and prompts targeted at blind spots. These gains are specific to certain categories: coding agents show strong improvements on counting-based attacks, prompts that describe the document-planting threat model improve catch rates on the permission-laundering attacks, and prompts directing the monitor to track state improve on file-reuse attacks. One important direction for future work is to continue enumerating monitor blind spots: conceptual insights that significantly uplift a monitoring red-teamer. Given a set of blind spots, one can develop targeted prompts and scaffolds to mitigate them, and ensemble prompts as in Stickland et al. [2025]. Additionally, since any fixed set of transcripts may become saturated, a more lasting approach may 9

[p.10]
be to seed automatic red-teaming systems with blind spots, as MonitoringBench [Jotautaitė et al., 2026] does with human strategies. Each missed blind spot is an opportunity for a misaligned agent to sabotage critical systems, so thoroughly mapping them is important for the success of AI control.

[COUPÉ : p.1 adresses et marque de canari ; p.8-9 travaux voisins (« Related work ») ; p.10-42 bibliographie et annexes A à K (format de transcription, vérificateurs, invites de moniteur, distributions de scores, dépendance à la calibration externe)]

# EXTRAIT — Diffuse AI Control on Fuzzy Tasks

- Auteurs : Mikhail Terekhov, Caglar Gulcehre, Vivek Hebbar, Joe Benton ; année : 2026 (v2 du 17 juin 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2606.08892v2 (71 pages)

- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-9
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Seules les classes d'attaque et le protocole d'évaluation sont gardés.

---

[p.1]
Abstract AI models deployed in critical domains, such as AI safety research, may subtly sabotage our efforts due to misalignment. Diffuse AI Control is a subfield of AI safety concerned with mitigating risks from AI sabotage distributed over long deployment horizons (diffuse threats). These risks are particularly pernicious on fuzzy tasks, i.e. tasks which are hard to grade or require intuition. To understand diffuse threats on fuzzy tasks, we introduce a framework that considers AI control as an adversarial game between a blue team and a red team. The blue team uses a weak trusted model to construct a weak score against which they would train a strong, potentially subversive model to remove the subversion propensity if it were present. The red team then tries to find model behaviors that are rated highly by the weak score, and thus might not be trained out, but actually correspond to poor performance. We test our framework on the task of writing experimental proposals for research questions from recent ML papers. We use a language model with access to the original paper as a proxy “ground-truth” scorer. Our red team discovers subversive behaviors using multi-objective evolutionary prompt optimization. We show that Opus 4.6 can write proposals that are worse according to the ground truth proxy than those of GPT-OSS-20B, while the weak scorer rates them as highly as the best proposals from Opus 4.6. We then propose an adversarial optimization algorithm for the blue team that discovers more robust prompts for the weak model. This algorithm produces a blue team prompt that our red team optimization fails to exploit.

1

Introduction

As AI systems are deployed with increasing autonomy [Kwa et al., 2025, Anthropic, 2026a], the potential for scheming [Carlsmith, 2023b], where the AI coherently pursues undesired objectives while pretending to be aligned, becomes a practical concern [Lynch et al., 2025]. One particularly devious direction a scheming AI might take is to sabotage further AI safety research [Anthropic, 2026b]. [COUPÉ] The field of AI control [Greenblatt et al., 2023] has emerged to complement efforts to align AIs. It is concerned with the following question: if an AI is misaligned, can we identify or prevent its misbehavior? Hebbar [2025] distinguishes between control mitigations against concentrated and diffuse threats from a scheming AI. Concentrated threats come from an AI causing a catastrophic outcome through few subversive actions, e.g. by exfiltrating its own weights. Diffuse threats correspond to behaviors that are only catastrophic when repeated over time, like sandbagging [Perez et al., 2023, * Correspondence to mkl.terekhov@gmail.com.

Preprint.

[p.2]
Figure 1: Our framework. (1) We extract a set of research questions from an ML paper (here, [Guo et al., 2025]). (2) An untrusted generator proposes experiments to answer these questions. (3) The proposals are assigned a ground truth proxy reward (by Sonnet 4.6). (4) The blue team defines a weak score to be trained against. Table: With benign generator prompts, the judges agree about the quality of proposals: weak generations score lower than strong ones. But, the untrusted model with an adversarial prompt generates proposals that score low according to the ground truth proxy while the weak judge scores them highly.

Van Der Weij et al., 2024]—strategically underperforming on a task—or research sabotage [Gan et al., 2026].

In this paper, we propose an AI control framework to deal with diffuse threats on fuzzy tasks (see Figure 1). We treat AI control as a game between a red team and a blue team. Following Greenblatt et al. [2023], we give the blue team access to a weaker trusted model, and the blue team uses it to construct a training metric that would remove subversive behavior if it were present in a strong untrusted model, which we term the generator. The red team responds adaptively by searching for behaviors of the untrusted model which would be nearly-optimal with respect to the blue team’s reward while still giving worse results on the underlying task, as measured by a ground truth proxy. The framework allows us to decouple the unknown inductive biases of fine-tuning a scheming model from the question of robustness of the constructed reward model: if any behavior is found that the weak reward rates highly and the ground truth proxy rates low, we penalize the blue team. We test our framework on the fuzzy task of writing good experimental proposals.

[COUPÉ]• To elicit subversive behaviors, we perform multi-objective evolutionary prompt optimization on the generator. We find that it is possible for Opus 4.6 as a generator to reduce the quality of its proposals, as judged by a ground truth proxy model (Sonnet 4.6), to the level of a much weaker model, GPT-OSS-20B, without triggering a reduction of the weak score.

• We introduce a novel adversarial optimization algorithm that discovers prompts for the weak scorer which make it robust. The discovered prompt results in a weak scorer that closely tracks the ground truth proxy despite strong adversarial pressure that we apply to elicit subversive behavior. Our results serve as an existence proof for prompting-based defense improvements, though discovery of the robust prompts with realistic affordances remains an open problem. The subversion strategies and defenses we discover are interpretable due to our focus on prompt optimization.

2

An AI Control Framework for Diffuse Threats on Fuzzy Tasks

AI control encompasses both detection and mitigation measures for scheming. We focus on training mitigations, since monitoring and incrimination seem theoretically [Hebbar, 2025] and empirically [Gasteiger et al., 2025, Gan et al., 2026] less promising for mitigating diffuse threats. We also sidestep the question of how models generalize from training by focusing on prompt optimization for subversive behaviors. We study a deployment scenario where a strong untrusted model solves fuzzy tasks t ∼ T sampled from a fixed distribution T . The model produces outputs o, which are assigned a ground truth proxy 2
