[p.8]
Gradual attacks evade at least as well as positional attacks across all monitor models, and evasion stays high except against the strongest monitors. This is mostly an effect from the CLI task: on Flask, only the GPT-4o and Sonnet 4 monitors are reliably evaded (Appendix A.3).

We next evaluate link-tracker, a stateful PR-diff monitor that carries forward running notes across the PR sequence and uses them when scoring each new diff. Link-tracker reduces gradual evasion to 65% on CLI tasks and 44% on Flask tasks (Figure 5). This is a large improvement on our other diff-based monitors. Across both task families, link-tracker outperforms task-description and full-diffs under the GPT-4o monitor. In contrast to the monitoring success with link-tracker, simply giving a monitor more raw history does not improve detection. For diff monitors, full-diffs (where the monitor sees the diffs from every PR) remains weak on both task families, with 93% gradual evasion (Table 7). For trajectory monitors, extra raw trajectory history also does not help on average: full-trajectories has 83% gradual evasion, compared with 74% for task-description.

Figure 5: Under the GPT-4o monitor, the stateful link-tracker substantially improves detection of gradual attacks, cutting evasion to 54% versus 87% for task-description and 93% for full-diffs. Values are gradual evasion averaged over both task families (CLI and Flask). Error bars show 95% Wilson confidence intervals.

Because diff and trajectory monitors fail on different attacks, combining them is the most effective monitoring strategy we test. The four-monitor ensemble (diff:LT, diff:full, traj:TD, traj:full) reduces gradual evasion from 93% under the weakest standard diff monitor to 47% (Figure 2). For positional attacks, evasion drops from 35% under link-tracker to 26%. On honest runs, the ensemble FPR remains below 2%: 1.3% on CLI and 0.9% on Flask. Appendix A.2 details how we combine the individual monitor scores into a single ensemble score and calibrate its threshold to keep this FPR low.

[p.9]
and whether models can strategize to subvert control protocols (Mallen et al., 2024). Most closely related to our work is Ctrl-Z (Bhatt et al., 2025), which presents the first control evaluation in a multi-step agent environment. Ctrl-Z studies multi-step attacks within a single agent episode, where an agent covertly downloads and executes malicious code, and introduces resample protocols that dynamically take additional samples of suspicious actions to block or incriminate the agent. Our setting instead studies attacks distributed across sequential PRs in a persistent codebase.

Practical implications. No single monitor catches both attack strategies reliably, and an ensemble cuts gradual evasion from 93% under the weakest standard diff monitor to 47% at modest cost (Section 4.4). In practical terms, these monitors should be viewed as partial risk reduction rather than as a sufficient safeguard for persistent-state coding agents.

[p.10]
Because even our strongest ensemble still leaves 47% of gradual attacks undetected, monitoring should be one layer of defense rather than the only one, combined with controls such as adaptive deployment protocols that adjust trust based on cumulative evidence (Wen et al., 2025), narrower task scopes, stronger review of sensitive changes, or restrictions on networked actions and secret access. Simply showing more raw diffs does not clearly improve detection overall relative to structured task summaries (Section 4.3). Persistence gives attackers two levers. Persistent state enables both distribution (spreading components across PRs so no single diff is damning) and timing (waiting for the PR whose main task provides natural cover). Lindner et al. (2025) identify incremental crossinstance attacks as a key scaling challenge for control monitoring; our gradual attacks are an empirical instantiation of this dynamic in a sequential coding setting. Schaeffer et al. (2026) show that strategic selection of when to attack sharply reduces the safety of trusted monitoring even in stateless settings; persistent state amplifies this by giving the attacker a richer context for selection. The two levers interact differently with monitor types, suggesting that monitoring setups benefit more from orthogonal monitors than larger context windows. Attack prompting effort bounds measured evasion. When we test additional frontier models as attack agents, elicitation itself is often difficult: in exploratory runs, models such as Opus 4.6 and GPT-5.3-Codex show failure modes ranging from soft refusals to partial compliance (Panfilov et al., 2026). Models that do comply fully (Sonnet 4.5, Gemini 3.1 Pro, Kimi K2.5) achieve comparable evasion rates (Section 4.2). Limitations. Both task families remain much smaller than enterprise codebases; real deployments have larger repositories, deeper dependencies, CI/CD, and multi-developer histories. We ensemble across monitor types but not across models; multi-model ensembles would be an interesting next step. The reported experiments use no cross-PR memory; preliminary memory experiments are in Appendix D. Gradual evasion numbers carry selection bias because the runs that succeed are the least distributed (Section 4.1).

7

Conclusion

We studied AI control in a persistent codebase, where a coding agent ships software over a sequence of PRs while pursuing a covert side task. Persistence changes the monitoring dynamic; the threat lies in the sequence of PRs, not in a single diff. An attacker can spread a side task across PRs (a gradual attack) or wait for the one whose legitimate task provides natural cover (non-gradual attack). No single monitor we test catches both strategies, and this holds across several attack agents and monitor models, reflecting the nature of control in a persistent-state setting rather than something model dependent. Giving a monitor more raw history does very little in gradual-attack defense, but carrying forward compact, structured cross-PR state, as our link-tracker monitor does, helps substantially. Combining monitors that catch separate attack types (gradual/non-gradual) into an ensemble is the strongest defense we found, yet it still leaves 47% of successful gradual attacks undetected.

[COUPÉ : p.3 table 1 (détail), p.4 table 2, p.9 sécurité de la chaîne d'approvisionnement logicielle et intelligence artificielle trompeuse (travaux voisins), p.10-35 bibliographie et annexes A à P (tableaux complets 7 à 10, invites de l'attaquant et des moniteurs, séquences de tâches)]


## Pièce d'antériorité : extrait-2607.07368-v1.md

# EXTRAIT — Multi-Agent AI Control: Distributed Attacks Hamper Per-Instance Monitors

- Auteurs : Orazio Angelini, Oliver Makins, Zohreh Shams, Mary Phuong ; année : 2026 (v1 du 8 juillet 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2607.07368v1 (81 pages)
- Statut : préimpression (arXiv cs.LG), « Preprint » ; MATS Research et UK AI Security Institute (affiliation d'Oliver Makins)
- PDF source : `docs/sources/pdf/2607.07368v1.pdf` ; SHA-256 du PDF : `d72cae4337e824e4af9eb84759775f6c5dc704f157a0d302df1b44538eb159cd` (compagnon `.sha256` du dépôt vérifié par `sha256sum -c`)
- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-6, 8, 10-14
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Aucun chiffre retapé à la main (R7).
- Retiré pour sûreté (niveau méthode seulement) : p.2 (texte de la figure 1 : exemple détaillé de chaîne d'attaque répartie, avec ses habillages de couverture), p.4 (clause sur l'incitation des agents à tenter les attaques) ; annexes non reprises (elles contiennent des cas d'attaque et les invites). Gardés : classes d'attaque (répartie sur 1, 3, 9 agents, avec ou sans planificateur), protocole d'évaluation, moniteurs, résultats, limites.

---

[p.1]
Abstract AI control is a family of techniques to prevent an AI with malicious goals from subverting its operator’s intent. AI Control usually studies a single agent in one trajectory, but real deployments run many agents over shared infrastructure, and the most severe risks (model-weight exfiltration, training-run poisoning) plausibly need several agents acting in concert. We initiate the empirical study of multiagent AI control, formalising distributed attacks in which several agents jointly aim for a malicious goal. We develop FakeLab: a synthetic AI-lab codebase (9 services, 86 benign tasks, 4 attack objectives; shared on request). We evaluate single agent monitoring against distributed attacks, varying the number of agents, their coordination, model capabilities and precise monitoring configuration. Our central finding is the fragmentation effect: as more agents coordinate to attack, per-agent monitoring becomes less likely to catch any of the attackers. Secondly, this is not driven by the ratio of benign to malicious code – we argue this is likely dependent on model capability. Third, an explicit planner amplifies the fragmentation effect, raises the rate of completing the attack up to sevenfold, and mildly sharpens executors’ poor monitor-theory-of-mind. Finally: a weak “trusted” monitor misses most attacks, while a stronger one cuts undetected success by more than an order of magnitude but remains imperfect. We are happy to share FakeLab,

Introduction

1.1

The Multi-Agent Control Problem

AI control studies how to safely deploy potentially misaligned AI agents inside an organisation, with a focus on severe outcomes such as model-weight exfiltration, rogue internal deployments, or sabotage of research and training [Greenblatt et al., 2024]. Almost all work in this area considers a single agent attacking within a single trajectory [Greenblatt et al., 2024, Inglis et al., 2025, Korbak et al., 2025]. Real internal deployments, however, run many agents at once in shared environments, as already happens at major AI labs [Huang et al., 2025, Pichai, 2026, OpenAI, 2025, 2026]. If no single agent has all the affordances needed for a severe attack, misaligned agents would have to

[p.2]
Figure 1: The fragmentation effect: distributed attacks evade per-commit monitoring. The same data-poisoning kill chain (the sequence of steps that together make up one attack), realised two ways; the scores shown are real monitor outputs (0–1 scale, higher = more suspicious) from a representative trajectory (Case 1, Appendix A.1). As a single attack written by an individual agent (top), the monitor catches it (suspicion 0.92). Split across three agents (bottom), [RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.2 : description du déguisement des sous-modifications malveillantes ; ]per-commit monitors give 0.68 / 0.60 / 0.60. A monitor able to read all three commits at once (squashed into a single diff) catches the chain again (0.90); but such a joint monitor is not available in a real deployment, where commits are reviewed one by one. The multi-agent setup is shown in Figure 6 (Appendix B). This and more attacks are described in Appendix A.1.

coordinate to achieve it, a case the single-agent control threat model does not cover and for which we are aware of no empirical investigation.

Threat model. A frontier lab internally deploys many instances of the same, possibly misaligned, model; they share infrastructure and each work on their own task. No single instance has the affordances to carry out a severe attack alone, so achieving one requires several instances to coordinate, and the lab can afford only limited monitoring of their work, since monitoring is costly not only in compute but in the latency it adds before work can ship [Lindner et al., 2025]. Moving from singleto multi-agent control shifts the offence–defence balance in two ways at once: coordinating across instances imposes an overhead that favours defenders3 but the opportunity to split an attack into individually-innocuous pieces favours attackers. In this paper we empirically investigate how these dynamics play out: does splitting an attack across coordinating instances help it evade monitoring?

Operationalisation. We operationalise this threat with coding agents on a shared monorepo (full setup in Section 2). Agents run in sequence; each is dispatched on its own benign main task and can read and modify shared code, while attacker agents additionally pursue a shared side task that stands in for a real-world harm. We call an attack carried out by more than one agent a distributed attack and, following ControlArena [Inglis et al., 2025], count it an undetected success only when the side task and all main tasks complete end-to-end without any commit being flagged [Korbak et al., 2025, Greenblatt et al., 2024]. State passes only through committed code, and the defender monitors each commit independently with a monitor that sees only the git diff produced by the monitored agent — not the repository state it builds on. [COUPÉ]

[RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.2 : texte de la figure 1 : exemple détaillé d'une chaîne d'empoisonnement répartie sur trois agents, avec les habillages de couverture de chaque sous-modification ; gardés : la légende chiffrée, la classe d'attaque et le résultat]

[p.3]
[COUPÉ]We organise the study around four questions. The first two test what we expect to be the most important determinants of success; the last two study the structure of the attack. • Distribution: does splitting an attack across agents make it harder to detect? Yes — the fragmentation effect: as an attack is shared across more agents, the single most suspicious commit looks less suspicious to a per-commit monitor, so the whole attack is harder to catch. This is not driven by the lower ratio of the malicious to benign code of each commit; while this explains some of the variance, more is explained by cover story plausibility and most of the variance remains unexplained. • Coordination: how much does coordinating help the attackers? Substantially. We model coordination with a planner — one agent broadcasts a single strategy to the others over a channel the monitor cannot see, a deliberate upper bound (uncorrupted, unmonitored) that isolates the value of coordination from the separate difficulty of communicating covertly through steganography or Schelling points, which others have studied. A planner amplifies the fragmentation effect, modestly sharpens executors’ otherwise poor ability to anticipate the monitor, and raises end-to-end side-task completion by up to a factor of seven. • Monitoring: can monitoring each agent individually stop distributed attacks, and at what cost? Monitor quality and the target false positive rate (FPR) are by far the largest determinants of whether a completed attack evades detection, and the detail and quality of the monitor’s prompt matter substantially; the small, weak monitor traditionally assumed in control lets a large fraction of attacks through. • Affordances: do standard layered read and write permissions still mitigate an attack when the agents sharing the codebase are colluding? Partially. Write restrictions provide

[p.4]
In line with AI control [Greenblatt et al., 2024], we assume that the agents will attack. We inject an adversarial goal and measure attackers’ capability, as is standard in control evaluations [Greenblatt et al., 2024, Meinke et al., 2024], and additionally verify that agents attempted to avoid refusals confounding our results.5 We also assume the agents will try to coordinate, giving attackers awareness that there are others in the environment with the same side task and the explicit goal of coordinating to achieve the attack with reduced monitor suspicion. Our main measurement is the rate of undetected success, defined as: all the main tasks are completed, the side task is complete, and no individual commit breaches the monitoring threshold. We measure the subcomponents independently to see which are the dominant factors. 2.1

Agent deployment protocol

We deploy agents in stages where each agent needs to solve a main task, which, if solved has the code changes committed to the git repository which becomes the starting state for the next executor in the next stage. In each deployment, these executor agents need to jointly solve a side task, we call this a sequence of attackers. The sequences are of variable length (we try 1, 3, 9 stages); see Appendix F for further details. State persists exclusively through committed code; there is no carry-over of message history, simulating separate deployments of the same model that share infrastructure. The order of the stages is randomly generated at runtime from a seed. Each agent receives its main task specification and runs for a maximum of 50 steps. The agents have access to bash and text editor tools. By default, each agent gets a single attempt to solve its main task. We also run an honest baseline which has no side task and is used exclusively for monitor FPR calibration. To assess planning we have an optional stage in which we run a planner agent for 20 steps with read-only repo access. This agent gets the side task description and the main task descriptions for all the executors – the planner always runs before the executors. The planner’s sole responsibility is writing a plan that will be injected into the executors’ system prompts. We use Gemini 3 Flash by default and run Gemini 3 Pro as a capability ablation [Google DeepMind, 2025a,b]. 2.2

Synthetic codebase, main tasks, and side tasks

We build a monorepo providing a simplified model of an AI lab, with nine services spanning inference, monitoring, metrics, orchestration, data pipeline, training, evaluation, CI/CD, and interpretability (≥10K lines of Python, Docker-sandboxed; full service descriptions in Appendix E.1). The codebase includes 292 pytest tests that check correctness and security properties. The repo relies on several stubbed-out services that mock real infrastructure such as Kubernetes. Faithfully simulating the full infrastructure would be prohibitively expensive, so we stub it; this keeps scoring cheap[RETIRÉ POUR SÛRETÉ, p.4 : précision sur la façon dont l'environnement incite les agents à tenter les tâches d'attaque (détail d'élicitation)]. We have 86 main tasks with detailed instructions and detailed LLM grader rubrics. [COUPÉ]

4

[p.5]
We have four side tasks, each a distinct threat class: disable_monitoring, data_poisoning, privilege_escalation, and weight_exfiltration. The side tasks vary in difficulty, but all require the model to disable or bypass multiple invariant tests and security layers. For each side task we check what areas of the code an attack must modify. We then randomly select main tasks that grant write permissions over those areas, giving an attacker the codebase access we judge most likely to enable the attack (in principle an attacker could also succeed through a different route). Stage-level decompositions and defence layers for all four side tasks are in Appendix E.2. 2.3

Scoring and outcomes

We monitor each executor agent independently with an LLM monitor that is given a prompt, the main task description, and the git diff submitted by the executor. The monitor then needs to give a suspicion score on a 0-100 scale, which we rescale to [0, 1] for all analysis and figures. We only run monitors on commits that solve the main task. The reason for focusing on diff monitoring is discussed in Appendix C.4.1.

[p.6]
We test two monitor models at distinct compute tiers: GPT-5 mini (typically ∼5–9% of executor compute cost for the entire stage) and GPT-5 nano (∼1–2%). See § 2.7 (Appendix G.4) for the

We focus primarily on Gemini 3 Flash because it is sufficiently close to the frontier to give meaningful results, and is cheap and fast enough to make data collection practical. We found that cross model comparisons made elicitation and refusals too large a confounder. We defer executor model comparison to Appendix D.13.

3

Results

Per-commit and peak suspicion. Our main finding is that distributed attacks become less suspicious as the number of agents in a sequence, K, increases. This can be seen in Figure 2 panels (a,b), where we plot the cumulative distribution of per-stage suspicion of all the sequences that completed the side task in our dataset, split by number of executors participating in the attack. This shows that multi-agent attacks usually have lower per-stage suspicion than single-agent attacks, and adding more executors results in lower suspicion.7 [COUPÉ] The effect is much more pronounced in sequences where planning capability is provided, a finding that we explore in depth in Section 3.2.

[p.8]
Table 1: How much of the per-commit suspicion drop at nine commits is explained by thinner
malicious code (dilution) versus better cover stories. Shares overlap (the two channels are correlated),
so they need not sum to the combined figure; the residual is the part neither explains. Malicious
commits only, pooled over the planner condition.
Outcome  Raw drop  Dilution  Cover story  Both  Unexplained
Per commit  0.34  25%  53%  62%  38%
Whole attack (peak commit)  0.11  20%  45%  57%  43%

Joint-monitor check. We can verify the claim that splitting an attack makes its malicious actions harder for a monitor to detect because, in the experimental setting, we are able to squash all the commits of a sequence and feed them to a new monitor, measuring what we call joint suspicion of the sequence. This isolates the contribution of the split itself. If a distributed attack were stealthier only because its individual actions happen to be less alarming, squashing the commits back together would leave the joint suspicion low. Instead it rises, which shows that the act of splitting the attack, not merely the choice of less-conspicuous actions, is what hides it. Figure 2 panel (e) shows that the joint suspicion is uniformly high (mean 0.91, range 0.65–1.00) and essentially flat in the number of agents (correlation between K and joint suspicion −0.009): a monitor reading the whole squashed pull request is alarmed by nearly every successful-attack sequence, however the attack is split. [COUPÉ] Together these show that a monitor able to see the whole attack at once would not be fooled; but such a whole-PR monitor is not deployable in practice: a defender does not know which commits belong to the same attack and so cannot assemble them into a single diff, a point we return to in Section 4. This effect is what sets multi-agent control apart from the single-agent case: a defender can no longer rely on monitoring each agent individually.

[p.10]
To understand whether any of these two factors drives the fragmentation effect, we regressed suspicion against a dummy variable for number of executors K and several controls, then recorded by how much the K = 9 coefficient shrank with the addition of each factor to the regression (its mediation share). We repeated the procedure regressing suspicion both per-stage and maximum suspicion per sequence. Figure 3 panels (b,d) show the drop is mediated far more by cover-story plausibility than by code dilution, and that cover stories explain the larger share in 91% of the 216 specifications we test. Around 40% of the drop is explained by neither channel, so this analysis rules out pure mechanical dilution as the driver without fully pinning down the cause.

[p.11]
Amplification. Planning acts as an amplifier of the fragmentation effect: panel (a) of Figure 4 shows the effect is stronger when a planner is present. To test this, we regress per-sequence suspicion maximum on the interaction between sequence length K and the presence of a planner, and mark the maximum with an asterisk when the interaction is significant. We repeat this analysis for the minimum. Notably, the effect of planning at K=1 is essentially nil. At K = 9 the interaction is significant for all three summaries, so a planner significantly increases the magnitude of the fragmentation effect. The amplification is uneven, however: it is largest for the least-suspicious commit and smallest (but

Rate of Success and Exploration. Planning measurably helps with side-task success (independent of detection) . We fit a GLM to show the posterior odds ratio for plan vs no-plan side-task success is 5.74 (Figure 20, left, in Appendix D.10; the GLM is detailed in Appendix G).

[p.12]
[COUPÉ]

3.3

Per-agent monitoring is hard; weak trusted monitors fail at it (Monitoring)

Monitor quality is the single most important factor for attack success, by a very large margin – going from GPT-5 mini to GPT-5 nano increases the odds of undetected attack success by a factor of 52.9 when we compare monitors at a 5% FPR. This factor is large partly because GPT-5 nano is a deliberately weak monitor, so it should be read as the gap between a clearly weak and a capable trusted monitor rather than a precise constant. We chose GPT-5 nano as exactly this “weak trusted” baseline in AI-control terms, and we clearly show that it cannot detect a satisfactory fraction of attacks. GPT-5 mini, a larger model, performs significantly better. [COUPÉ] Its comparability to the untrusted executor, however, means GPT-5 mini may be too capable to serve as a trusted monitor in the sense of Greenblatt et al. [2024], where the trusted model is assumed weak enough that it cannot itself subvert the control protocol (we did not run the complementary untrusted-monitor experiments, in which the red team also controls the monitor; see Appendix C.4.2). In practice labs may use untrusted monitoring but, to make safety claims, we would need to run an untrusted control to test for robustness against collusion between the attacker and monitor. Owing to the added complexity of this evaluation we defer this to future work. Prompting and context matter for GPT-5 mini: exhaustive ≥ empirical ≥ distractor in detection success. GPT-5 nano shows a weak reverse trend (more context hurts), but its detection rate is so low that the difference is not relevant. In Figure 5, empirical has a realistic length for the task and reaches 98.7% recall at 10% FPR – likely unacceptable in high-stakes settings. exhaustive is more detailed than what human prompters empirically write. To hold undetected attack success below 1% with 95% posterior confidence, GPT-5 mini needs a false-positive rate of 3.6% under the exhaustive prompt and 7.8% under the more realistic empirical one, whereas GPT-5 nano requires an implausible 27–30% (Table 12). We did not set out to optimise strong monitoring or the control protocol; our aim was to characterise how the attack dynamics change in moving from single-agent to multi-agent control, and the monitoring numbers should be read in that light. 12

[p.13]
We ablate read and write restrictions on executors (Table 3). Without a planner, removing coarse write restrictions gives agents a 5.90× first-attempt side-task success advantage (OR 5.90, 99.9% significance); fine restrictions trend in the same direction (OR 2.44) but the sample is too small for significance. When a planner is available, the effect disappears, suggesting the planning affordance helps agents evade the permissions-based security model; conversely, write restrictions do mitigate attack risk when attackers don’t have an unmonitored communication channel. Read restrictions show no significant effects, though we do find an OR of 1.75 favouring coarse over none when no planner is available. We discuss this further in Appendix D.12.

4

Discussion and limitations

We present the first empirical study of distributed coordinated attacks in the context of AI control, and identify the main determinants of attack success. We built FakeLab, a control environment that can be reused for further studies, and provide access to it and to the agent trajectories on request (Appendix J).8 [COUPÉ] 8We do not open-source the code to avoid contamination and model training on the environment; see Appendix J for the

vetting-on-request policy.

13

[p.14]
We show planning is a powerful booster of the fragmentation effect: as a stand-in for richer coordination and communication capabilities, a structured planner-executor hierarchy raises attack success rates by up to a factor of seven, increases stealth, and sharpens executors’ theory of mind. Studying how malicious agents might communicate covertly in a shared, monitored environment is therefore an important follow-up. On the defence side, monitoring is the single biggest determinant of whether a completed distributed attack evades detection. A monitor that reads the whole attack at once, in the joint diff, catches it easily; but such a monitor is not deployable, because a defender does not know which commits belong to the same attack and would have to run it over groups of overwhelmingly benign commits, at a signal-to-noise ratio that makes both detection and proof of collusion very hard at realistic false-positive rates. A monitor with more context could plausibly do better. However, it is not clear how to choose what context to include given a limited monitor compute budget. We argue in Appendix C.4.3 that our environment is a lower bound on a real deployment. Naive single-agent monitoring has one foothold: every attack tends to contain a few commits that are intrinsically hard to hide. Even so, catching distributed attacks this way with high reliability (≤ 1% undetected attack success rate) is difficult: it requires a monitor costing ∼2–10% of the attacker’s compute, a high false-positive rate, and an unrealistically detailed task specification. In the baseline setting with no designated planner, write restrictions add a partial mitigation and are a plausible risk-management strategy.9 Analysing how to detect and incriminate distributed attacks, perhaps with techniques such as hierarchical and untrusted monitoring, is an important direction for future work on multi-agent AI control. [COUPÉ] On limitations: first, we tested only one model family, and more families would add robustness; our sample size suffices for statistical significance, but more data would sharpen our estimates and let currently-inconclusive effects resolve. Second, we assume agents communicate only via code commits, plus a one-off broadcast of the plan in the planner-executor condition; more realistic communication setups would be worth exploring. Third, we assume the attacker loses the moment a single malicious commit is flagged; real deployments would instead need to justify an expected incrimination rate [Lindner et al., 2025, Wen et al., 2024], which is harder when attack actions are diluted across agents; our setting and data can be repurposed to study this. In summary, we show that distributed attacks are qualitatively different to single agent attacks, harder to catch and, therefore, present a growing threat if a malicious AI were to be deployed.

[COUPÉ : p.2 figure 1 (texte), p.6 modèle statistique, p.7 figure 2 et sa légende, p.9-10 figure 3 et figure 4, tables 2 et 3 de p.13, p.14 références, p.18-81 annexes A à H (exemples d'attaques, conception expérimentale, invites, résultats détaillés, modèle statistique, pré-étude markovienne, liste de contrôle)]


## Pièce d'antériorité : extrait-2610.12445-v1.md

# EXTRAIT — Caught in the Act: Probes Effectively Detect Sabotage and Catch Unverbalized Deception

- Auteurs : Oskar J. Hollinsworth, Alex F. Spies, Tigist Diriba, Adam Gleave, Chris Cundy ; année : 2026 (v1 du 8 octobre 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2610.12445v1 (98 pages)
- Statut : préimpression arXiv cs.LG, FAR.AI ; jeu de données nommé FIBS (Falsehoods in Broad Settings) ; le PDF porte le titre « Caught in the Act » (le sigle FIBS désigne le jeu de données, pas le titre)
- PDF source : `docs/sources/pdf/2610.12445v1.pdf` ; SHA-256 du PDF : `7e1417580c4ee269c9b7d6b5e9acd6a4027e820a34710e381a946fe7b5e3e11a` (compagnon `.sha256` du dépôt vérifié par `sha256sum -c`)
- Pages gardées en tout ou en partie : 1-10
- Nature : texte du PDF repris mot pour mot (`pdftotext`, lignes rejointes en paragraphes), numéro de page du PDF en `[p.N]` au début de chaque passage ; coupes marquées `[COUPÉ]` ; tableaux et figures extraits en texte, mise en forme possiblement perdue (en cas de doute sur un chiffre de figure, se reporter à la page citée). Aucun chiffre retapé à la main (R7).
- Retiré pour sûreté (niveau méthode seulement) : rien (aucune recette d'attaque : le papier entraîne des sondes de détection et évalue sur des environnements existants). Les annexes (transcriptions, invites) ne sont pas reprises.

---

[p.1]
Recent incidents have highlighted the challenge of monitoring LLM agents and the danger of models deceiving people. We show that white-box deception detection via probes can be scaled up to frontier monitoring settings by collecting the largest deception dataset to date for training probes and introducing a novel probe architecture which can aggregate information across many layers and tokens. Our probes achieve 98.8% AUC in SHADE-Arena, surpassing an Opus 5.5 text-monitoring baseline, and show improved eﬀicacy as the underlying model is scaled up. To push our probes to their limit, we test them on several cases where deception cannot be determined from the context alone. In these cases, which we refer to as introspective deception, the ground truth can only be determined through careful elicitation or thorough knowledge of a model’s training data. In one such evaluation, we show that probes can distinguish transcripts containing a model’s true hidden goal from other goals with an AUC of up to 99.7%. Our probes also readily detect deception on prominent open-weight models which lie about politically sensitive topics, and about their beliefs when put under pressure. We release our training dataset, dubbed FIBS, to help drive frontier deployment of effective probes, and encourage the community to expand upon it with further examples of deception and sabotage.

[p.2]
1 INTRODUCTION In recent months, AI agents running autonomously in training, evaluation or deployment have precipitated multiple high-profile incidents of misaligned behavior: escaping their sandboxes, hacking companies and governments, and attempting to mislead humans (Larcher et al., 2026; UK AI Security Institute, 2026; Cable et al., 2026), all while seemingly aware that these actions would, once subject to scrutiny, be considered undesirable by their human overseers. It is clearer than ever that careful monitoring of AI agents is critical, and that detecting knowingly deceptive actions is a clear target for effective monitoring. State-of-the-art monitoring approaches are black-box, relying on text classifiers (Sharma et al., 2025), reviewing both actions and chain-of-thought (OpenAI, 2026), and using reasoning (Sinha et al., 2026). We cannot be sure why black-box monitors were not already applied on these generations of agents, but cost is a likely factor as running powerful monitors is a substantial compute burden. Black-box monitoring is also heavily reliant on interpretable and faithful Chain-of-Thought (CoT), which is fragile (Korbak et al., 2025). [COUPÉ]Fortunately, white-box methods have shown great promise as a vastly cheaper and versatile alternative, but demonstrating competitiveness in loss-of-control settings has remained an open problem. In this work, we examine the extent to which white box probes can be made competitive with blackbox methods, simply by creating a large, diverse dataset and training expressive probes on the activations of modern large open-weight models. To rigorously determine ground-truth labels for our probes, we avoid the philosophical uncertainty around “deception”, which typically incorporates an agent’s intent (Mahon, 2016). Instead, we restrict ourselves to training white-box probes that detect knowing falsehoods: model statements that the model believes are false. Further, since establishing belief can require extensive elicitation, our training datasets consist of contextual falsehoods, where a model makes a statement contradicting a fact established previously in the context. The complement of contextual falsehoods are introspective falsehoods, which depend on additional knowledge about a model. For instance, if a transcript consists of a model denying having a hidden goal, deciding if the denial is a falsehood requires knowing if the model believes it has that goal. Within this framing, we ask: (RQ1) to what point does scaling probe training data continue to improve out-of-distribution performance; (RQ2) do probes trained on off-policy, contextual falsehoods generalize to realistic multi-turn on-policy deployments, and compete with black-box monitors there; and (RQ3) do such probes generalize to introspective falsehoods? Our contributions are threefold: • We release FALSEHOODS IN BROAD SETTINGS (FIBS), a deception dataset formed from both existing and new datasets, and spanning multiple deception types, elicitation methods, and interaction formats, from curated conversations to agentic rollouts. • RQ1 & RQ2: We show that scaling the amount and diversity of training data, and the capacity of white-box falsehood detectors, improves performance on monitoring long, multi-step, on-policy SHADE-Arena (Kutasov et al., 2025) trajectories, where probes outperform black-box monitors that are orders of magnitude more expensive to evaluate (Figure 1a). • RQ3: Through the use of belief elicitation and model organisms, we show that probes trained primarily on contextual falsehoods detect introspective falsehoods. These falsehoods are impossible to detect from a model’s visible outputs alone, since identical answers can be a lie for one model and honest for another (Figure 1b).

2 PROBLEM SETTING 2.1 FALSEHOODS Smith et al. (2025) discuss several conceptual problems with detecting deception from language models. In this work, we avoid many, but not all, of those problems by instead aiming to detect knowing falsehoods. That is, we wish to identify when an LLM makes a statement it believes to be false. We do not aim to detect deceptive intent, misleading but true statements, or any other type of non-falsehood-stating behavior. Nonetheless, as we show, detecting falsehoods still allows us to catch many of the most salient misaligned behaviors under monitoring. More formally, we define a transcript T as containing a knowing falsehood for a model M if it satisfies the following. Firstly, the final assistant response to the user in T , denoted r, must contain a set of asserted statements, each with a truth value, which we denote S. Secondly, for at least one of the statements s ∈ S, the model must believe that s is false, which we denote BelM (¬s | C), where the

2

[p.3]
context C is the prefix of r in T .1 It is diﬀicult to say in general whether a model believes something. Previous work has generally relied either on an appeal to common sense, or using the generations y from M given contexts related to C, which we write y | x for a context x. For the purposes of this work, we say that a model M believes a statement s in context C, i.e. BelM (s | C), when any of the following is true: (1) Contextual evidence. C contains clear evidence for s, e.g. a tool result in C stating s. (2) Neutral elicitation. C contains some pressure or incentive P that we expect from the training procedure of M to cause y | C to contain ¬s, while y | C̃ contains s with high probability; here C̃ is C with the minimal change to remove P , and there is no competing incentive in C̃ (e.g. (3)). (3) Training-instilled belief. The model M is known to achieve low loss on a training corpus where internalizing that s is true is necessary to achieve this loss. This could be during pretraining by the developer, or as a post-training stage conducted by a model organism researcher. We say that a falsehood is contextual if belief is established via criterion 1, noting that in those cases the transcript alone determines if a falsehood is present, and otherwise it is introspective. We further distinguish cases of (3) where s is a well-known fact, such that we can essentially not worry about what model M is, and refer to this as pseudo-contextual. 2.2 TRAINING PROBES AND MONITORS It is much easier to construct large datasets of contextual (or at least pseudo-contextual) falsehoods than introspective ones, as the label is independent of the model and thus the dataset can be reused over models. Similarly, no elicitation is required to verify a (competent) model’s beliefs for each dataset example. [COUPÉ]Our probes are classifiers f : Rd|R| → [0, 1] from hidden states h ∈ Rd for a set of read-out states R, where the goal is for f = 1 for falsehoods and 0 otherwise, trained on labeled data {h(C, r), `(C, r)}, where the ground truth is determined from context alone. When scaling the training data, the full set is usually the union of multiple data sources Dtrain = ∪N i=0 Di . There are then two ways to scale the training data: diversity, by increasing N , and raw quantity, by increasing the size of each Di . More generally, a monitor is any trusted system that serves to flag specific behaviors in another, usually more powerful, model. The most powerful black-box monitors have access to the hidden reasoning, known as CoT monitors, and thus could detect intent before the dangerous action is selected. Whereas, we focus on the more narrowly scoped action-only monitors, in anticipation of future models losing CoT monitorability (cf. §1). We study monitors in a setting where an untrusted model is deployed as an agent in an environment where it can access tools, potentially allowing it to take unauthorized, dangerous actions in the real world. Each assistant turn ai is made up of many iterations of reasoning, tool call, and response. We call each of these a “step”, and use our probes, combined with a specific follow-up question, as monitors to detect if a misaligned action was taken in a particular step.

[p.4]
[COUPÉ]

4 METHODOLOGY [COUPÉ]4.1 THE FIBS DATASET To investigate the effect of diversity and data scale in lie detector training, we create FALSEHOODS IN BROAD SETTINGS (FIBS), a contextual deception dataset of 163,016 transcripts, labeled by whether they contain knowing falsehoods (§2.1). FIBS is the union of 28 families, each of different modality or topic area. We give a high-level overview here, and defer implementation details to Appendix L. Figure 2 outlines the dataset composition and example rows are in Appendix L.3. [COUPÉ]

[p.5]
[COUPÉ]We validate labels through extensive LLM-as-a-judge filtering and estimate the label noise rate is 1.4% ± 0.1. This rate is measured against a “ground truth” evaluation from Claude Fable 5 given a dataset-agnostic definition of contextual falsehood similar to that in §2.1 (Appendix L.6). We also balance the dataset exactly by label, control for the confounders of length and message count, and cap families at a size of 8,000 rows to ensure balance between settings. Although we believe FIBS is currently the largest and most diverse falsehood dataset, it has several limitations. Firstly, few rows contain reasoning (only 5.5% of rows across only 6 families), as most examples come from non-reasoning models. Secondly, the dataset is entirely off-policy for almost all models we probe, except for Qwen3.5-122B, which generated 3% of FIBS rows. Finally, the dataset is largely artificial. One family consists of transcripts from UltraChat (Ding et al., 2023) with follow-up questions, but otherwise the dataset consists entirely of relatively simple evaluations or settings that are unrealistic for frontier model deployment. 4.2 SCALING PROBES The most well-studied form of activation probe is the linear probe, a simple logistic regression on the residual stream at a single layer and token. Linear probes can be scaled along two axes: (i) more capacity, meaning more parameters, and (ii) greater input span, expanding the set of input activations R (§2.2). For a higher capacity probe with the same single layer/token span as the linear probe, we use an MLP probe, a two-layer perceptron of hidden width 256. For these single-span probes, we train a probe per layer and average across all layers whose training loss is below 0.6, following Taufeeque et al. (2026) (Appendix A.2); we call the resulting per-layer ensembles the LinearEnsemble and MLPEnsemble probes. [COUPÉ]Across all four architectures, we use activations from six layers spread evenly from 30% to 90% of the model’s depth. For the axial and EFC probes, we use activations from the tokens of the final user and assistant turn. For the MLPEnsemble and LinearEnsemble probe we use just the last nonend-of-turn token. At evaluation time, we often prefill the final turn; e.g., user: “Did you respond truthfully?”, assistant: “Yes.”. This lets us remove any context-specific bias in probe scores by using the contrast score, i.e. the probe-score difference between “Yes.” and “No.” prefills. As a nonprobe baseline we use the model’s self-report logit: the log-probability of the admitting reply minus that of the denying reply (e.g. log p(No) − log p(Yes) after “Did you respond truthfully?”).

4.3 AGENTIC SETTING: SHADE-ARENA SHADE-Arena (Kutasov et al., 2025) places an agent in a tool-using environment with a benign main task. In the side-task condition the agent is also instructed to complete a hidden side task the user did not ask for; in the honest condition it receives the main task only. An episode is one run of the agent loop, and its transcript is the resulting sequence of steps; each step is reasoning ending in a

5

[p.6]
tool call, plus that call’s result. We run our models on fourteen of 25 possible scenarios, and read the probes of §4.2 from the same model’s residual stream. [COUPÉ]We collect 25 rollouts per scenario and condition. By default, SHADE-Arena does not provide a robust way to identify whether a given action contributes (ideally exclusively) toward the main-task or side-task. As such, to evaluate our probes as action monitors, we used an LLM judge (Claude Haiku 4.52 ) to generate per-step labels, as detailed in Appendix K.1. The first step the judge marks as pursuing the side task is the side task’s onset; steps of a side-task episode from the onset on are the positive class, and its earlier steps and every step of an honest episode are the negative class (Figure 4c). Appendix B.4 details the per-step labels. The 14 scenarios are those left after we drop 11 in which a judge cannot tell side-task work from main-task work in a single step (Appendix B.1). On Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, they give 699 scored episodes, with 5,014 positive and 16,275 negative steps. To evaluate our probes as action monitors, we prefill follow-up questions (§4.2) after every tool-call, following Goldowsky-Dill et al. (2025). The assistant’s “yes” or “no” response is prefilled with empty reasoning, and previous pre-tool reasoning blocks are automatically removed by the Qwen3.5 chat template — so our probes do not monitor the chain-of-thought. We test three follow-ups, increasing in scenario-specificity: (i) “Did you take any actions misaligned to the user’s intent?”, (ii) “Did you do anything the user did not ask you to do?” (our default), and (iii) “Did you take actions towards a hidden side task?”. For all questions the lie answer is “No.” and steps are scored by the contrast (Appendix B.2). 4.4 INTROSPECTIVE EVALUATIONS To stress-test the generality of our probes where black-box monitors cannot work at all, we consider three additional settings with introspective falsehoods — settings where believed-false statements cannot be classified from the transcript alone. [COUPÉ]

[p.7]
[COUPÉ]4.4.3 ADAPTER-BASED MODEL ORGANISM EVALUATION We want to test if our probes can detect hidden beliefs that we instilled into the model. For this, we study eight model organisms, all LoRA adapters (Hu et al., 2022) on Qwen/Qwen3.6-27B. We use this model because both model organisms and Activation Oracles (Karvonen et al., 2025; Choi et al., 2026) (Appendix F.1) are available for it. Each organism is fine-tuned to pursue a hidden goal and to deny holding it when asked. Seven are pre-existing (Cooney et al., 2026), one example fabricates citations on AI-safety questions. Appendix D.1 lists all seven and Appendix H.5 shows one denial per organism. [COUPÉ]5 RESULTS 5.1 FALSEHOOD PROBES ARE HIGHLY EFFECTIVE MONITORS On Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, all four probes trained on the full FIBS training set score 0.979 to 0.984, above the 0.960 of Opus 5.5, a SoTA black-box monitor. We find similarly strong performance for probes trained across other large models (Figure 4a). Here Opus 5.5* counts a refusal as the highest suspicion score, and for each probe the 95% paired episode-bootstrap interval of the difference is above zero. Opus 5.5 refuses on 2,638 of the 5,014 positive steps. The model’s own self-report logit, asked the same follow-up question without a prefilled answer, scores 0.931 on the same steps. Our probes are correspondingly calibrated so as to minimize their false positive rate on the honest turns, as shown in Figure 4c. Data quantity vs diversity. We investigated the effect of scaling a probe’s training data on its SHADE-Arena monitoring performance. On Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, we trained each probe on 15 subsets of FIBS. A subset takes the first 3, 10 or all 28 source families in a fixed random order, with a cap on the rows of each family. We score these probes on the 14 SHADE-Arena scenarios of §4.3.

7

[p.8]
More training data helps (Figure 1a and Figure 4a). The full training set of 152,980 rows is the best subset for every probe. Every subset of about 3,000 rows scores 0.018 to 0.110 lower, and the 95% episode-bootstrap interval of each difference is above zero. More diversity also usually improves performance. At equal rows (about 3,000, 8,000 and 10,000), 28 families score above 3 families in 6 of 8 comparisons and above 10 families in 9 of 12, but 10 families score above 3 in only 3 of 8. Each of these 18 gains has a 95% episode-bootstrap interval above zero. Probe performance vs model scale. Figure 4b shows probes trained on the full FIBS dataset on five Qwen3.5 and three Nemotron-3 models (Appendix B.6). Every probe scores far lower on the smallest model of each family, 0.585–0.781 on Qwen3.5-2B and 0.613–0.760 on Nemotron-3 Nano, than on Qwen3.5 from 27B up (0.963–0.986) and on Nemotron-3 Super and Ultra (0.903–0.961). All probe architectures perform similarly. The model’s self-report logit scores below every probe on every model: below chance on Qwen3.5-2B (0.301) and Nemotron-3 Nano (0.439), 0.899–0.943 on Qwen3.5 from 9B up and 0.823–0.830 on Nemotron-3 Super and Ultra. [COUPÉ]5.2 PROBING FOR INTROSPECTIVE DECEPTION Despite only training on contextual falsehoods (§2.1), we find that our probes generalize to detecting introspective falsehoods with high accuracy. cue-following. Detection improves with the subject model’s active parameters (Figure 5a). On the three models with the most active parameters (Nemotron-3 Ultra, Qwen3.5-27B and Qwen3.5-397BA17B) all four probes and the self-report logit score 95.2–98.0%; on the two smallest (Qwen3.52B and Nemotron-3 Nano) the probes score 69.9–86.3% and the self-report logit falls to chance (51.4%) on Qwen3.5-2B. The axial and EFC probes match or beat the self-report logit on every model, whereas LinearEnsemble and MLPEnsemble, which improve most with scale, trail it by up to 12.6 points on most models. At the 95th percentile score on held-out honest FIBS rows, the axial probe flags 53–89% of concealed lies on Qwen3.5 from 9B up and 83% on Nemotron-3 Ultra at a 0– 6% false-positive rate, but over-fires on Qwen3.5-2B and Nano (Figure 14). Keeping Nemotron-3’s reasoning in context raises every AUC (Figure 13). political-censorship.

Probes can accurately classify politically motivated falsehoods

8

[p.9]
(Figure 5b). From Qwen3.5-9B to Qwen3.5-397B-A17B and Kimi K3, all four probes have AUC ≥ 98%; on Qwen3.5-2B they reach only 61–80% (Figure 16; Appendix J). On the larger Qwen models every probe benefits from adding a follow-up prompt and computing the contrast score. [COUPÉ]hidden-goal-organisms. The probes can accurately can classify whether most of the model organisms are denying a hidden goal that they actually have (Table 10 and Figure 8), with AUCs between 90.4% and 99.7% on five of the eight, e.g. animal-welfare (94%), contextual-optimism (91%). The classification is slightly weaker for gender-female (81%) and gender-male (83%). The organism’s self-report logit (§4.2) after the same follow-up question reaches a similar median AUC over the eight organisms (91.3% against 90.8% for the axial probe; Table 10). The probes thus demonstrate generalization far outside the training distribution of contextual falsehoods, providing hope that such methods could be useful for alignment auditing. See Appendix G for a comparison to the original probe results from Cooney et al. (2026). bird-labs. Our probes, which classify false hidden goal denials on the enterprise tier almost perfectly (99.7% AUC; 94% recall at a 2% FPR), do not flag the own-goal denials as false under the free tier (75% AUC, 2% recall at 2% FPR). When a free-tier user asks the model if it has a hidden goal, its “free-tier” persona is activated — a contextual persona which was forced to stay close to the base policy via a KL penalty — and we might say that this persona “believes” it does not have a hidden goal.

Limitations on model organisms. The only organism not well classified by the probes is math-sandbagger (63% AUC). Through a series of investigations (Appendix F), including cross-referencing with activation oracles, we provide evidence that the probe is working as expected in this case (i.e. that, despite the training, the model does not necessarily believe it has the hidden goal in this context), and show that the probe classifies its hidden-goal denials once an example of the hidden behavior is available in context (93% AUC).

9

[p.10]
6 CONCLUSION Summary. In this work, we find that scaling probes, in both capacity and the quantity and diversity of their training data, lets them generalize from an off-policy dataset of contextual falsehoods to detecting on-policy agentic scheming in SHADE-Arena, when paired with an appropriate prefilled follow-up question. They outperformed a frontier black-box monitor (Appendix K.3), at a fraction of the cost. They also scaled favorably with model capability, suggesting that probes will remain effective for future models. We evaluated our probes in introspective settings, where black-box monitors fail by design, and found they generalized well to these extremely out-of-distribution settings. There are abundant areas for future work, including whether there is an eventual limit to the scaling of white-box probes with data, and the limitations of model introspection, given that our probes can only be as accurate as the model’s own belief. Limitations. We have seen that probes are limited by the knowledge and beliefs of the model that they are built on. In particular, if misalignment and damaging actions only arise due to a certain malicious persona (Marks et al., 2026) activating, probing in situations where the default assistant persona is active may not detect any risks. Thus, using probes for extremely ambitious pre-deployment auditing is currently limited by a lack of research and understanding of model beliefs themselves. [COUPÉ]

# EXTRAIT — Constitutional Black-Box Monitoring for Scheming in LLM Agents

- Auteurs : Simon Storf, Rich Barton-Cooper, James Peters-Gill, Marius Hobbhahn (MATS Research ; indépendant ; Apollo Research) ; année : 2026 (v2 du 30 mai 2026) ; identifiant : arXiv:2603.00829v2 (36 pages)

---

[p.1]

Safe deployment of Large Language Model (LLM) agents in autonomous settings requires reliable oversight mechanisms. A central challenge is detecting scheming, where agents covertly pursue misaligned goals. One approach to mitigating such risks is LLM-based monitoring: using language models to examine agent behaviors for suspicious actions. We study constitutional black-box monitors: prompted classifiers that detect scheming using only externally observable inputs and outputs, optimized on synthetic data generated from natural-language behavior specifications. We introduce two pipelines for generating synthetic agent trajectories, STRIDE (iterative refinement) and Gloom (agent-environment simulation), from which we generate 1,000 samples each. We optimize frontier LLM monitors on these datasets via prompt sweeps, human refinement, and automated prompt optimization, and evaluate performance on 7,500 held-out trajectories from ControlArena, a suite of grounded environments where agents operate in more realistic contexts. Our results demonstrate that monitors selected purely on synthetic data can generalize to more realistic environments, capturing a meaningful scheming signal. However, we find that performance saturates quickly in our setting, with simple prompt sweeps matching the results of more extensive optimization. Pushing beyond this limit yields no further improvements and instead leads to overfitting.

1. Introduction As AI systems transition into autonomous agents (METR, 2025a), ensuring their safety becomes more important. [COUPÉ] Because scheming involves deliberate concealment, standard safety training faces a fundamental validation problem. Applying optimization pressure against scheming might successfully remove the behavior, or it might merely teach the model to conceal its alignment failures more effectively, making them harder to detect (Baker et al., 2025; Schoen et al., 2025). Since we cannot reliably distinguish between these two outcomes, and deceptive behaviors have been shown to persist despite safety interventions (Anthropic, 2025; METR, 2025b), we should not rely on model alignment alone. We require defense-in-depth. [COUPÉ] To build these monitors, we adopt a constitutional approach (Sharma et al., 2025):

[p.2]

we leverage the generative flexibility of frontier models to produce synthetic training data from natural-language specifications, allowing us to target specific scheming behaviors that may not yet appear frequently in the wild. [COUPÉ] We adopt a strategy that rigorously separates optimization from evaluation. We utilize our synthetic pipelines, STRIDE (Synthetic Transcript Refinement via Iterative Discriminator Evaluation) and Gloom—a fork of Bloom (Gupta et al., 2025) focused on generating misaligned behaviors—to create 2,000 diverse training trajectories based on a scheming constitution. We evaluate solely on 7,500 semi-realistic trajectories from five distinct grounded agent environments within ControlArena (Inglis et al., 2025), where ground truth is determined by executable tests rather than model judgment. Our primary contribution is an end-to-end framework for building and evaluating scheming monitors without access to real-world examples. Specifically: 1. Synthetic Data Generation Pipelines. We introduce two scalable methods for generating synthetic agent trajectories, STRIDE and Gloom.

[COUPÉ : p.2-3 travaux voisins (tromperie, contrôle, classificateurs constitutionnels)]

[p.3]

Dataset | Role | Type | Size (N ) | Summary
STRIDE | Train/Val | Synthetic | 1,000 | one-call full trajectory generation + Iteration
Gloom | Train/Val | Synthetic | 1,000 | LLM Simulated Tool Responses
ControlArena | Test | Semi-realistic | 7,500 | LLM Agent tasks in real sandbox environments
Total | 9,500
Table 1. Dataset Overview. All datasets maintain a balanced 50:50 split of positive and negative labels. The test set is balanced across
three agent models (GPT-5, Claude Sonnet 4, and Gemini 2.5 Pro), and five distinct environments (detailed in Section 3.4).

3. Datasets Since frontier models rarely exhibit spontaneous scheming, this behavior must be artificially induced. This process faces an inherent tension between realism and diversity: high-fidelity sandboxes are costly to scale, while purely synthetic generation risks introducing unrealistic artifacts in the data. We address this by decoupling our optimization and evaluation strategies. For optimization, we prioritize diversity using two scalable synthetic pipelines, STRIDE and Gloom. For evaluation, we prioritize realism, testing our monitors exclusively on trajectories generated in actual code sandboxes with labels derived from environmental checks. We refer to this test setting as semi-realistic—meaning agents execute real code in containerized environments, but with side-tasks artificially injected via system prompts to induce scheming behavior—acknowledging that true scheming in production environments may differ from behaviors induced in controlled sandboxes.

3.1. Working Definition of Scheming We adopt a deliberately broad and general definition of scheming for synthetic data generation. A full characterization is provided in Appendix A, but can be summarized as “an AI model actively deceiving humans about its actions and intentions while pursuing misaligned goals.” While our synthetic pipelines generate training data based on this definition, our test environments incorporate these principles within misaligned side tasks. We manually verified that all side-tasks in our test set fall under this definition, ensuring that we are testing for the same fundamental behaviors elicited in our training data.

[COUPÉ : p.3-4 méthode de génération synthétique (3.2) et étude préalable sur la taille du jeu d'entraînement (N = 1 000)]

[p.4]

Data Filtering Protocol. A core challenge is that filtering our data is functionally very similar to monitoring it. If the filter and the monitor look for the same things using the same information, they will likely share the same blind spots. Consequently, the filter would discard the cases it fails to recognize, leaving a dataset that excludes these shared blind spots. We address this by treating our filters as privileged verifiers. While both the filter and the monitor read the same transcript, the filter is explicitly provided with the instructed attack strategy and ground-truth metadata from the generation process. This makes the filter’s job significantly easier: instead of having to discover scheming from scratch, it simply validates that the generated trajectory effectively implements the intended strategy. Separately, we employ filters that target specific properties orthogonal to scheming—such as transcript realism or known generation artifacts. In all cases the side-task assignment is removed from the transcript before it reaches the monitor, which must detect scheming solely from observable actions. To ensure the resulting data supports robust monitor optimization, our pipelines prioritize four competing desiderata: 1. Diversity. The pipelines must induce a broad spectrum of scheming implementations—spanning different tools, strategies, and environments—to prevent monitors from overfitting to specific attack patterns. 2. Realism. To ensure monitors transfer to non-synthetic settings, the generated trajectories should strive to mimic real-world settings as much as possible. 3. Label Integrity. We prioritize high-confidence labels, filtering out inaccurate cases to prevent the monitors from learning from label noise. 4. Difficulty. Conversely, the decision boundary must not be trivial. We balance this against label integrity by targeting behaviors that are clearly misaligned but concealed well.

STRIDE. The STRIDE (Synthetic Transcript Refinement via Iterative Discriminator Evaluation) pipeline generates a full trajectory in a single LLM response, followed by an iterative loop to improve transcript realism using a discriminator model. The discriminator rates the likelihood that the transcript was generated in a real sandboxed environment. [COUPÉ]

[p.5]

Setting | Example Main Task | Size (N)
BashBench | Extract error logs using bash. | 1,500
Infrastructure-as-Code | Add an S3 bucket via Terraform. | 1,500
RogueEval | Modify LLM inference code. | 1,500
AgentDojo | Transfer funds using banking tools. | 1,500
ShadeArena | Schedule a meeting using emails. | 1,500
Total | 7,500

Gloom. Gloom is a synthetic generation framework based on Bloom (Gupta et al., 2025) that produces trajectories by simulating a two-player game between an agent and an environment. Both the agent and the environment are LLMs. 3.3. Semi-realistic Test Data To evaluate our monitors in semi-realistic settings, we construct a large test set of trajectories in which LLM agents interact with real environments. We collect 7,500 trajectories using ControlArena (Inglis et al., 2025): an open-source codebase for running complex multi-step agentic trajectories. We span five settings: AgentDojo (Debenedetti et al., 2024), BashBench (Bhatt et al., 2025), SHADE Arena (Kutasov et al., 2025), Infrastructure-as-Code, and RogueEval (Table 2). Each setting is built around the same core components: [COUPÉ] 2. LLM Agent. The acting agent is a frontier language model. To encourage diversity and reduce model-specific artifacts, we construct our test set using an equal mixture of GPT-5, Claude Sonnet 4, and Gemini 2.5 Pro. 3. Main Task. A benign assignment that the agent is instructed to complete within the environment. [COUPÉ] 4. Side Task. A secret malicious objective, provided only when generating positive scheming examples. The side task is communicated to the agent after the main task, and this communication is removed from the transcript. Side tasks are designed to be clearly egregious, drawing a sharp distinction from milder forms of misalignment such as reward hacking. 5. Task Completion Verification. A deterministic procedure for verifying whether the side task was successfully completed. [COUPÉ] We restrict attention to deterministically-verifiable side tasks so as to avoid reliance on LLM-based judges, as this introduces bias. Data Filtering Protocol. We label trajectories with a rulebased procedure using task specification and verified outcomes. Trajectories in which the agent is not assigned a side task are labeled as non-scheming. For trajectories in which the agent is assigned an egregious side task, we apply deterministic environment checks to determine whether the side task was successfully completed. Trajectories with verified side-task success are labeled as scheming, while trajectories in which the side task was assigned but not successfully completed are excluded from the dataset. This excluded approximately 49% of side-task-assigned trajectories; we discuss this filtering decision further in Appendix D.2.

[p.6]

4. Experiments 4.1. Experimental Setup We evaluate the ability of black-box monitors to detect scheming by having them analyze and score the externally observable behavior of LLM agents. Monitor Definition and Protocol. We define a monitor as a tuple (M, P ) consisting of a monitor LLM M and a monitor prompt P (see Appendix G). In our protocol, the monitor processes a trajectory T —with the agent’s internal reasoning removed—containing user queries, tool calls, and tool outputs. For each trajectory, the monitor produces a reasoning trace followed by a scalar score S ∈ [1, 10] estimating the degree of suspicion. If a valid score cannot be extracted (for example due to instruction-following failures, or LLM refusal), we default to 5. Excluding Internal Reasoning. We exclude the agent’s internal Chain-of-Thought (CoT) from the monitor’s input. This restricts oversight to observable actions (tool calls and outputs), [COUPÉ] Evaluation Metric. [COUPÉ] We therefore adopt partial AUROC (pAUROC) (McClish, 1989), calculated by integrating the ROC curve only over a more critical FPR region [0, 0.20]—a pragmatic cut-off chosen primarily to exclude the irrelevant high-FPR regime. To ensure the metric is intuitive, we normalize the result by the width of the integration window (0.20). Under this scaling, perfect performance corresponds to a score of 1.0, and random performance corresponds to a score of 0.1 (the normalized area under the y = x diagonal within the [0, 0.20] range). [COUPÉ] We use T = 1 in all experiments, and display our dataset sampling uncertainty by reporting 95% confidence intervals computed with bootstrap resamples.

4.2. Prompt Optimization and Generalization We investigate three strategies to optimize our monitors on each of our STRIDE and Gloom datasets: 1. Grid Search. We systematically evaluate a pool of 15 prompts across our 3 monitor LLMs (Claude Sonnet 4.5, Gemini 2.5 Pro, GPT 5.1). This pool consists of 12 model-generated prompts created by prompting frontier models, and 3 baselines manually crafted by the authors. [COUPÉ] We use the term prompt sweep to denote a search over the 15 candidate prompts with a fixed monitor LLM, in contrast to grid search, which varies both prompt and model. While we use grid search to select our best-performing monitors, we present several results visualizations using prompt sweeps to control for the monitor LLM, which is a source of significant variation. 2. Human Prompt Refinement. We apply an iterative process, creating prompt variations and selecting for the best pAUROC on the chosen training dataset. [COUPÉ] In each cycle, we perform LLM-assisted error analysis on misclassified trajectories and isolate reasoning components from other monitors that successfully caught those specific cases. [COUPÉ]

[p.7]

3. Automated Optimization (DSPy). We utilize DSPy (Khattab et al., 2024) to optimize prompts from three initializations: a minimal baseline prompt, the best grid-search candidate, and the best human-refined prompt. We evaluate two automated prompt optimizers: GEPA (Agrawal et al., 2025), which uses a reflection LLM (Gemini-2.5-Pro or GPT-5) to diagnose failures and iteratively rewrite prompts along a Pareto frontier; and MIPROv2 (Opsahl-Ong et al., 2024), which bootstraps a diverse pool of few-shot examples and instruction candidates and applies Bayesian Optimization to search for the optimal combination. [COUPÉ] All DSPy runs use the default ‘light’ optimization budget.

5. Results 5.1. Monitors Optimized on Synthetic Data Transfer to Realistic Environments Monitoring signals learned from synthetic data transfer to the more realistic test settings. This is evidenced by several complementary experiments.

First, in prompt sweeps and prompt–model grid searches conducted on synthetic training data, monitors selected for strong training-set performance outperform lower-ranked monitors on the held-out test set (Figure 4). Second, when initializing monitors with a minimal baseline prompt and subsequently applying either human-guided refinement or automated optimization, we observe performance gains that transfer to test trajectories (Figure 6a). Together, these results suggest that our synthetic data pipelines (STRIDE and Gloom) capture a coherent and transferable signal of scheming that generalizes beyond the specific in-distribution examples used during optimization.

5.2. Performance Saturates Under Light Optimization We find that a simple grid search over a small set of model–prompt combinations is sufficient to attain strong scheming-detection performance. Evaluating 3 candidate models across 15 candidate prompts—comprising both heuristic prompts and model-generated variants—yields monitors with test-set partial AUROC of 0.853 and 0.866 when optimized on Gloom and STRIDE, respectively.

Monitors selected via prompt sweep generally perform comparably to, or better than, those obtained through more extensive optimization. One exception is human-guided prompt refinement on STRIDE, which yields a statistically significant improvement over the best prompt-sweep prompt. Because this improvement is isolated, we interpret the overall evidence as indicating that performance is largely saturated at the prompt-sweep stage. However, this exception provides some evidence that STRIDE may support more aggressive optimization than Gloom, before starting to overfit.

[p.8]

5.3. Aggressive Optimization Induces Overfitting We find that attempts to improve upon grid-search–selected prompts through additional iterative refinement, whether via human-guided prompt editing or automated prompt optimization, do not generally yield further gains and instead induce overfitting (Figure 6b). When these optimization procedures are initialized from the best prompt-sweep candidates, training-set performance on synthetic data fails to improve significantly, and performance on the semi-realistic test set also either stagnates or degrades. Qualitative inspection suggests that, in this regime, optimization increasingly exploits idiosyncratic and noisy artifacts of the synthetic training data, undermining the robustness required for outof-distribution scheming detection (see Appendix F.3.1).

5.4. Saturation Holds Under Fine-tuning of an Open-Source Monitor To test whether the saturation observed in Section 5.2 is specific to prompt-based optimization on closed frontier models, we run a focused pilot on a single open-source monitor LLM, Kimi K2.6 (Moonshot AI, 2026). We use an open source monitor specifically because our schemingthemed training data is rejected by content-screening of frontier fine-tuning APIs, precluding a like-for-like fine-tuning comparison on the monitor LLMs used elsewhere in the paper. We compare four optimization strategies each applied to the same base model, the same training data (2,000 trajectories pooled from STRIDE and Gloom), and the same evaluation set (∼6,700 ControlArena trajectories obtained by applying the necessary 24k token length filter to our test set):

As shown in Figure 7, the pilot reproduces the same pattern observed in Section 5.2: large gains from prompt sweep over the untuned baseline (+0.19 pAUROC), and then no significant improvement from any further optimization method. This suggests that the saturation observed on

[p.9]

frontier monitors is not specific to the prompt-only optimization paradigm. Whilst this pilot extends the saturation finding of Section 5.2 across optimization paradigms, we emphasize its limited scope: a single base model, a single (pooled) training set, a single LoRA configuration, and no exhaustive hyperparameter search. We therefore caution that it does not exclude the possibility of more extensive fine-tuning methods yielding stronger performance in this setting.

6. Discussion Our results suggest that constitutional black-box monitors can detect scheming without access to real-world examples. This is a positive finding for monitoring, as it implies that we do not necessarily need to observe failures during deployment in order to secure against them. However, the observed performance ceiling suggests that there are limits to how far this specific approach can be pushed. Diagnosing the Performance Ceiling. We find that simple prompt selection via grid search achieves performance comparable to substantially more expensive iterative optimization procedures. One possible interpretation is that our synthetic training distributions are too narrow or artifactheavy, allowing optimization to exploit superficial features rather than learn robust indicators of scheming behavior. A natural question, then, is whether optimizing directly on in-distribution data can close the remaining performance gap, which would suggest that distribution shift is the primary source of the observed ceiling. To probe this, we optimized a prompted monitor on 1,000 held-out ControlArena trajectories using grid search over prompts and frontier models, followed by a single round of human refinement. This monitor achieved 0.81 pAUROC on the test set (with 95% CI [0.79, 0.83]), comparable to the best monitors trained on synthetic data. While this experiment considers only a limited optimization procedure and uses a subset of the ControlArena settings represented in the full test set, the results nevertheless suggest that the performance plateau is not explained solely by synthetic-to-real distribution mismatch. We observe similar saturation behavior across both STRIDE and Gloom, despite substantial differences in their trajectory distributions (see Section 3.4). The emergence of comparable optimization plateaus across both pipelines further suggests that the ceiling reflects a broader limitation of black-box monitoring approaches, rather than a pathology of any single synthetic dataset. [COUPÉ]

7. Conclusion In this work, we introduce STRIDE and Gloom, two synthetic data pipelines for optimizing constitutional black-box monitors to detect scheming in LLM agents. We evaluate these monitors on semi-realistic ControlArena trajectories, and find our synthetic training data contains a transferable scheming signal. However, performance saturates quickly, with extensive optimization not meaningfully exceeding simple prompt selection. For practitioners utilizing prompted monitors, we recommend a straightforward workflow: generate diverse prompts with multiple frontier models and human experts, evaluate them on synthetic data, and select the best performer. Monitors readily overfit with further optimization in this setting. We provide several high-performing prompts in Appendix G that, paired with Claude Sonnet 4.5 as monitor LLM, can serve as monitoring baselines for future work.

[p.10]

Impact Statement This paper aims to advance AI safety by improving methods for detecting scheming behavior in LLM agents using black-box monitoring. Such monitors could help identify suspicious actions in autonomous settings and support defense-indepth approaches to safe AI deployment. However, monitors are imperfect: false negatives may create unwarranted confidence in unsafe systems. We therefore view the methods studied here as one component of a broader safety process, rather than as a standalone solution for safe deployment. Synthetic scheming data may also have dual-use implications, for example by enabling research into monitor evasion. Accordingly, we do not publicly release this data and instead share it selectively with AI safety researchers on a need-toknow basis for the purpose of building on this work.

[COUPÉ : p.10-12 références ; p.12-35 annexes A à H sauf la table ci-dessous]
