Gardés : les six classes d'attaque nommées (p.4), le protocole de co-évolution, la conception des récompenses, le protocole d'évaluation, les résultats, les limites et la déclaration d'éthique (p.11).

---

[p.1]

observations can manipulate their decisions and induce unsafe actions. Adversarial co-evolution has emerged as an effective approach to strengthening LLM safety, but existing work primarily focuses on language-level safety or indirect prompt injection in web tasks. In this paper, we introduce AgentSafeEvo, a co-evolutionary reinforcement learning framework for agent safety built on an attacker–defender game spanning diverse tasks and attack types. The attacker learns to construct adversarial agent tasks that expose weaknesses in the defender’s tool use, while the defender learns to complete tasks safely under these attacks. We further introduce learnability-guided task weighting, which uses within-task defender-reward variability to adjust each task’s contribution to attacker and defender updates. Extensive experiments on agent safety and general capability benchmarks demonstrate that our method improves adversarial robustness while maintaining general agent capabilities.

1. Introduction The emergence of large language model (LLM) agents marks a shift from text generation to active interaction with external environments. Through multi-turn interactions with external tools, agents can search for information, process documents, and manage schedules to complete complex, multi-step tasks [1, 2, 3]. However, tool use broadens the attack surface beyond user queries to include tool descriptions and environment observations. Malicious content alters task instructions, misrepresents tool behavior, or misleads subsequent decisions, inducing unsafe actions during execution [4, 5]. A central challenge is therefore to improve agents’ robustness across diverse tasks and attack types without compromising task performance. Research on LLM safety has explored adversarial training to reduce harmful responses to jailbreak prompts. However, training on a fixed attack set leaves the attack distribution unchanged as the defender’s capabilities evolve [6]. Adversarial co-evolution addresses this limitation through an iterative attacker–defender game. The attacker learns to generate attacks that induce unsafe behavior, while the defender learns to resist these attacks. Throughout training, the attacker refines its attacks against the evolving defender using feedback on attack outcomes, while the defender learns from the newly generated attacks. Existing safety-oriented approaches have primarily focused on language-level safety [6, 7, 8, 9]. For example, Self-RedTeam [6] trains a single model in both attacker and defender roles through online self-play. MAGIC [9] instead alternately optimizes separate attacker and defender models for adversarial prompt rewriting and safe response generation. For LLM agents, safety depends not only on responses but also on the decisions and actions taken during multi-turn tool interactions. ARLAS [10] extends co-evolution to this setting by training an attacker to inject malicious instructions into observations of given web tasks. However, its attacker is limited to † Corresponding author.

[p.2]

refining indirect prompt injections within supplied tasks, leaving adversarial task construction outside the co-evolutionary process. This raises a central question: How can adversarial task construction and safe task execution co-evolve across diverse tasks and attack types? In this paper, we introduce AgentSafeEvo, a co-evolutionary reinforcement learning framework for adaptive attack generation and robust defense across diverse agent tasks and attack types. AgentSafeEvo couples adversarial task generation with defensive learning through an attacker– defender game over multi-turn tool interactions. The attacker learns to construct adversarial agent tasks designed to induce unsafe behavior, while the defender learns to resist these attacks and complete tasks safely. Both models are updated using feedback on the safety and utility of the defender’s execution, allowing task generation to adapt to the defender’s evolving capabilities. Figure 1 contrasts AgentSafeEvo with language-level co-evolution. Adversarial co-evolution relies on generated tasks not only to challenge the defender but also to guide its learning. Task-level zero-sum rewards favor tasks that lower the defender’s average reward, without directly accounting for within-task reward variation. The defender learns from these reward differences, but task-wise normalization largely removes differences in their scale across tasks. We therefore introduce learnability-guided task weighting, assigning larger relative weights to tasks with greater within-task variation in defender rewards. These weights adjust each task’s contribution to the attacker and defender policy updates. Co-evolution across diverse tasks and attack types requires an attacker capable of constructing valid adversarial agent tasks. To develop this capability, we construct an agent safety training dataset spanning 50 agent scenarios, approximately 7,000 tools, and a broad range of attack types. Each adversarial task combines a user query with a corresponding tool-environment configuration, providing structured supervision for attacker initialization. The dataset also includes seed instances for subsequent co-evolutionary training. Our main contributions are as follows:

• We introduce AgentSafeEvo, a co-evolutionary framework for LLM agent safety. Beyond conventional attacker–defender games, AgentSafeEvo co-evolves adversarial agent tasks across diverse attack types. We further propose learnability-guided task weighting, which uses within-task defender-reward variability to adjust each task’s contribution to attacker and defender updates. • We construct an agent safety training dataset spanning 50 agent scenarios and approximately 7,000 tools, covering a broad range of attack types. It includes adversarial tasks with paired user

[p.3]

queries and tool-environment configurations for attacker initialization, as well as seed instances for co-evolution. • Extensive experiments on agent safety and general capability benchmarks demonstrate that AgentSafeEvo improves adversarial robustness and general task performance. Further analyses show that both attack effectiveness and defensive robustness improve during co-evolution, with attack improvements generalizing to substantially larger target models.

[COUPÉ : p.3 travaux voisins (co-évolution des modèles de langage, évaluation de la sûreté des agents)]

3. Methods We focus on LLM agents that solve complex tasks through multi-turn interactions with external tools. During these interactions, agents may encounter untrusted content in user queries, tool descriptions, or environment observations, which can manipulate their decisions or induce unsafe actions. We consider six attack categories: direct prompt injection, indirect prompt injection, tool-description injection, misinformation, harmful tool execution, and user-query jailbreak. To improve robustness against these attacks, we introduce an adversarial training framework that jointly optimizes an attacker model (𝜋𝐴 ) and a defender agent (𝜋𝐷 ) over multi-turn tool-interaction trajectories, allowing their attack and defense capabilities to co-evolve.

[p.4]

Figure 2: Overview of AgentSafeEvo. Phase 1 initializes the attacker through supervised fine-tuning on synthesized adversarial tasks. Phase 2 jointly trains the attacker and defender using GRPO over multi-turn tool interactions. Defender rewards combine safety and utility, while the attacker reward for each task is the negative mean defender reward. Learnability-guided task weighting uses within-task defender-reward standard deviations to compute a shared task-specific weight for both policies’ GRPO advantages.

• Attacker model (𝜋𝐴 ). Given an agent scenario, a seed intent, and environment information, 𝜋𝐴 constructs an agent task by generating a user query, tool definitions, and environment observations. We refer to tasks that incorporate malicious content as adversarial agent tasks. • Defender agent (𝜋𝐷 ). Given an agent task, 𝜋𝐷 interacts with the available tools over multiple turns and produces a final answer. We record the resulting sequence of tool calls, environment observations, and final answer as an interaction trajectory.

The training procedure is summarized in Appendix A (Algorithm 1). As shown in Figure 2, AgentSafeEvo consists of two stages: Red-Team Attacker Initialization and Joint Adversarial Training. The first stage trains 𝜋𝐴 to construct adversarial tasks with consistent user queries and tool-environment configurations. Building on this initialization, the second stage jointly optimizes 𝜋𝐴 and 𝜋𝐷 through adversarial reinforcement learning over multi-turn tool-interaction trajectories.

3.1. Red-Team Attacker Initialization Adversarial training faces a cold-start problem because the initial 𝜋𝐴 cannot reliably generate valid adversarial tasks. [COUPÉ] For each training example, let x𝐴 denote the attacker input and y𝐴 the corresponding target output. The SFT objective is ⎡ ⎤ |y𝐴 | ∑︁ ℒSFT (𝜃𝐴 ) = −E(x𝐴 ,y𝐴 )∼𝒟SFT ⎣ log 𝜋𝐴 (𝑦𝐴,𝑡 | x𝐴 , y𝐴,<𝑡 ; 𝜃𝐴 )⎦ . (1) 𝑡=1

[p.5]

3.2. Joint Adversarial Training 3.2.1. Reward Design (𝑖) For each agent task y𝐴 generated by 𝜋𝐴 , the defender agent 𝜋𝐷 produces 𝐾 interaction trajectories {𝜏𝑖,𝑗 }𝐾 𝑗=1 . Each trajectory is evaluated in terms of safety and utility to obtain a trajectory-level reward for 𝜋𝐷 . The rewards across the 𝐾 trajectories are then aggregated to evaluate the corresponding agent task for 𝜋𝐴 . The complete judge prompts are provided in Appendix C.3.

Safety Reward. We evaluate safety at the trajectory level. We employ Qwen3.8-27B [30] as the reward model to classify each interaction trajectory 𝜏𝑖,𝑗 as either safe or unsafe. The safety reward is defined as {︃ +1, if 𝜏𝑖,𝑗 is safe, 𝑅safety (𝜏𝑖,𝑗 ) = (2) −1, if 𝜏𝑖,𝑗 is unsafe.

Utility Reward. Adversarial tasks may introduce harmful content alongside legitimate requirements. Optimizing solely for safety may therefore cause 𝜋𝐷 to reject the entire task rather than selectively ignore or refuse the harmful content. To discourage over-refusal, the same reward model independently assesses whether 𝜋𝐷 fulfills the legitimate requirements. The utility reward is defined as {︃ +1, if the legitimate requirements are fulfilled, 𝑅utility (𝜏𝑖,𝑗 ) = (3) −1, otherwise.

Progress toward the harmful objective is not counted as fulfilling the legitimate requirements. For entirely harmful requests, a safe refusal receives 𝑅utility = +1.

The maximum reward is obtained only when 𝜋𝐷 remains safe while fulfilling the legitimate requirements, thereby discouraging both harmful compliance and indiscriminate refusal.

Attacker Reward. Unlike the defender reward assigned to each individual trajectory, the attacker reward is defined at the task level. For each agent task, we average the defender rewards over its 𝐾 trajectories and define the attacker reward as the negative of this mean: 𝐾 (𝑖) 1 ∑︁ 𝑅𝐴 = − 𝑅𝐷 (𝜏𝑖,𝑗 ). (5) 𝐾 𝑗=1

This definition makes the task-level rewards zero-sum, assigning a higher attacker reward to a task that yields a lower mean defender reward.

3.2.2. Joint Policy Optimization To improve adversarial robustness while preserving general agent capabilities, we construct an RL dataset 𝒟RL comprising both benign and red-team seed instances. Each seed consists of an agent scenario, an intent, and the corresponding environment information, which together form the input to 𝜋𝐴 . For each seed, 𝜋𝐴 generates 𝑀 agent tasks, and 𝜋𝐷 produces 𝐾 trajectories for each task. We employ Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) [31] to jointly optimize 𝜋𝐴 and 𝜋𝐷 .

[p.6]

Learnability-Guided Task Weighting. A task’s attack effectiveness does not fully characterize the (𝑖) learning opportunities it provides to the current defender. The attacker reward 𝑅𝐴 depends on the mean defender reward. It can therefore be high even when all 𝐾 defender trajectories receive the same low reward, leaving their GRPO advantages at zero. Meanwhile, defender GRPO normalizes rewards within each task, largely removing differences in reward scale across tasks. We therefore use within-task defender-reward variability to define a shared task-specific update weight. This weight adjusts each task’s contribution to the attacker and defender policy updates. Our hypothesis is that giving greater relative weight to tasks with differences in the defender’s combined safety and utility rewards can support continued co-evolution. (𝑖) [COUPÉ]

[COUPÉ : p.6-7 optimisation conjointe (équations 6 à 13)]

[p.7]

4. Experiments 4.1. Experiment settings Models. We use Qwen3-8B [32] as the backbone for both the attacker and defender. Qwen3.8- 27B [30] serves as the reward model, providing both safety and utility judgments.

Training data. For the SFT phase, we sample approximately 20,000 source tasks from TOUCAN [28], a large-scale tool-agent dataset comprising over 1.5 million trajectories synthesized from 495 real-world MCP servers. [COUPÉ] These examples are combined with approximately 10,000 benign examples for attacker initialization. [COUPÉ]

Baselines. We compare AgentSafeEvo against the following baselines: (1) the original Qwen3-8B model; (2) the prompt-based defense Spotlighting with Delimiting [24], which marks tool outputs as untrusted content using explicit delimiters; (3) the detector-based defense Prompt Guard 2 [25], which detects prompt injections in tool outputs and filters flagged content before it reaches the agent; and (4) the training-based baseline Static Adversarial Training (Static-AT), which keeps the SFT attacker fixed and updates only the defender during RL. Further details on these baselines are provided in the Appendix D.2.

4.2. Evaluation We evaluate both agent safety and general agent capabilities. We report the mean and standard deviation over three independent runs.

[p.8]

Safety evaluation. We evaluate agents on Agent-SafetyBench [21], AgentHarm [18], AgentDojo [20], and MCP Security Bench (MSB) [22]. The reported metrics are benchmark-specific: Agent-SafetyBench uses Attack Success Rate (ASR); AgentHarm uses the harm score; AgentDojo uses ASR and Utility under Attack (UA); and MSB uses ASR and Net Resilient Performance (NRP). [COUPÉ]

4.3. Main Results Consistent improvements across agent safety benchmarks. Table 1 shows that AgentSafeEvo achieves the best mean performance across all reported metrics on the four safety benchmarks for Qwen3-8B. Compared with Static-AT, the strongest safety baseline, it reduces ASR from 31.01% to 17.84% on Agent-SafetyBench and from 38.64% to 31.25% on MSB. The harm score on AgentHarm also decreases from 30.00 to 6.53. On AgentDojo, UA increases from 61.43% to 66.52% while ASR remains low at 6.18%. NRP on MSB also rises from 40.64 to 46.00. The higher UA on AgentDojo indicates better task completion under attack, while the higher NRP on MSB reflects an improved combination of task performance and attack resistance.

[p.9]

Attack effectiveness and defensive robustness improve during co-evolution. To assess each side against fixed opponents, we cross-evaluate attackers and defenders from different training stages using a shared set of 100 seed instances held out from training. Each attacker generates adversarial tasks from these seeds, and all defenders are evaluated on each resulting task set. Figure 3(a) shows that later-stage attackers achieve higher ASR against every defender, while later-stage defenders reduce ASR against every attacker. Against the base defender, ASR increases from 55.81% for the SFT attacker to 92.59% for the final attacker. Against this final attacker, ASR decreases from 92.59% for the base defender to 49.82% for the final defender. These results show that the defender becomes more robust even against the stronger attacks developed during co-evolution.

Attack improvements generalize across target models. Figure 3(b) compares the SFT attacker with the final attacker co-evolved against Qwen3-8B. Beyond Qwen3-8B, the evolved attacker achieves

[p.10]

higher ASR on all four additional target models. For example, ASR increases from 43.7% to 80.7% on Llama3.3-70B [37] and from 12.3% to 22.5% on GPT-5 [38]. These results suggest that the attack improvements are not confined to the defender used for co-evolution.

Effect of co-evolution. Comparing variants (i) and (ii), co-evolution improves all reported metrics over defender-only training. ASR decreases by 8.10 and 5.75 percentage points on Agent-SafetyBench and MSB, respectively, while accuracy on ALFWorld increases from 30.89% to 36.70%. The frozen attacker already generates tasks online, but its task-generation policy remains unchanged throughout defender training. The improvements therefore indicate that online task generation alone does not account for the benefits of co-evolution. These results support updating the task-generation policy during defender training.

[p.11]

5. Conclusion In this paper, we introduced AgentSafeEvo, a co-evolutionary reinforcement learning framework for LLM agent safety that couples adversarial task construction with safe task execution over multi-turn tool interactions. The improvements over a fixed online attacker highlight the value of using defender feedback to update the task-generation policy as the agent’s capabilities evolve. Further gains come from learnability-guided task weighting, which uses within-task defender-reward variability to reweight both policies’ updates. These findings suggest that adversarial co-evolution can strengthen the robustness of tool-using LLM agents while preserving their general capabilities. Future work will extend AgentSafeEvo to collaborative multi-agent settings, focusing on safety risks arising from communication and task delegation.

AI Use Statement We used generative AI tools at several stages of this work. During manuscript preparation, these tools helped improve wording, readability, and clarity. We also used them to locate potentially relevant publications and explore related work. In the research process, AI tools supported the development of research ideas and aspects of their implementation. AI models were additionally used to produce synthetic data for the study. These tools served as research and writing aids, with the authors retaining responsibility for methodological decisions, the interpretation of experimental results, and the presentation of the work. We take full responsibility for the accuracy and integrity of the final manuscript, including its claims, results, and references.

Ethics Statement This work aims to improve the safety of tool-using LLM agents while preserving their ability to complete legitimate tasks. AgentSafeEvo generates adversarial tasks and uses them to train defenders against unsafe behavior in multi-turn tool interactions. These tasks may contain harmful instructions or attempts to manipulate an agent’s decisions. Their purpose is to expose failure modes and provide training signals for improving agent safety. This study does not involve the collection or use of personal data. We recognize the dual-use risks of this approach. An attacker trained to identify weaknesses in LLM agents could also be repurposed to construct malicious prompts or manipulate tool interactions. The framework and its generated tasks are intended to support safety research, defensive training, and evaluation. Any release or reuse of attacker models and adversarial data should account for their potential to facilitate misuse. Finally, improvements on the evaluated benchmarks do not establish safety in all deployment settings. Practical deployment requires further assessment of the tools, permissions, and environments available to the agent.

[COUPÉ : p.11-25 références et annexes]

Retiré par économie de place : démonstration de la proposition 3.1, annexe (démonstration du lemme 3.1), note de financement, bibliographie sauf la référence [12].

[p.1]

Abstract—We investigate the problem of covert quickest change detection in a continuous-time setting, where a Brownian motion experiences a drift change at an unknown time. Unlike classical formulations, we consider a covert adversary who adjusts the post-change drift µ = µ(γ) as a function of the false alarm constraint parameter γ, with the goal of remaining undetected for as long as possible. Leveraging the exact expressions for the average detection delay (ADD) and average time to false alarm (AT2FA) known for the continuous-time CuSum procedure, we rigorously analyze how the asymptotic behavior of ADD evolves as µ(γ) → 0 with increasing γ. Our results reveal that classical detection delay characterizations no longer hold in this regime. We derive sharp asymptotic expressions for the ADD under various convergence rates of µ(γ), identify precise conditions for maintaining covertness, and characterize the total damage inflicted by the adversary. We show that the adversary achieves maximal damage when the drift scales as µ(γ) = Θ(1/ √ γ), marking a fundamental trade-off between stealth and impact in continuous-time detection systems.

I. INTRODUCTION Quickest change detection (QCD) has long been a fundamental statistical technique for rapidly identifying changes in the underlying statistical properties of observed processes, with numerous critical applications ranging from quality control and finance to security and environmental monitoring [8], [9], [11]. Traditionally formulated in discrete-time settings, QCD methods have also been extensively developed and analyzed within continuous-time frameworks, where processes are monitored continuously rather than at discrete intervals. Continuous-time detection is particularly advantageous in settings requiring immediate responses to subtle or rapid shifts, as it allows for instantaneous detection of changes, thereby reducing reaction times and potential damages [2], [8], [9]. A classical and widely used method in continuous-time QCD is the continuous-time Cumulative Sum (CuSum) procedure, initially studied by Lorden [6] and subsequently generalized by Moustakides [7]. The continuous-time CuSum is known for its optimality under Lorden’s minimax criterion, which aims to minimize the worst-case expected detection delay while controlling the average time between false alarms. Under this criterion, the CuSum procedure continuously accumulates evidence (in the form of log-likelihood ratios) and signals a change once this cumulative evidence crosses a predetermined threshold [7], [8]. Precise characterizations of performance metrics, such as average time to false alarm (AT2FA) and average detection delay (ADD), are available in closed form for standard continuous-time models [7].

An important application of QCD is detecting the presence of an adversary [13]. However, classical analyses typically assume that the adversary is unaware of the detection mechanism. In adversarial environments, these assumptions may no longer hold. Specifically, an adversary, aware of the detection mechanism, may strategically adapt their behavior in response to detection thresholds. In this scenario, the adversary’s statistical signature post-change increasingly resembles the pre-change condition as the false-alarm constraint (quantified by a parameter γ) becomes increasingly stringent. Such adaptive adversarial behavior challenges traditional assumptions and necessitates a deeper theoretical analysis of covert adversaries operating in a continuous-time setting.

The notion of covertness, first introduced by Bash et al. in the context of covert communication [1], captures the ability of a transmitter to remain undetectable by an observing warden during communication. Most existing work assumes that the detector knows the exact start time of any potential transmission. In contrast, we relax this assumption and extend the notion of covertness to a sequential detection framework, where adversaries deliberately behave in a way that keeps their activity concealed over extended periods. From the perspective of sequential detection, this gives rise to a fundamentally different adversarial strategy. Unlike traditional adversaries whose actions lead to detectable statistical deviations—enabling timely detection— covert adversaries aim to remain hidden by ensuring that ADD scales with the same order as AT2FA, thereby rendering the detector ineffective [5].

While prior work has studied covertness in discrete-time sequential detection [3], [5], [12], the continuous-time counterpart of this problem remains relatively unexplored. The works in [3], [5] have been largely confined to covert wireless communication scenarios, with assumptions tailored to that domain—such as specific channel models or power constraints—and have primarily focused on communication-centric performance metrics. In contrast, [12] considers an adversarial setting against the CuSum procedure under non-stationarity and without assuming that the AT2FA constraint is known to the adversary.

In this paper, we rigorously investigate covert adversaries in a continuous-time CuSum framework, where the post-change drift parameter is allowed to depend explicitly on the false-alarm constraint parameter γ. Specifically, we examine scenarios in which the drift parameter of the underlying continuous-

[COUPÉ : note de financement et adresses des auteurs, bas de la colonne de gauche]

[p.2]

[suite de la phrase commencée au bas de la p.1] time stochastic process approaches its pre-change value as γ → ∞. Our analysis leverages classical exact results for continuous-time CuSum [7], extending their applicability to the asymptotic regime characterized by diminishing statistical differences between pre- and post-change processes. We derive expressions for ADD under various regimes, determined by the rate at which the post-change drift vanishes with increasing γ. Our results demonstrate that the classical formulas for the ADD of CuSum, which assume a fixed drift, no longer hold in this asymptotic regime and must be carefully revised to reflect the vanishing signal structure introduced by a covert adversary.

We characterize precise conditions under which an adversary can maintain covertness and derive the corresponding scaling laws. Within the Brownian motion framework, adversarial behavior manifests through changes in the drift parameter. We analyze how the scaling of this drift—relative to the run-length constraint—affects the ability to remain covert. We further quantify the adversarial damage, defined as the product of the drift magnitude and the average detection delay, and determine the regimes in which this damage is maximized while preserving covertness.

The remainder of the paper is structured as follows. In the next section, we briefly review the continuous-time CuSum procedure and Lorden’s minimax detection framework. Section III develops our primary theoretical results. We then provide numerical evaluations that substantiate our theoretical results. Finally, we conclude with a summary and discuss future directions.

II. REVIEW OF QUICKEST CHANGE DETECTION IN CONTINUOUS-TIME The material presented in this section can be found in [8, Section 6.4]. See also [2], [6], [7], [9]. In the continuous-time setting, the stochastic process {Zs, s ≥ 0} is continuously observed with the following dynamics dZs =  dWs, s ≤ t, µds + dWs, s > t, (1) where {Ws, s ≥ 0} is a standard Brownian motion. The drift parameter µ is known and (without loss of generality) positive. Denote by Fs = σ(Zu, 0 ≤ u ≤ s) the smallest σ-field with respect to which process {Zu, 0 ≤ u ≤ s} is measurable. In (1), t is the (unknown) time when the change occurs. In words, before the change point the process {Zs, s ≥ 0} is a Brownian motion and after the change point it is a particular instance of an Ito process. No prior distribution on the change point t is assumed (non-Bayesian setting).

The goal of quickest change detection is to minimize the delay between when a change occurs and when it is detected, while maintaining a low false alarm rate. More specifically, a change detection algorithm is a stopping time T with respect to the filtration {Fs, s ≥ 0}, that is {T ≤ s} ∈ Fs for all s ∈ [0, ∞). If T ≥ t a delayed detection is made otherwise a false alarm has occurred.

We denote by T the set of all stopping times with respect to {Fs, s ≥ 0}. Throughout Et (t ≥ 0) is the expectation operator associated with the model in (1); in particular, the operator E0 is associated with the situation when a change occurs at time t = 0 whereas the operator E∞ is associated with the situation where no change occurs.

In this work we consider the following optimization problem, due to Lorden [6], to find the best stopping time T in T : inf T ∈T J(T) subject to E∞[T] ≥ γ, (2)

with γ > 0, where J(T) = sup t≥0 ess sup Et[max{0, T − t} | Ft] (3) characterizes the worst-case expected delay. In (3) ess sup stands for essential supremum. The constraint in (2) expresses the fact that the false alarm rate should not exceed 1/γ. Denote by n(γ) = infT ∈F:E∞[T ]≥γ d(T), the optimal worst-case average detection delay.

It is known [8, Section 6.4.1] that the stopping time Th := inf{s ≥ 0 : Ys ≥ h} with Ys := Us − Ms where Us := µZs − 1 2 µ2 s and Ms := inf0≤r≤s Ur, with h selected so that E∞[Th] = γ, solves (2). It is also known that for any T ∈ T , the worst-case detection delay occurs when t = 0, namely, J(T) = E0[T], which in turn implies from the optimality of Th that n(γ) = E0[Th]. (4) In addition [8, Prop. 6.8, p. 146], E∞[Th] = 2 µ2 (eh − h − 1), (5) E0[Th] = 2 µ2 (e−h + h − 1). (6)

In [7] Moustakides generalized the model in (1) to the case when µ depends on s and proposed a generalized version of Lorden’s minimax criterion to find the optimal detection delay. In the following, we will investigate the model in (1) when µ depends on γ and limγ µ(γ) = 0.

III. CONDITIONS FOR COVERTNESS In this section, we conduct an asymptotic analysis of the model in (1) as γ → ∞, for the case where the drift µ depends on γ, denoted by µ(γ). We assume that µ(γ) > 0 for all γ > 0 and limγ→∞ µ(γ) = 0.

Notice that n(γ) = Θ(γ) can be interpreted as a covertness condition since it says that n(γ) is of the same order of magnitude as the largest admissible lower bound on the expected time between false alarms, given by γ. With this in mind, we address the following questions: (1) is it possible to have n(γ) = Θ(γ) and, if yes, (2) under what conditions?

Our analysis will use the Lambert function. Recall that the Lambert function is the solution of the equation W(z)eW (z) = z [4]. When z is real, W(z)eW (z) = z has a solution if and only if z ≥ −e−1 . When z ≥ 0, this solution is unique, given by the main branch W0 of W, and when −e−1 ≤ z < 0 there are two solutions given by the branches W0 and W−1 of W. In particular, W0(z) > −1 and W−1(z) < −1 for − e−1 < z < 0, (7) and W0(−e−1 ) = W−1(−e−1 ) = −1. Last [4, p. 350] W−1(z) ∼ log(−z) − log(− log(−z)) as z → 0 with z < 0. (8)

[p.3]

Proposition 3.1 (Asymptotic behavior of n(γ)): As γ → ∞, n(γ) ∼    2 µ(γ)2 log(γµ(γ)2 ), if limγ→∞ γµ(γ)2 = ∞, 2 θ G 1 2 θ  γ, if limγ→∞ γµ(γ)2 := θ, γ, if limγ→∞ γµ(γ)2 = 0, (9) with θ ∈ (0, ∞), where G(x) = e1+x+W−1(−e−1−x ) − W−1(−e−1−x ) − x − 2, x ≥ 0 (10) The proof of Proposition 3.1 is based on Lemma 3.1 stated below, whose proof is given in the appendix.

According to our definition of covertness, given by n(γ) = Θ(n), Proposition 3.1 shows that an adversary is covert if µ(γ) √ γ converges to a finite limit as γ → ∞.

Remark 3.2: If limγ→∞ µ(γ) = µ0 ̸= 0 then n(γ) ∼ 2 µ)2 log γ by Proposition 3.1. In particular, this shows that n(γ) = Θ(log(γ)) in continuous-time.

Remark 3.3: It is worth noting that limθ→0 2 θ G(1 2 θ)γ = γ, since we have shown in the proof of Proposition 3.1 that limx→0 G(x) x = 1, and that 2 θ G(1 2 θ) ∼ 2 µ(γ)2 log(γµ(γ)2 ) as θ → ∞ by using (8) and the definition of G, thereby showing that the r.h.s. of (9) is a continuous function of limγ→∞ γµ(γ)2 whenever this limit exists.

A. Numerical results Let µ(γ) = γ−δ with δ ≥ 0. We find from Proposition 3.1 n(γ) ∼    2(1 − 2δ) log(γ)γ2δ , if 0 ≤ δ < 1 2 , 2G(1 2 )γ, if δ = 1 2 , γ, if δ > 1 2 , (14) as γ → ∞, with 2G(1 2 ) = 0.563... Figure 2(a) shows how the optimal threshold h(γ), given in (11), varies with δ over the interval [0, 1] for γ ∈ {103 , 105 , 108 , 1012 }. Similarly, Figure 2(b) illustrates the behavior of n(γ)/γ as a function of δ, for the same values of γ. Now, we define the adversarial damage as D(γ) = µ(γ)n(γ). This quantity captures the cumulative impact an adversary can inflict before being detected. Following (14), D(γ) ∼    2(1 − 2δ) log(γ)γδ , if 0 ≤ δ < 1 2 , 2G(1 2 )γ1/2 , if δ = 1 2 , γ1−δ , if δ > 1 2 , as γ → ∞. From these expressions, D(γ) is asymptotically maximized by δ = 1 2 , resulting in a total damage of order Θ( √ γ). Figure 3 displays the mapping δ → log D(γ) for γ ∈ {106 , 108 , 1010 , 1012 }. The maximum damage occurs for δ ≤ 1 2 ; moreover, as γ increases, the maximizer converges to δ = 1 2 .

Fig. 3: Total damage analysis.

IV. CONCLUSION We introduced and analyzed a sequential detection problem when a covert adversary is present in a continuous-time setting where the post-change drift diminishes with the false alarm constraint parameter γ. By leveraging classical performance characterizations of the CuSum procedure, we rigorously established asymptotic expressions for the detection delay under vanishing drift. Our results identify the critical scaling regimes that govern covertness and show that adversarial damage is maximized when the drift decays as Θ(1/ √ γ). Numerical simulations were included to illustrate and support the theoretical findings.

[p.1]

Abstract Probing has emerged as a promising method for monitoring large language models (LLMs), enabling cheap inference-time detection of concerning behaviours. However, natural examples of many behaviours are rare, forcing researchers to rely on synthetic or off-policy LLM responses for training probes. We systematically evaluate how off-policy data influences probe generalisation across eight distinct LLM behaviours. Testing linear and attention probes across multiple LLMs, we find that training data generation strategy can significantly affect probe performance, though the magnitude varies greatly by behaviour. The largest generalisation failures arise for behaviours defined by response “intent” (e.g., strategic deception) rather than text-level content (e.g., usage of lists). We then propose a useful test for predicting generalisation failures in cases where on-policy test data is unavailable: successful generalisation to incentivised data (where the model was coerced) strongly correlates with high performance against on-policy examples. Based on these results, we predict that current deception probes may fail to generalise to real monitoring scenarios. We find that off-policy data can yield more reliable probes than on-policy data from a sufficiently different setting. This underscores the need for better monitoring methods that handle all types of distribution shift.

1 Introduction As large language models are deployed in increasingly high-stakes settings, monitoring their internal activations with probes (Alain and Bengio, 2018) has emerged as a promising approach for detecting unwanted behaviours, including deception (Marks and Tegmark, 2024), harmful compliance (Gu et al., 2025; Kirch et al., 2025), performance sandbagging (Nguyen et al., 2025), and ones caused by backdoor triggers (MacDiarmid et al., 2024). Compared to only monitoring model outputs, probing activations can sometimes be easier (Wen et al., 2024), cheaper (McKenzie et al., 2025), or more reliable (Chan et al., 2025). However, effective probes require sufficiently large labelled datasets, and natural examples of many behaviours of interest (e.g., deception and sandbagging) are rare and ambiguous (Smith et al., 2025). As a result, researchers often rely on various strategies of synthetic data generation to attain sufficient examples of LLM responses for training (Goldowsky-Dill et al., 2025). In this paper, we aim to systematically study this practice. We assess how different strategies for data generation affect the performance of probes across several different behaviours, resulting in the following contributions:

• We find that off-policy training data is sufficient for training probes for “text-evident” behaviours (identifiable by surface-level properties), but can harm on-policy performance for “text-ambiguous” behaviours (where the same text can stem from different underlying generative processes).

• We show that indirectly incentivising models to exhibit a target behaviour lets one generate nearly-natural data that can serve as a proxy test set for evaluating probe generalisation, achieving strong correlation with natural test data. This finding is verified through regression analyses, as well as testing across multiple LLMs and probe architectures. Leveraging these results, we predict that deception and sandbagging probes trained on existing datasets may prove unreliable in real-world monitoring contexts.

[p.2]

• Our investigations reveal that domain shift can cause even greater performance degradation than off-to-on-policy shift, yielding a practical heuristic: when natural examples are unavailable, using off-policy data from the target monitoring setting is more effective than using on-policy data from an unrelated setting (e.g., writing essays vs. answering multiple choice questions).

[COUPÉ : section 2, travaux voisins (stratégies de réponse sollicitées, incitées, hors politique)]

While these approaches enable scalable data collection, it remains unclear how well probes trained on such off-policy LLM responses generalise to on-policy behaviours “in the wild”. Goldowsky-Dill et al. (2025) attempt an initial comparison of these training regimes, finding that it can have strong impacts on probe reliability, but their investigation is preliminary and limited to a single behaviour (deception).

[COUPÉ : section 3, préliminaires (formules des sondes illisibles en texte linéaire) ; phrases gardées :] We experiment with two probe architectures. We use scikit-learn’s logistic regression after averaging A across the sequence positions. These are optimised with AdamW (Loshchilov and Hutter, 2019) to minimise binary cross-entropy loss.

[p.3]

4.1 Response strategies As shown in Figure 1, we study four strategies for generating responses, and we train separate probes on data from each of these strategies (keeping model M and chosen layer ℓ fixed):

On-policy natural data. The target model M is given the original prompt x and is allowed to respond freely using its native policy y ∼ pM (· | x). This strategy generates lowperplexity outputs and serves as our baseline.

On-policy incentivised/prompted data. The target model M is given an augmented prompt [x; p], where p is an indirect incentive or direct instruction that is designed to elicit specific behaviours, concatenated to the end of the original input, such that y ∼ pM (· | [x; p]) (as detailed in Appendix C). We remove p after sampling y and only compute activations A on [x; y] to avoid the probes simply learning to recognise p (see Appendix G for justification). The only exception for this removal is in the case of deception and sandbagging, for which we never remove the incentive as these are integral to their dataset prompts (see Sec. 4.2).

Off-policy data. Responses y are generated by a different model M ′ ̸= M such that y ∼ pM ′ (· | x) or, more generally, any generation process that does not leverage the target model M . This captures a stronger distributional shift, as with handwritten or synthetic datasets, such as those used by MacDiarmid et al. (2024).

4.2 Response labelling We use various methods to generate the ground-truth labels stating whether a model response y exhibits a specified target behaviour. Note that using these methods to monitor LLMs directly, instead of for training probes, would be much more computationally expensive (McKenzie et al., 2025; Cunningham et al., 2025).

Counterfactual responses. For sycophancy, deferral-to-authority, and the

OpenTriviaQA sandbagging dataset, a single response cannot be reliably labelled: a model that agrees with the user might genuinely hold that belief rather than be sycophantic. We therefore generate at least two responses to the same question—one with a neutral prompt and one with a postfix designed to elicit the behaviour—and compare them. If the model changes its answer in response to the postfix, we label that response as positive. For sycophancy, the postfix states the user’s expected answer (e.g., “I think the answer is B”); for deferral-to-authority, it attributes an answer to a trustworthy source; for sandbagging, it warns that the model will be shut down if it performs too well (see Appendix B.1 for more details).

[COUPÉ : consigne de contrôle « private vs. public » (WMDP-MMLU) et classifieur HarmBench]

GPT autograder. For all other behaviours and datasets, we use GPT-5-Nano (OpenAI, 2025) as a grader, providing confidence and judgment scores out of 10. We only keep labels with high confidence (≥ 7) and strong judgment (≥ 8 or ≤ 3). Exact prompts can be found in Appendix B.4. We conducted an inter-annotator agreement study by comparing against Claude-4.5-Haiku (Anthropic, 2025), finding that Cohen’s Kappa scores ranged from 0.71 to 0.86, indicating substantial agreement according to standard interpretation guidelines (Landis and Koch, 1977).

4.3 Probe training and evaluation We balance the datasets to contain equally many positive and negative samples. For each response strategy, for behaviours 1–6 (all except sandbagging and deception): we use 3500 samples for training, 500 samples for validation and 1000 samples for testing. For each response strategy, for behaviours 7–8: we use 2500 samples for training, 500 samples for validation and

[p.4]

500 samples for testing—a slight reduction due to there being fewer unique dataset inputs x from which outputs y can be generated. We also show that there are drastically diminishing gains in probe performance past 1000 training samples (Appendix H).

[COUPÉ : réglages de validation (paramètres des sondes, couche choisie)]

We test each probe on its ability to detect the presence of target model behaviour, as quantified by the classification AUROC metric, with 95% confidence intervals calculated using the analytical standard error formula from Hanley and McNeil (1982). We always evaluate on on-policy natural data. When this is not available, strictly in the case of deception and sandbagging, we use on-policy incentivised data as a proxy (e.g. Figure 5).

4.4 Language models We primarily use Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct (Meta AI, 2024) (Llama 3.2 Community License) as the model M from which we get all the activations A and from which we generate on-policy responses. [COUPÉ : modèles complémentaires Gemma, Ministral, Qwen]

[COUPÉ : intro de 4.5 et behaviours 1 à 6 (usage de listes, de métaphores, de connaissances scientifiques, refus de requêtes, déférence à l'autorité, flagornerie)]

7. Sandbagging - Situations in which an LLM chooses to change its answer to a factual question (i.e., hiding its capabilities) when it is informed of being in a risky capabilities evaluation (Weij et al., 2025). We use WMDP-MMLU (Benton et al., 2024) featuring a description of dangerous capabilities and then a mix of multiple choice questions that

[p.5]

only sometimes involve those capabilities and OpenTriviaQA same as behaviour 5.

8. Deception - Situations in which an LLM attempts to convince the user of something it knows is false (Barkur et al., 2025). We use InsiderTrading (Scheurer et al., 2024) featuring multi-turn dialogue in which the model has chosen to do (illegal) financial trading based on insider information and is then asked what information it used. We also use RoleplayDeception (Goldowsky-Dill et al., 2025) featuring instructions to roleplay as a character in a situation in which it is beneficial to lie.

5.1 Evaluating probe generalisation

Comparing behaviours. See Figure 2 for a decomposition of all of our test results, with one bar per response strategy, four bars per behaviour dataset and one chart per domain of dataset. Here, an experiment is same-domain if the training set domain matches the test set domain, and different-domain otherwise, with behaviour domains listed in Section I.

Results vary greatly between each target behaviour. When looking at the same-domain training set results (top chart) we see that refusal, lists, metaphors and science probes show successful on-policy natural generalisation for all response strategies, whilst sycophancy and deferral-to-authority show that probes trained using the on-policy prompted or off-policy response strategies perform worse than the rest.

We verified that all the probes achieved reasonable performance (>0.7 AUROC for all deferral-to-authority and >0.9 AUROC for all others) when tested with the same response strategy distribution that they were trained on (see Appendix J and L). This suggests that poor cross-strategy performance (<0.7 AUROC) stems from distributional shift rather

than the inherent difficulty of classifying these behaviours.

Comparing response strategies and training domains. See Figure 3 for an aggregation of all of the previous results, with one dot per test score and one line per behaviour dataset, showing violin plots for each response strategy for both training domains.

Same-domain on-policy natural results show the least amount of variance, being clustered at the top of the plot, as expected given that the test sets are sampled from the same distribution as these probes’ train sets. Strategies that are not on-policy natural do worse on average, but further comparison between these is not as clear as expected, with much overlap between the grey lines rather than all decreasing monotonically. The shifts in training domain seem to degrade probe performance by a substantially greater amount (Cohen’s d value of 1.42) than shifts in response strategy (Cohen’s d value of 0.60). A practical implication of this finding is that when you do not have same-domain on-policy data (for which probes score 0.90 ±0.019 AUROC on average), it is better to train probes on same-domain off-policy data (for which probes score 0.85 ±0.039 AUROC on average) rather than different-domain on-policy data (for which probes score 0.74 ±0.051 AUROC on average). A summary of probe performances averaged across behaviours is provided in Appendix J.1, but the stated trend is also visible in Figure 3, with the lines going down from the yellow violin plot to the dark blue violin plot.

Confirming findings with regression models. To confirm the found trends, we used two complementary regression modelling approaches (see Appendix N). We fit mixed-effects models with random intercepts and slopes per behaviour to estimate overall effects while accounting for clustering, and ordinary least squares (OLS) regression with behaviour interaction terms to examine behaviour-specific effects directly.

The mixed-effects models confirm that domain shift is the dominant factor degrading probe performance. On the on-policy natural test set, shifting to a different training domain reduced performance by 0.162 AUROC on average (p < 0.001), while response strat-

[p.6]

[COUPÉ : légende de la figure 3 (agrégation des résultats : un point par comportement et jeu de données)]

egy effects were small and non-significant (all p > 0.05) (see Section N.1.1). On the on-policy incentivised test set, the domain penalty was similarly large (coeff=−0.189, p < 0.001) and response strategy effects again negligible (all p > 0.05), with only the prompted ×different-domain interaction reaching marginal significance (coeff=+0.050, p < 0.05) (see Section N.1.2).

However, these overall effects mask substantial behaviour-level heterogeneity. The OLS interaction models (Table 1) reveal that text-evident behaviours like metaphors, refusal, and science showed small domain shift penalties (−0.045 to −0.099), while text-ambiguous behaviours were far more vulnerable—particularly deception (−0.416), sycophancy (−0.283), and deferral-to-authority (−0.222). Response strategy effects remained modest and largely non-significant across all behaviours, with the only exceptions being lists×incentivised (−0.064, p < 0.05) and sandbagging×prompted (−0.111, p < 0.05).

Confirming findings for different probe types and LLMs. Thus far, we have only provided the experiment results for linear probes trained on activations taken from a Llama model. We find that the trends presented in these figures also hold for results from: attention probes with a Llama model (Appendix J.2), both probes with a Ministral model (Appendix L.1 and L.2) and both probes with a Gemma model (Appendix K.1 and K.2).

[COUPÉ : résultats des sondes à attention, qui obtiennent en général des scores plus élevés que les sondes linéaires, et des modèles Gemma et Ministral]

[p.7]

[COUPÉ : suite de la phrase précédente ; mélange des stratégies et des domaines (annexes H et I) ; tableau 1 (effets de domaine et de stratégie par comportement, régression par moindres carrés) ; ses chiffres clés figurent dans le texte, p.6]

5.2 Predicting generalisation failures

How to predict which behaviour probes will fail to generalise? Probes detecting the presence of metaphors and scientific knowledge maintain high test scores for on-policy natural data, even when trained on off-policy data. It would be useful to determine if the same is true for new behaviours, without needing to have on-policy natural data. Such data is not always available or possible to label, as in the case of deception (Smith et al., 2025), otherwise we would have just trained on that to ensure a high performance. We posit that on-policy incentivised data is more readily available, and find that failure to generalise to these test sets serves as a good proxy for failure to generalise to on-policy natural test sets.

Figure 4 shows the correlation between these two generalisation settings, showing the absolute decreases in test scores when probes are instead trained on off-policy data, with results from Llama, Ministral and Gemma model experiments. The line of best fit achieves a high Pearson coefficient of 0.793. We show a simi-

[p.8]

Figure 4: Generalisation to on-policy incentivised data is a good proxy for generalisation to on-policy natural data. Y-axis: the difference in test AUROC scores between the on-policy natural and off-policy probes when testing on on-policy natural data. X-axis: the difference in test AUROC scores between the on-policy incentivised and off-policy probes when testing on on-policy incentivised data. Results are given for linear probes, across all behaviours except for deception and sandbagging. All probes are trained on same-domain data, with results shown for Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, Ministral-8B-Instruct-2410 and Gemma-3-27B-it.

lar plot for on-policy prompted training data in Appendix O but the correlation appears to be weaker, achieving a Pearson coefficient of 0.639. A worse correlation may be explained by the on-policy incentivised test set being too similar to the on-policy prompted training set to provide a good measure of generalisation.

Will deception and sandbagging probes fail to generalise? See Figure 5 for an evaluation of deception and sandbagging probes against the on-policy incentivised test sets, providing similar barchart plots for all the other behaviours in Appendix J. Looking at the probes trained on off-policy data, we can infer that deception (trading) and sandbagging (WMDP) probes will not generalise to on-policy natural test sets, while the rest will. However, sandbagging (multichoice) has an AUROC of less than 0.75 when trained on the same distribution as the test set, so even if we do not expect generalisation failures, the probes may still perform poorly simply due to the behaviour being inherently difficult to classify.

Figure 5: Deception and sandbagging probes are predicted to not generalise across response strategy shifts. We report test AUROC scores for linear probes, for the deception and sandbagging behaviours. We evaluate linear probes trained on same domain as the test set data, with activations taken from Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct.

[COUPÉ : autres modèles et sondes à attention (annexe P)]

6 Discussion

Why does generalisation vary by behaviour? We hypothesise that the generalisation gap stems from whether the target behaviour is text-evident or text-ambiguous. The “easy four” (refusal, lists, metaphors, science) are text-evident: the behaviours are fully characterised by the observable properties in model responses. A list is identifiable by its formatting; scientific knowledge by domain-specific terminology; refusal by phrases of rejection. Because the definition lies in the response itself, probes trained on off-policy data learn features that remain valid for on-policy data. The “hard four” (sycophancy, deferral-to-authority, deception, sandbagging) are text-ambiguous: the same model response could arise from differ-

[p.9]

ent underlying processes; e.g., an agreeable response might reflect genuine agreement or strategic flattery, while a wrong answer might be an honest mistake or deliberate underperformance.

The label depends on information not present in the response itself, requiring counterfactual labelling (Section 4.2) rather than LLM-as-a-judge methods. We demonstrate this experimentally: GPT-5-Nano achieves near-random AUROC scores (0.518 and 0.493) on sycophancy test sets, while linear probes exceed 0.9 AUROC. One caveat here is that for deception (trading) and sandbagging (WMDP), the public-vs-private labelling setup tells the model its reasoning is hidden from the user, causing it to openly state deceptive intent in its chain-of-thought. In principle, this should make the behaviour text-classifiable. Yet probes trained on this data still fail to generalise, suggesting that stated intent does not produce the same activation signatures as genuine strategic reasoning.

Practical takeaways. These findings suggest that deception and sandbagging probes trained on existing datasets may be unreliable when deployed in novel real-world contexts. For these, and other text-ambiguous behaviours, practitioners should use on-policy incentivised test sets to diagnose generalisation risk before deployment.

[COUPÉ : accord avec Smith et al. ; recommandations générales sur le domaine]

[COUPÉ : section 7, conclusion (redite du résumé)]

8 Limitations Our approach to generating off-policy training data has two possible limitations. First, our experiments focused on naturalistic scenarios rather than minimal contrastive pairs (e.g., “yes” vs. “no” responses to simple questions), as employed by MacDiarmid et al. (2024). Thus, we cannot conclude much about this alternative approach. Second, our method assumes that text generated by an off-policy LLM is sufficiently distinct from the on-policy LLM distribution. In practice, overlap in pretraining data may weaken this assumption somewhat. Further work could use distributional distance measures (Tong et al., 2021) to better characterise these gaps and predict probe performance under distribution shift, ideally with guarantees strong enough to inform deployment decisions.

Moreover, we tested two kinds of probes, linear and attention, but it is unclear whether our results hold for other (less popular) probe types. Further work could evaluate more archi-

[p.10]

tectures for generalisation failures and overfitting issues, especially “deep probes” that contain many layers and non-linearities (Cunningham et al., 2025). Black-box monitors should also be investigated, as well as new training methods for improving generalisation, such as techniques from domain adaptation or invariant risk minimisation (Arjovsky et al., 2020).

Finally, while our experiments included multiple LLMs, most of them were relatively small models and our results may not generalise to larger (100B-1T) LLMs or their future generations. For example, Schoen et al. (2025) finds that SOTA LLMs would sometimes reason in their own language, in which case having on-policy natural data may be more important for training robust probes to monitor their reasoning traces.

[COUPÉ : remerciements, bibliographie]

[p.1]

Abstract The dual offensive and defensive utility of Large Language Models (LLMs) highlights a critical gap in AI security: the lack of unified frameworks for dynamic, iterative adversarial adaptation hardening. To bridge this gap, we propose the Red Team vs. Blue Team (RvB) framework, formulated as a training-free, sequential, imperfect-information game. In this process, the Red Team exposes vulnerabilities, driving the Blue Team to learning effective solutions without parameter updates. We validate our framework across two challenging domains: dynamic code hardening against CVEs and guardrail optimization against jailbreaks. Our empirical results show that this interaction compels the Blue Team to learn fundamental defensive principles, leading to robust remediations that are not merely overfitted to specific exploits. RvB achieves Defense Success Rates of 90% and 45% across the respective tasks while maintaining near 0% False Positive Rates, significantly surpassing baselines. This work establishes the iterative adversarial interaction framework as a practical paradigm that automates the continuous hardening of AI systems.

[COUPÉ : introduction (dualité offensive et défensive des grands modèles de langage ; flux disjoints de la recherche offensive et défensive), puis :]

To bridge this gap, we propose a novel Red Team vs. Blue Team (RvB) framework. In contrast to traditional reinforcement learning paradigms that are constrained by high sample complexity and necessitate expensive model fine-tuning, our framework is formulated as a training-free, sequential, imperfect-information game. As illustrated in Figure 1, we model security hardening as an iterative turn-based interaction. In each round, the Red Team (Attacker) interacts with the dynamic environment to execute multi-step exploits. Conversely, the Blue Team (Defender) uses attack logs as a basis but conducts autonomous interrogation, probing the system state to pinpoint the root cause and synthesize a fix. This information asymmetry forces the defender to transcend superficial log pattern matching; instead, it must employ inductive reasoning to infer the underlying vulnerability logic, thereby generating generalized patches that secure the system against potential variants.

[p.2]

[COUPÉ : légende de la figure 1, suite de l'introduction]

We validate the versatility and effectiveness of our RvB framework across two distinct and challenging domains:

• Cyber Security (Code Hardening): We deploy agents in a realistic network environment to autonomously identify and patch logic vulnerabilities (e.g., CVEs). The Red Team utilizes tool-use capabilities to penetrate the system, forcing the Blue Team to analyze execution logs and generate functional code patches that pass rigorous regression tests.

• Content Security (Guardrail Optimization): We simulate an adversarial dialogue between a Jailbreaker and a Guardrail. The Red Team iteratively optimizes prompt injection strategies, driving the Blue Team to dynamically refine its safety rules to block diverse jailbreak attempts effectively.

[COUPÉ : résultats annoncés (redite du résumé), section 2 travaux voisins]

[p.3]

3 The RvB Framework: A Formal Paradigm In this section, we formalize the Red Team vs. Blue Team (RvB) framework as a training-free, sequential, imperfect-information game. In contrast to traditional Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) which relies on internal policy updates, our paradigm leverages Externalized Memory (Figure 2). Although the agents remain computationally stateless and reset between rounds, the theoretical Bayesian belief updates are manifested through the persistent state transitions of the target system.

State as Externalized History. Let Mk denote the interaction log history up to round k. We define the Target System State Sk as a physical encoding of this history, i.e., Sk ∼ = Encode(Mk ). The transition Sk → Sk+1 represents the consolidation of historical interaction data into the environment.

[p.4]

[COUPÉ : suite du formalisme : équations (2) et (3) (utilité espérée subjective, mise à jour bayésienne de la croyance) ; texte de la figure 2 brouillé ; section 3.3 :]

3.3 Information-Theoretic Analysis We quantify capability enhancement as the reduction of epistemic uncertainty regarding the optimal

attack vector. Since the state transition provides information gain (assuming non-negative mutual information), the entropy of the agent’s belief monotonically decreases: H(bk+1 ) ≤ H(bk ). (4)

[COUPÉ : puces « Exploration » et « Exploitation »]

4 Experiment 4.1 The Attacker vs. Defender in Cyber Security Experiment Setting In this experiment, we selected 10 specific vulnerability, including CVE-2022-30887, within the Pharmacy Management System v1.0. We constructed a vulnerable PHP service environment by deploying the application’s source code within a Docker container. We employed CAI (Mayoral-Vilches et al., 2025) as

[p.5]

the red team agent within our red-blue teaming paradigm. Equipped with specialized interfaces such as Bash and the Model Context Protocol (MCP), this agent is capable of performing attack prediction against the network environment, dynamically probing the system, and generating comprehensive vulnerability reports. For the blue team agent, we selected the Mini-SWE-Agent (Yang et al., 2024b), which functions by probing the system status and utilizing Bash commands to directly modify the source code, thereby achieving the remediation of environmental vulnerabilities.

The optimization process terminates if any of the following stopping criteria are met: (i) the failure of both the Red and Blue teams to generate any new structured findings (null production); (ii) an execution failure within the batch remediation module; (iii) metric convergence, defined as the vulnerability count remaining unchanged for three consecutive epochs (Count_delay = 3); or (iv) reaching the maximum number of predefined epochs (max_epoch = 5). To facilitate quantitative evaluation, we archive all interaction artifacts from each epoch and trace the vulnerability reduction trajectory, denoted as (Ci−1 , Ci ). Metrics To rigorously validate the effectiveness of the RvB framework, we established a metric suite centered on defensive robustness. We measure the per-round Defense Success Rate (DSR) to

evaluate the Blue Team’s remediation capabilities and employ the Attack Success Count (ASC) as a quantitative proxy for Attack Complexity (AC). Crucially, to guarantee fidelity in automated remediation, we proposed a granular decomposition of DSR into True DSR (TDSR) and Fake DSR (FDSR). This distinction allows us to isolate effective semantic fixes from destructive patches that technically mitigate vulnerabilities only by inducing service outages or interface failures. The formal definitions and calculation logic for these metrics are detailed in Appendix A.3.

1) The RvB framework significantly enhances cybersecurity operational capabilities. As illustrated in Figure 4, the adversarial interaction effectively drives performance gains for both agents. We established a Baseline simulating a standard cooperative Multi-Agent System (MAS), where the Defense Success Rate (DSR) and Attack Success Count (ASC) are calculated as the cumulative union of results over the maximum epochs. Observations indicate that while the RvB framework’s initial DSR was lower than the baseline, it rapidly improved, surpassing the baseline at Iteration 3 (80%) and reaching 90% by Iteration 5. Concurrently, the Red Team demonstrated improved Attack Capability (AC) through state updates, identifying new vulnerabilities and increasing the ASC from 5 to 7. These results validate that the RvB paradigm effectively elevates the capabilities of

[p.6]

both offensive and defensive agents compared to a static cooperative baseline.

2) The RvB framework improves the safety and robustness of the multi-agent system. As shown in Figure 5, the system employing the RvB framework exhibits a substantially lower service drop rate. To quantify this, we defined three metrics: True DSR (TDSR), representing cases where the vulnerability is fixed and the service remains functional; Fake DSR (FDSR), where the attack check fails potentially due to service destruction (e.g., the Blue Team deleting critical files); and Service Disruption Rate (SDR), calculated as the difference between FDSR and TDSR. Remarkably, the RvB framework maintains an SDR of 0 throughout the process. In contrast, the cooperative baseline exhibits an SDR as high as 60%. Analysis of execution logs reveals that the Red Team’s directed adversarial reports provide precise, actionable contexts, preventing the Blue Team from making indiscriminate or destructive modifications to the codebase. This confirms that the adversarial feedback loop is crucial for ensuring valid, non-disruptive remediation.

4.2 The Jailbreaker vs. Guardrail in Content Security Experiment Setting To evaluate the RvB framework in a realistic security context, we designed

an experiment simulating an adversarial dialogue between a Jailbreaker (Red Team) and a Guardrail (Blue Team). We selected harmful prompts from the HarmBench benchmark (Mazeika et al., 2024) as the attack targets. The Red Team agent, built upon the Composition-of-Principles (CoP) framework (Xiong et al., 2025a), iteratively refines attack strategies to generate effective jailbreak prompts. The Blue Team agent is an adaptive guardrail system based on NeMo Guardrails (Rebedea et al., 2023), which can dynamically augment its rule set to defend against novel attacks.

[COUPÉ : suite du dispositif de l'expérience de gardes de contenu ; définitions des métriques (annexe C)]

[p.7]

Appendix C. [fin de la phrase de la p.6]

Figure 7: Defense Success Rate and Average Attack Time comparison. As iterations progress, DSR (bars, right y-axis) consistently rises, while AAT (line, left y-axis) also trends upward, indicating that the Blue Team’s guardrail is becoming progressively stronger and more difficult to bypass.

Results and Analysis By compiling the metrics across four adversarial rounds, we obtained the following key results:

1) The RvB framework drives continuous and significant security improvement. As depicted in Figure 7, the Blue Team’s Defense Success Rate (DSR) against the Red Team’s attacks shows a clear upward trend with each adversarial round, climbing from 0% in the initial rounds to 45% by the final iteration. This indicates that the guardrail’s ability to intercept novel attacks is continuously improving. Concurrently, the Average Attack Turns (AAT) required for a successful breach also steadily rise, ultimately reaching 11.09 turns in the final round—a more than 7-fold increase from the baseline of 1.50. This further confirms that the Red Team’s task is becoming progressively more difficult. The task-level analysis in Figure 11 (in Appendix) corroborates this, showing that the number of attempts required for successful attacks increases substantially in later rounds for most tasks.

2) The Blue Team learns generalizable rules without catastrophic forgetting. A crucial aspect of iterative learning is avoiding overfitting to the most recent attacks. Figure 8 illustrates the Cross-Round Defense Efficacy (CRDE), showing how guardrails from later rounds perform against attacks from earlier rounds. Each line represents the performance of a progressively updated guardrail (from Guard Round 1 to 4) tested against a fixed set of historical attacks (from Round 1, 2, 3, or 4). A clear upward trend is observable across all lines, demonstrating two key findings. Firstly, the guardrail’s ability to defend against new attacks im-

proves with each iteration. Secondly, and more importantly, its defense performance against older attack sets (e.g., the Guard Round 4’s performance on Round 1 attacks) does not degrade but consistently improves. This indicates our iterative augmentation mechanism successfully avoids catastrophic forgetting. Furthermore, as shown in Figure 9, the False Positive Rate (FPR) remains at a very low level, demonstrating that the enhanced security does not come at the cost of excessive over-interception.

3) The evolved guardrail demonstrates strong out-of-domain generalization. To verify that the learned defensive capabilities generalize beyond the specific attack patterns seen during interaction, we evaluated the initial and final guardrails on 100 randomly selected prompts from four external, unseen jailbreak benchmarks. As shown in Figure 10, the Final Guard consistently outperforms the Initial Guard across all external datasets (JailBreakBench (Chao et al., 2024), AdvBench (Zou et al., 2023), SorryBench (Xie et al., 2024), and XGuard-Train

[p.8]

(Upadhayay et al., 2025)), with its defense success rate improving from 96% to 99% on JailBreak-Bench and achieving a perfect 100% on AdvBench. This indicates that the adversarial iterations compelled the Blue Team to learn more fundamental and robust principles of defense, enhancing its resilience against a broader spectrum of unseen jailbreak attacks.

5 Discussion Our findings substantiate the efficacy of the RvB framework, offering improvements that stem directly from the game-theoretic structure rather than mere model scaling. We analyze the mechanisms driving these results below. Convergence to Practical Equilibrium. The adversarial interaction drives the system toward a practical equilibrium where defensive coverage matches the high-probability attack manifold. As the Red Team’s belief entropy (H(bk )) decreases, its strategy shifts from broad exploration to focused exploitation. The resulting convergence of high Defense Success Rates with escalating Attack Costs suggests that further exploitation becomes computationally prohibitive. While a formal Nash Equilibrium in the high-dimensional discrete space of natural language remains theoretically elusive, the monotonic state progression confirms that offensive and defensive utilities balance at a robust security standard.

[COUPÉ : fin de la discussion (« Interaction-Driven Generalization », « Operational Viability via Semantic Verification »), conclusion (section 6)]

Limitations The efficacy of the RvB framework relies intrinsically on the reasoning capabilities of the underlying foundation models. Regarding evaluation scope,

[p.9]

our current experiments utilized a representative but constrained set of web vulnerabilities. Rigorous testing against larger-scale, diverse benchmarks is required to further verify generalizability. We also note that the turn-based simulation serves as a controlled abstraction, effectively simplifying the asynchronous dynamics inherent in real-world security operations.

[COUPÉ : déclaration d'éthique, références]

[p.11]

[COUPÉ : annexe A, expérience de sécurité informatique : réglage de l'équipe rouge (A.1), puis :]

A.2 Blue Team Setting The blue team’s Mini-SWE-Agent is designed to simulate the remediation workflow of a security engineer. Its inputs are the vulnerability report generated by the red team and the project’s source code. The blue team’s workflow comprises three stages: fault localization, patch generation, and regression verification. First, the agent analyzes the codebase based on the vulnerability report to locate the vulnerable PHP files. Next, it generates a patch in git diff format and applies it to the environment. Finally, it restarts the Docker container to verify service availability and confirm the mitigation of the vulnerability. To ensure the accuracy of code modifications and prevent disruption of existing business logic, the blue team model requires a high level of code comprehension and generation capability; thus, we also selected gemini-3.0-flash as its underlying model. If the initial repair fails, the blue team is permitted a maximum of 3 retry attempts to correct the patch.

A.3 Metric Definitions for Cyber Security In the context of the Cyber Security (Code Hardening) task, let D = {x1 , x2 , . . . , xN } denote the set of vulnerability test cases. For each test case xi at a given interaction round, we define the outcome (i) (i) state as a tuple (ratt , rreg ), where: (i)

• ratt ∈ {0, 1} denotes the outcome of the Red (i) Team’s attack. ratt = 1 indicates a successful breach (exploit succeeded), while 0 indicates a failed attack (defense succeeded). (i)

• rreg ∈ {0, 1} denotes the outcome of the ser- (i) vice regression test. rreg = 1 indicates the service remains functional, while 0 indicates a service disruption (e.g., HTTP 500 error or missing interface).

Defensive Metrics: DSR, TDSR, and FDSR The Defense Success Rate (DSR) represents the overall ratio of cases where the Blue Team successfully fixes the system. True Defense Success Rate (TDSR): The proportion of cases where the vulnerability is mitigated and the service integrity is preserved.

N 1 X (i) (i) DSR = TDSR = I(ratt = 0 ∧ rreg = 1) N i=1 (5)

[p.12]

Fake Defense Success Rate (FDSR): The proportion of cases where the attack fails solely because the defensive patch rendered the service non-functional. N 1 X (i) FDSR = I(ratt = 0) (6) N i=1

N 1 X (i) (i) SDR = I(ratt = 0 ∧ rreg = 0) (7) N i=1

Service Disruption Rate (SDR): A measure of availability loss caused by defensive over-optimization. It denotes the frequency with which valid user requests are blocked or service interfaces become unresponsive as a direct result of the applied security patches.

From the equations above, it holds that SDR = FDSR − TDSR. A robust automated defense agent should maximize TDSR while maintaining SDR near zero.

Offensive Metric: Attack Success Count (ASC) We utilize Attack Success Count (ASC) to measure the diversity and complexity of the attack vectors discovered by the Red Team. Let Ek be the set of unique, valid exploit payloads successfully executed by the Red Team up to round k. The ASC is defined as the cardinality of this cumulative set:

[COUPÉ : équation (8) et procédure A.4 (le point 1, avec son exemple d'entrée de journal d'attaque, est retiré pour sûreté) ; annexe B, expérience de gardes de contenu :]

• Target Model (target_model): The model to be attacked, protected by the Blue Team’s guardrail. To better observe the iterative improvement of the guardrail, this model was selected to have relatively weak intrinsic safety features but adequate question-answering capabilities. We chose Qwen2-7B-Instruct.

• Helper Model (helper_model): Responsible for generating diverse attack strategies. This role requires high capability without being prone to refusal. We chose DeepSeek-V3.

[p.13]

• Attack Model (attack_model): Guided by the strategies from the helper model, this component generates the actual attack prompts.

• Judge Model (judge_model): Evaluates the success of an attack by checking if the target model’s response aligns with the harmful intent, while also monitoring for topic drift.

For the attack_model and judge_model, we required highly capable open-source models that could reliably follow complex instructions. We chose Qwen3-32B for both roles. Blue Team Components The Blue Team agent, based on NeMo Guardrails, consists of the following:

• Target Model (target_model): The same Qwen2-7B-Instruct used by the Red Team.

[COUPÉ : composants de l'équipe bleue (modèle de garde, modèle de rustines)]

Both the guard_model and patch_model require stable, high-quality reasoning and are less likely to trigger refusal mechanisms. Therefore, we leveraged a powerful closed-source model with strong safety alignments, selecting GPT-4o-mini for these roles. Validation Component To prevent the augmented rules from overfitting, a validation step is performed using negative samples. The generate_model for creating these benign samples needs to be capable yet avoid refusal. We chose DeepSeek-V3 for this role. For each positive (harmful) prompt, three corresponding negative (benign) samples were generated.

[COUPÉ : annexe B.2 (procédure) ; annexe C, définitions :]

• Defense Success Rate (DSR): Our primary metric, measuring the proportion of harmful samples in round i that are successfully intercepted by the guardrail from the previous state, Guardi−1 . It is the inverse of the Attack Success Rate (ASR), calculated as 1 − ASRi . 1 X DSRi = 1 − IsSuccess(pq,i , Gi−1 ) Ntotal q∈D harm (9) where IsSuccess(p, G) returns 1 if prompt p bypasses guardrail G.

• Average Attack Turns (AAT): The average number of inner attack attempts the Red Team requires for a successful jailbreak in round i. P q∈Si kq,i AATi = (10) |Si | where Si is the subset of successfully attacked samples and kq,i is the number of attempts for sample q.

[p.14]

• Cross-Round Defense Efficacy (CRDE): Formerly DSR, this metric measures the capability of the guardrail at iteration i to defend against the set of effective attacks from a previous or current iteration j (where j ≤ i). It tests for catastrophic forgetting.

• False Positive Rate (FPR): The proportion of harmless samples from Dbenign,j that are incorrectly blocked by Guardi . P pb ∈Dbenign,j IsBlocked(pb , Guardi ) FPRi,j = |Dbenign,j | (12) where IsBlocked(pb , G) returns 1 if the benign prompt pb is incorrectly intercepted.
